. We also define the coordinate-dependent differential operator
\partial^{\mu}=g^{\mu\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}
. The symbol
\left\lvert g\right\rvert
is used for
-\det\big( g_{\mu\nu}\big)
, a scalar which is also coordinate-dependent. There are no preferred coordinate systems on
\mathcal{M}
: Unless otherwise specified, if a formula makes explicit use of tensor components, it is because the formula is covariant, that is, it retains the same expression regardless of the coordinate system being used.

Einstein’s equations with a stress-energy tensor
T\in\mathcal{T}^{0}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
acting as source term are

(1.1)

R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{R}{2}g_{\mu\nu}=\frac{8\pi G}{c^{4}}T_{\mu\nu}

for the Ricci scalar, Ricci tensor, Riemann tensor and Christoffel symbols

(1.2)

R=\mathchoice{R^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{R^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{R^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{R^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}},\ R_{\mu\nu}=\mathchoice{R^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07036pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07036pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.50754pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.0768pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu\alpha\nu}}}}{R^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07036pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07036pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.50754pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.0768pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu\alpha\nu}}}}{R^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07036pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07036pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.50754pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.0768pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu\alpha\nu}}}}{R^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[14.07036pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[14.07036pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.50754pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.0768pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu\alpha\nu}}}},\ \mathchoice{R^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[13.93369pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.93369pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.44296pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.0307pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha\beta\gamma}}}}{R^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[13.93369pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.93369pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.44296pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.0307pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha\beta\gamma}}}}{R^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[13.93369pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.93369pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.44296pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.0307pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha\beta\gamma}}}}{R^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[13.93369pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.93369pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.44296pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[6.0307pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha\beta\gamma}}}}=\partial_{\beta}\Gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\gamma}-\partial_{\gamma}\Gamma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}+\Gamma^{\mu}_{\sigma\beta}\Gamma^{\sigma}_{\gamma\alpha}-\Gamma^{\mu}_{\sigma\gamma}\Gamma^{\sigma}_{\beta\alpha},\ \Gamma^{\alpha}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{g^{\alpha\lambda}}{2}\big(\partial_{\mu}g_{\nu\lambda}+\partial_{\nu}g_{\mu\lambda}-\partial_{\lambda}g_{\mu\nu}\big).

Our convention for the normalization of the wedge product between basis 1-forms is that

\mathrm{d}x^{\mu_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{\mu_{p}}=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{p}}\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\ \mathrm{d}x^{\mu_{\sigma(1)}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{d}x^{\mu_{\sigma(p)}}.

In particular, a general 2-form
\omega
is written in the basis
\{\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{\nu}:\ \mu<\nu\}
using the same components that appear in its expression
\omega_{\mu\nu}\ \mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\otimes\mathrm{d}x^{\nu}
as a tensor:

\omega=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\omega_{0j}\ \mathrm{d}x^{0}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{j}+\omega_{12}\ \mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2}+\omega_{13}\ \mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{3}+\omega_{23}\ \mathrm{d}x^{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{3}.

But we will also display the components of such a differential form as an antisymmetric matrix:

(\omega_{\mu\nu})=\left(\begin{array}[]{rrrr}0&\omega_{01}&\omega_{02}&\omega_{03}\\
-\omega_{01}&0&\omega_{12}&\omega_{13}\\
-\omega_{02}&-\omega_{12}&0&\omega_{23}\\
-\omega_{03}&-\omega_{13}&-\omega_{23}&0\end{array}\right).

A tensor product of the form
\mathrm{d}f\otimes\mathrm{d}f
is denoted by
\mathrm{d}f^{2}
. This confusing notation is standard when specifying a metric tensor in a coordinate system.

The scalar product on the space of 1-forms, that is, the map defined by the inverse metric

g^{-1}:\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}(\mathcal{M})\times\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}(\mathcal{M})\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{M}),\quad g^{-1}(\psi,\omega)=g^{\mu\nu}\psi_{\mu}\omega_{\nu},

generalizes to scalar products
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{g}:\bigwedge\nolimits^{p}(\mathcal{M})\times\bigwedge\nolimits^{p}(\mathcal{M})\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{M})
for all
p
: If
p=0
this is simply defined as
\langle f,h\rangle_{g}:=fh
, while if
p>0
it is uniquely defined by its action on blades:

(1.3)

\big\langle\psi^{(1)}\wedge\cdots\wedge\psi^{(p)},\omega^{(1)}\wedge\cdots\wedge\omega^{(p)}\big\rangle_{g}:=\det\big( g^{\mu\nu} \psi^{(j)}_\mu\omega^{(k)}_\nu\big)_{j,k=1,\ldots,p},\quad\psi^{(j)},\omega^{(k)}\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}(\mathcal{M}).

This yields the formula

(1.4)

\big\langle\psi,\omega\big\rangle_{g}=\frac{1}{p!}\psi_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{p}}\omega^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{p}},\quad\psi,\omega\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{p}(\mathcal{M}).

Then, for each
p=0,\ldots,4
, the Hodge star
{\star}:\bigwedge\nolimits^{p}(\mathcal{M})\longrightarrow\bigwedge\nolimits^{4-p}(\mathcal{M})
is defined as the unique
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{M})
-linear map satisfying

(1.5)

\psi\wedge({\star}\omega)=\big\langle\psi,\omega\big\rangle_{g}\ \mathrm{vol}_{g},\quad\psi,\omega\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{p}(\mathcal{M}),

where

\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\sqrt{\left\lvert g\right\rvert}\ \mathrm{d}x^{0}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{3}\quad\text{for positively oriented coordinate systems }(x^{\mu})

is the canonical volume form defined by the metric
g
. We note some useful properties of
{\star}
:

(1.6)

({\star}\psi)\wedge({\star}\omega)=-\psi\wedge\omega,\quad{\star}{\star}\psi=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\psi&\text{ if }\psi\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{p}(M)\text{ for odd }p,\\
-\psi&\text{ if }\psi\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{p}(M)\text{ for even }p,\end{array}\right.\quad{\star}1=\mathrm{vol}_{g}.

If
g=\eta
is the Minkowski metric and a system of inertial coordinates
\{x^{\mu}\}
is used (see (
2.1
) ahead), one checks that this definition of the Hodge-star operator yields

\hskip-10.00002pt\begin{array}[]{rclcrcl}{\star}(1)&=&\phantom{-}\mathrm{d}x^{0}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{3},&&{\star}(\mathrm{d}x^{0}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{3})&=&-1\\
{\star}(\mathrm{d}x^{0})&=&-\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{3},&&{\star}(\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{3})&=&-\mathrm{d}x^{0}\\
{\star}(\mathrm{d}x^{1})&=&-\mathrm{d}x^{0}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{3},&&{\star}(\mathrm{d}x^{0}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{3})&=&-\mathrm{d}x^{1}\\
{\star}(\mathrm{d}x^{2})&=&\phantom{-}\mathrm{d}x^{0}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{3},&&{\star}(\mathrm{d}x^{0}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{3})&=&\phantom{-}\mathrm{d}x^{2}\\
{\star}(\mathrm{d}x^{3})&=&-\mathrm{d}x^{0}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2},&&{\star}(\mathrm{d}x^{0}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2})&=&-\mathrm{d}x^{3}\\
{\star}(\mathrm{d}x^{0}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{1})&=&-\mathrm{d}x^{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{3},&&{\star}(\mathrm{d}x^{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{3})&=&\phantom{-}\mathrm{d}x^{0}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{1}\\
{\star}(\mathrm{d}x^{0}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2})&=&\phantom{-}\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{3},&&{\star}(\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{3})&=&-\mathrm{d}x^{0}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2}\\
{\star}(\mathrm{d}x^{0}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{3})&=&-\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2},&&{\star}(\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2})&=&\phantom{-}\mathrm{d}x^{0}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{3}\\
\end{array}

The codifferential
\delta:\bigwedge\nolimits^{p}(\mathcal{M})\longrightarrow\bigwedge\nolimits^{p-1}(\mathcal{M})
is the operator

(1.7)

\delta={\star}\circ\mathrm{d}\circ{\star}={\star}\mathrm{d}{\star}

(note that the composition sign
\circ
is usually omitted when composing operators that act on differential forms), where
\mathrm{d}:\bigwedge\nolimits^{p}(\mathcal{M})\longrightarrow\bigwedge\nolimits^{p+1}(\mathcal{M})
is the exterior derivative. We remark that a more general formula than (
1.7
) is normally taken as the definition of
\delta
, but it reduces to our formula as above in the case of a 4-dimensional manifold with a
({-}\,{+}\,{+}\,{+})
metric. The following expressions for the operators
\mathrm{d}
and
\delta
are valid in any coordinate system and will be extensively used: For
\psi\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{p}(\mathcal{M})
,

(1.8)

(\mathrm{d}\psi)_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{p+1}}=\sum_{j=1}^{p+1}(-1)^{j+1}\partial_{\mu_{j}}\psi_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{j-1}\mu_{j+1}\cdots\mu_{p}},

(1.9)

(\delta\psi)^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{p-1}}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\left\lvert g\right\rvert}}\ \partial_{\lambda}\big(\sqrt{\left\lvert g\right\rvert}\psi^{\lambda\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{p-1}}\big).

Note that the indices of
\psi
appearing in (
1.9
) are raised, highlighting the fact that
\delta
depends on the metric, as is clear from its definition (
1.7
) in terms of the Hodge star. Formulas (
1.8
) and (
1.9
) are remarkable because, although they feature
partial
derivatives as opposed to
covariant
ones, they still manage to be valid in any coordinate system — in fact they are equivalent to

(1.10)

(\mathrm{d}\psi)_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{p+1}}=\sum_{j=1}^{p+1}(-1)^{j+1}\nabla_{\mu_{j}}\psi_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{j-1}\mu_{j+1}\cdots\mu_{p}},\quad(\delta\psi)^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{p-1}}=-\nabla_{\lambda}\psi^{\lambda\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{p-1}}.

But, contrary to what happens for (
1.10
), the indices of formulas (
1.8
) and (
1.9
) cannot be freely raised/lowered on both sides of the equation as in manifestly tensorial equations (unless
g=\eta
is the Minkowski metric), so care must be taken when applying them on a general manifold.

Finally, for a function
f:(a,\infty)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
, where
a\in\mathbb{R}
, we often denote the limits
\lim_{r\to a^{+}}f(r)
and
\lim_{r\to\infty}f(r)
(should they exist) by the symbols
f(a)
and
f(\infty)
, respectively. We also make the following definition, to be used throughout Section
4
:

Definition 1.1
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{R}
. For two strictly positive functions
f,g:(a,\infty)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we say that
g
is
bracketed
by
f
and write
f\simeq g
(it is an equivalence relation) when there exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that

C_{1}f(r)\leq g(r)\leq C_{2}f(r)\quad\text{for all }r>a.

If
f
and
g
in the above definition are continuous, a sufficient condition for
f\simeq g
is that the quotient
f/g
attain finite, non-zero limits as
r\to a^{+}
and
r\to\infty
. Indeed, the existence of these limits implies that the continuous, strictly positive functions
f/g
and
g/f
remain bounded above in two intervals of the form
(a,a+\varepsilon)
and
(N,\infty)
, while continuity also yields finite bounds for them in
[a+\varepsilon,N]
.

2.
Electromagnetism in Special Relativity

In this section we define on Minkowski spacetime the theories of electromagnetism that we wish to study, and we explain why BLTP theory gives rise to better-behaved fields and potentials than the conventional Maxwell theory, at least in the case of a single, static point charge.

2.1.
The Maxwell and BLTP theories of electromagnetism

Minkowski spacetime is by definition the manifold
\mathbb{R}^{4}
endowed with a Lorentzian metric
\eta
which, in a suitable coordinate system
(x^{0},x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
(called
inertial
), takes the form

(2.1)

\eta=-\mathrm{d}x^{0}\otimes\mathrm{d}x^{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\mathrm{d}x^{j}\otimes\mathrm{d}x^{j}.

All four coordinates are assumed to have a dimension of
[Length]
. The last three,
\bm{x}=(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
, are interpreted as giving a spatial location, while
x^{0}/c
(where
c
is the speed of light) has the physical meaning of time as perceived by an inertial observer. Given a differentiable function
f:\mathbb{R}^{4}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
of the variables
(x^{0},\ldots,x^{3})
, we often abuse notation and write
(1/c)\partial_{t}f
in place of
\partial_{0}f
for its partial derivative with respect to the variable
x^{0}
.

Now let a
charge-density
scalar field
\rho:\mathbb{R}^{4}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
, with dimension
\texttt{[Charge]}\texttt{[Length]}^{-3}=\texttt{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\texttt{L}^{-\frac{3}{2}}\texttt{T}^{-1}
, and a
current-density
vector field
\bm{j}:\mathbb{R}^{4}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
, whose components
j_{1},j_{2},j_{3}:\mathbb{R}^{4}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
are measured with dimension
\texttt{[Charge]}\texttt{[Velocity]}\texttt{[Length]}^{-3}=\texttt{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\texttt{L}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\texttt{T}^{-2}
, be given satisfying the
continuity equation

(2.2)

\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial t}+\nabla\cdot\bm{j}=0,

where the divergence operator
\nabla\cdot
uses derivatives with respect to the three spatial coordinates — this will also be the case for gradients
\nabla
, curls
\nabla\times
and Laplacians
\Delta
in what follows. We make no assumption on the regularity of
\rho
,
\bm{j}
or any of the other fields described in this section; our goal is simply to formally state equations that are applicable to sufficiently regular unknowns.

Maxwell’s equations
in their most general form, often called the
macroscopic
form, stipulate that the distribution of charges and currents modeled by
\rho
and
\bm{j}
generate EM fields
\bm{E},\bm{D},\bm{B},\bm{H}:\mathbb{R}^{4}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
, all of whose components having the dimension
\texttt{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\texttt{L}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\texttt{T}^{-1}
, that satisfy the systems

(2.3)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bm{B}}{\partial t}+\nabla\times\bm{E}=\bm{0},\\
\\
\nabla\cdot\bm{B}=0,\end{array}\right.\qquad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\frac{1}{c}\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bm{D}}{\partial t}-\nabla\times\bm{H}=-\displaystyle\frac{4\pi}{c}\bm{j},\\
\\
\nabla\cdot\bm{D}=4\pi\rho.\end{array}\right.

The fields
\bm{E},\bm{D},\bm{B},\bm{H}
are respectively known as the
electric
,
electric displacement
,
magnetic
and
magnetizing
fields. The continuity equation (
2.2
) is a compatibility condition for this system, as it follows immediately from the two non-homogeneous equations. The homogeneous ones are equivalent, at least locally, to the existence of a
scalar potential

\varphi
and a
vector potential

\bm{A}
— which however are not uniquely defined — such that

(2.4)

\bm{B}=\nabla\times\bm{A},\quad\bm{E}=-\nabla\varphi-\frac{1}{c}\frac{\partial\bm{A}}{\partial t}.

The Maxwell equations are often considered to be a system for the unknowns
\varphi
and
\bm{A}
. We also note their dimensions:

\dim(\varphi)=\dim(A_{j})=\texttt{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\texttt{L}^{\frac{1}{2}}\texttt{T}^{-1},\quad\text{where }\bm{A}=(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}).

However, these equations alone are clearly not enough to determine the fields from their sources. A so-called
vacuum law
, also commonly referred to by the name
constitutive relations
, must be stipulated: one relation between the electric fields
\bm{E}
and
\bm{D}
, and another one between the magnetic fields
\bm{B}
and
\bm{H}
. We call the relations

(2.5)

\bm{D}=\bm{E},\quad\bm{H}=\bm{B}

Maxwell’s vacuum law
and label the set of equations (
2.3
) and (
2.5
) the
Maxwell-Maxwell equations
, which form a system of linear, second-order PDEs (that is, in the unknowns
\varphi
and
\bm{A}
) that defines the conventional Maxwell theory. In turn, BLTP theory is defined by imposing the
BLTP vacuum law

(2.6)

\bm{D}=\bm{E}-\varkappa^{-2}\square\bm{E},\quad\bm{H}=\bm{B}-\varkappa^{-2}\square\bm{B}.

Here
\varkappa>0
is a postulated constant with dimension
\texttt{[Length]}^{-1}
, while

\square=\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\mu}=\eta^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}=-\frac{1}{c^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}+\Delta

is D’Alembert’s operator, acting componentwise on vector fields. Then (
2.3
) and (
2.6
) are called the
Maxwell-BLTP equations
, a system of fourth-order linear PDEs for
\varphi
and
\bm{A}
. See Remark
2.5
ahead for an explanation on why BLTP theory can be viewed as a
natural
modification of the conventional Maxwell theory.

Remark 2.1
.

The constant featuring in the equations of BLTP theory is not
\varkappa
itself, but rather its inverse
\varkappa^{-1}
(or powers thereof). If
\varkappa^{-1}
is set to 0, we see from (
2.6
) that the equations reduce to the Maxwell-Maxwell system. Hence, in everything that follows, we will be able to treat both theories under the same set of equations, with the understanding that the Maxwell-Maxwell equations are obtained by setting
\varkappa=\infty
, that is, by removing from the equations all terms accompanied by a positive power of
\varkappa^{-1}
.

2.2.
Field energy and momenta

The fields
\bm{E}
and
\bm{B}
determine the motion of test particles by acting on them via the Lorentz force: Let a test particle of rest mass
m
and charge
q
be observed in the coordinate system
(x^{\mu})
to be moving with velocity vector
\bm{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})
, kinetic energy
\mathcal{E}=mc^{2}\gamma
and linear momentum
\bm{p}=mc\gamma\bm{v}
, where
\gamma=\big(1-(v/c)\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
for
v=\sqrt{(v_{1})^{2}+(v_{2})^{2}+(v_{3})^{2}}
. Then the particle’s law of motion under the influence of the EM fields is the statement that the rate of change of its momentum is

(2.7)

\frac{\mathrm{d}\bm{p}}{\mathrm{d}t}=\bm{F}:=q\bm{E}+\frac{1}{c}(q\bm{v})\times\bm{B},

where
\times
denotes the vector cross product. In particular, the power imparted by the EM fields to the particle is

(2.8)

\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}}{\mathrm{d}t}=\bm{F}\cdot\bm{v}=\bm{E}\cdot(q\bm{v}).

With the help of (
2.7
) and (
2.8
), it is now possible to obtain expressions (in any general EM theory) for the following observer-dependent quantities that measure, at each event
(x^{0},\ldots,x^{3})
, how much energy and momentum are contained in the EM fields and where they are flowing into:

•

The EM fields’
energy density
: a scalar field
\mathfrak{e}
measuring the volumetric density of energy contained in them. Its dimension is
\texttt{[Energy]}\texttt{[Length]}^{-3}=\texttt{M}\texttt{L}^{-1}\texttt{T}^{-2}
.

•

The EM fields’
momentum density
: a vector field
\bm{\mathfrak{g}}
measuring the volumetric density of momentum contained in them. In particular, for each
j=1,2,3
, its component
g_{j}
in
(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
coordinates is a scalar measuring the density of field momentum in the direction of
x^{j}
, with dimension
\texttt{[Momentum]}\texttt{[Length]}^{-3}=\texttt{M}\texttt{L}^{-2}\texttt{T}^{-1}
.

•

The EM fields’
energy flux
: a vector field
\bm{\mathfrak{S}}
, pointing in the direction where energy flows, whose magnitude measures the amount of energy per unit time and unit area that crosses a small hypersurface orthogonal to its flow direction. It is known as the
Poynting vector
. In particular, for each
k=1,2,3
, its component
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
in
(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
coordinates is a scalar measuring the rate at which field energy crosses hypersurfaces normal to the direction of
x^{k}
, towards the positive direction, with dimension
\texttt{[Energy]}\texttt{[Length]}^{-2}\texttt{[Time]}^{-1}=\texttt{M}\texttt{T}^{-3}
.

•

The EM fields’
momentum flux
: a
(3\times 3)
-matrix field
\bm{\mathfrak{T}}
whose component
{\mathfrak{T}}_{jk}
in
(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})
coordinates is a scalar measuring the rate at which the
j
-th component of field momentum crosses hypersurfaces normal to the direction of
x^{k}
, towards the positive direction. The dimension of each component of
\bm{\mathfrak{T}}
is
\texttt{[Momentum]}\texttt{[Length]}^{-2}\texttt{[Time]}^{-1}=\texttt{M}\texttt{L}^{-1}\texttt{T}^{-2}
.

For the sake of completeness of our work, we wish to derive from scratch an expression for
\mathfrak{e}
in BLTP theory. So let us briefly explain how
\mathfrak{e}
,
\bm{\mathfrak{g}}
,
\bm{\mathfrak{S}}
and
\bm{\mathfrak{T}}
are derived for any general EM theory. Suppose that the fields act back on their source charge distribution modeled by
\rho
and
\bm{j}
via the Lorentz force. If
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is an arbitrary bounded open set with a smooth boundary
\partial V
, then the rate of change of field energy contained inside it,
\iiint_{V}\partial_{t}\mathfrak{e}\ \mathrm{d}^{3}\bm{x}
, is brought about by two processes: field energy entering the boundary of
V
at a rate of
-\oiint_{\partial V}\bm{\mathfrak{S}}\cdot\mathrm{d}S=-\iiint_{V}\nabla\cdot\bm{\mathfrak{S}}\ \mathrm{d}^{3}\bm{x}
, and field energy being lost into work done by the Lorentz force on the sources inside
V
at a rate of
-\iiint_{V}\bm{E}\cdot\bm{j}\ \mathrm{d}^{3}\bm{x}
. For this last expression, note that the
density
of work is found from (
2.8
) as
\bm{E}\cdot\bm{j}
, since the volumetric density of
q\bm{v}
is by definition the current density
\bm{j}
. Now energy conservation in
V
dictates a relation between these integrals which, given the arbitrariness of
V
, yields the conservation law

(2.9)

\frac{\partial\mathfrak{e}}{\partial t}+\nabla\cdot\bm{\mathfrak{S}}=-\bm{E}\cdot\bm{j}.

Proceeding similarly for the components of field momentum — note that (
2.7
) with
q
and
q\bm{v}
replaced by
\rho
and
\bm{j}
, respectively, is the
density
of momentum being transferred from the fields to the sources, — we find the equations of momentum conservation:

(2.10)

\frac{\partial\mathfrak{g}_{j}}{\partial t}+\nabla\cdot\bm{\mathfrak{T}_{j}}=-\rho\bm{E}-\frac{1}{c}\bm{j}\times\bm{B},\quad j=1,2,3,

where
\bm{\mathfrak{T}_{j}}=(\mathfrak{T}_{j1},\mathfrak{T}_{j2},\mathfrak{T}_{j3})
. It is at this point that the equations of the EM theory of our choice must be used: First the non-homogeneous Maxwell equations (
2.3
) are used to replace
\rho
and
\bm{j}
on the right side of (
2.9
) and (
2.10
) by expressions involving all four EM fields, which are then rewritten in terms of only the fields
\bm{E}
and
\bm{B}
by applying the vacuum law (
2.6
). Finally, using the homogeneous Maxwell equations (
2.3
) and some lengthy algebra, these right sides can be cast in the form
\partial_{t}(\ast)+\nabla\cdot(\bm{\ast})
, where
(\ast)
and
(\bm{\ast})
stand for some particular scalar and vector, which are then
defined
to be
\mathfrak{e}
and
\bm{\mathfrak{S}}
when this procedure is applied to (
2.9
), or
\mathfrak{g}_{j}
and
\bm{\mathfrak{T}}_{j}
when applied to (
2.10
). In the
\varkappa=\infty
case, this calculation can be found in many textbooks on electromagnetism (see
[
9
]
, p. 189). We wish to carry it out in the
0<\varkappa<\infty
case, but not now; we postpone it to the end of this section, where the language of tensor fields makes the task somewhat simpler. For now, we present the result that will be found for
\mathfrak{e}
(we will not need to consider
\bm{\mathfrak{g}}
,
\bm{\mathfrak{S}}
or
\bm{\mathfrak{T}}
in this paper):

(2.11)

\displaystyle 4\pi\mathfrak{e}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left\lvert\bm{E}\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\bm{B}\right\rvert^{2}}{2}+\varkappa^{-2}\left(-\bm{E}\cdot\square\bm{E}-\bm{B}\cdot\square\bm{B}-\frac{(\nabla\cdot\bm{E})^{2}}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left|\nabla\times\bm{B}-\frac{1}{c}\frac{\partial\bm{E}}{\partial t}\right|^{2}\right)

(2.12)

\displaystyle=\bm{B}\cdot\bm{H}+\bm{E}\cdot\bm{D}-\frac{|\bm{E}|^{2}+|\bm{B}|^{2}}{2}-\frac{\varkappa^{-2}}{2}\left((\nabla\cdot\bm{E})^{2}+\left|\nabla\times\bm{B}-\frac{1}{c}\frac{\partial\bm{E}}{\partial t}\right|^{2}\right).

This is the same formula that is stated, for example, in
[
2
]
and
[
13
]
.

Remark 2.2
.

Given that there are infinitely many possible expressions for
(\ast)
and
(\bm{\ast})
preserving the value of
\partial_{t}(\ast)+\nabla\cdot(\bm{\ast})
, there are also infinitely many possible definitions for the quantities
\mathfrak{e}
,
\bm{\mathfrak{g}}
,
\bm{\mathfrak{S}}
and
\bm{\mathfrak{T}}
. This fact remains true even if one restricts the search to a
symmetric

\bm{\mathfrak{T}}
as well as to quantities
\bm{\mathfrak{g}}
and
\bm{\mathfrak{S}}
related by
\bm{\mathfrak{S}}=c^{2}\bm{\mathfrak{g}}
, which are physically motivated impositions that hold for matter models in Classical Mechanics. Hence there is no definitive answer to the question of how the field energy and momenta are defined in BLTP theory or even conventional Maxwell theory. One generally settles for expressions that, in some way, feel the simplest or most natural to derive.

2.3.
SR solutions for a static point charge

Now we would like to restrict the EM equations to the case of a single static point of charge
Q\neq 0
sitting at position
(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})=\bm{0}
at all times
t
. The goal is to write down the fields that solve both the Maxwell-Maxwell and the Maxwell-BLTP systems corresponding to this particle’s associated densities, which are

(2.13)

\rho=Q\delta_{\bm{0}},\quad\bm{j}=\bm{0},

where
\delta_{\bm{0}}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is Dirac’s Delta concentrated at
\bm{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, formally viewed as a generalized function. Anticipating that the fields and their energy will be constant in time and only
\bm{E}
and
\bm{D}
will be nonzero, we see that (
2.12
) yields the following expression for the total
electric-field energy
at any given time:

(2.14)

\mathcal{E}=\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathfrak{e}\ \mathrm{d}\bm{x}=\frac{1}{8\pi}\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(2\bm{E}\cdot\bm{D}-|\bm{E}|^{2}-\varkappa^{-2}(\nabla\cdot\bm{E})^{2}\right)\ \mathrm{d}\bm{x}.

The well-known solution to the Maxwell-Maxwell system in this context is given at all events
(x^{0},\ldots,x^{3})
with
(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})\neq\bm{0}
by

\bm{D}^{\mathrm{(M)}}=\bm{E}^{\mathrm{(M)}}=\frac{Q}{r^{2}}\bm{e_{r}},\quad\bm{H}^{\mathrm{(M)}}=\bm{B}^{\mathrm{(M)}}=\bm{0},

where
r=\sqrt{(x^{1})^{2}+(x^{2})^{2}+(x^{3})^{2}}
and
\bm{e_{r}}=(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})/r
, and we have omitted the argument
(x^{0},\ldots,x^{3})
from the fields. The label M stands, of course, for
Maxwell
. We speak of “the” solution as opposed to “a” solution because one usually imposes reasonable boundary-type conditions to ensure its uniqueness — in the present case, this is the only solution that vanishes at spatial infinity. Next note that the electric field is conservative, that is, it is given in the form
\bm{E}^{\mathrm{(M)}}=-\nabla\varphi^{\mathrm{(M)}}
for an
electric potential

\varphi^{\mathrm{(M)}}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
, namely the
Coulomb potential
, which we view as a function
\varphi^{(\mathrm{M})}:(0,\infty)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
of
r
:

(2.15)

\varphi^{\mathrm{(M)}}(r)=\frac{Q}{r}.

However, plugging
\bm{E}^{\mathrm{(M)}}
and
\bm{D}^{\mathrm{(M)}}
into the field-energy integral (
2.14
) (recall
\varkappa^{-2}=0
in the Maxwell-Maxwell equations) gives
\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{(M)}}=\infty
, due to
\left\lvert\bm{E}^{\mathrm{(M)}}\right\rvert^{2}=Q^{2}/r^{4}
not being integrable around the location
r=0
of the particle. We say that a charged particle has
infinite self-energy
under the conventional Maxwell theory. Also note that
\varphi^{(\mathrm{M})}(0)
is undefined.

Now let us investigate the solution
\bm{E}^{\mathrm{(BLTP)}}
,
\bm{B}^{\mathrm{(BLTP)}}
etc. of the Maxwell-BLTP equations (
0<\varkappa<\infty
) corresponding to
\rho
and
\bm{j}
as in (
2.13
). We carry out the calculations in details in order to have a point of comparison for our work in the next section. We omit the superscript (BLTP) from the unknowns until the end. Given that the equations are now of higher order, one can expect more degrees of freedom as in the case of Maxwell theory, but there will again be restrictions coming from physically motivated impositions: First of all, in parallel to the Maxwell-Maxwell case, we restrict the search to time-independent solutions that vanish at spatial infinity. The Maxwell equations then imply
\nabla\times\bm{E}=\nabla\times\bm{H}=\bm{0}
. The equation
\nabla\cdot\bm{B}=0
in conjunction with the constitutive relation of
\bm{H}
in terms of
\bm{B}
gives
\nabla\cdot\bm{H}=0
, and thus
\bm{H}=\bm{0}
. Now
\bm{B}
is determined by the constitutive relation
\bm{0}=\bm{B}-\varkappa^{-2}\Delta\bm{B}
and the Maxwell equation
\nabla\cdot\bm{B}=0
, and, although there are non-zero solutions
\bm{B}
to this system, we have to consider
\bm{B}=\bm{0}
as the only physically meaningful one because otherwise we would be dealing with the unphysical situation of a magnetic monopole. Next, the equations for
\bm{D}
reduce to
\nabla\cdot\bm{D}=4\pi Q\delta_{\bm{0}}
and
\nabla\times\bm{D}=\bm{0}
, the latter as a consequence of
\nabla\times\bm{E}=\bm{0}
and
\bm{D}=\bm{E}-\varkappa^{-2}\Delta\bm{E}
. This yields as before

\bm{D}^{\mathrm{(BLTP)}}=\frac{Q}{r^{2}}\bm{e_{r}}.

Since
\nabla\times\bm{E}=\bm{0}
, the electric field is again conservative, at least in simply connected regions of spacetime (though we will find this to be true globally):
\bm{E}=-\nabla\varphi
for a scalar potential
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
that can be re-imagined as a function of
r
due to spherical symmetry of the sources
\rho
and
\bm{j}
. In particular
\bm{E}=-\varphi^{\prime}(r)\bm{e_{r}}
. Plugging this into the constitutive relation (
2.6
) of
\bm{D}
in terms of
\bm{E}
and using the standard formula for the Laplacian in spherical coordinates, we obtain the following equation for
\varphi
:

(2.16)

\frac{Q}{r^{2}}=-\varphi^{\prime}-\varkappa^{-2}\left(-\varphi^{\prime\prime\prime}-\frac{2\varphi^{\prime\prime}}{r}+\frac{2\varphi^{\prime}}{r^{2}}\right).

It is most easily solved by introducing a new unknown
\chi
that we call the
electric-field relative-deviation scalar
or simply
electric deviation
:

(2.17)

\chi(r):=\frac{1}{Q}r^{2}\varphi^{\prime}(r)+1.

Then (
2.16
) transforms into the homogeneous ODE

\frac{1}{r^{2}}=\frac{1-\chi}{r^{2}}-\varkappa^{-2}\left(-\frac{\chi^{\prime\prime}}{r^{2}}+\frac{2\chi^{\prime}}{r^{3}}\right)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\chi^{\prime\prime}=\frac{2}{r}\chi^{\prime}+\varkappa^{2}\chi,

whose general solution is
\chi(r)=C_{1}(1+\varkappa r)e^{-\varkappa r}+C_{2}(1-\varkappa r)e^{\varkappa r}
.
We must take
C_{2}=0
in order for
\bm{E}=-\varphi^{\prime}\bm{e_{r}}=\big(Q(1-\chi)/r^{2}\big)\bm{e_{r}}
to vanish at infinity. Then the corresponding
\varphi
that vanishes at infinity is defined by

\varphi(r)=\int_{\infty}^{r}\frac{Q\big(\chi(s)-1\big)}{s^{2}}\ \mathrm{d}s=\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{Q\big(1-C_{1}(1+\varkappa s)e^{-\varkappa s}\big)}{s^{2}}\ \mathrm{d}s=\frac{Q\big(1-C_{1}e^{-\varkappa r}\big)}{r},

with
C_{1}
allowed as a free parameter. The value
C_{1}=0
would yield
\varphi^{\mathrm{(BLTP)}}=\varphi^{\mathrm{(M)}}
, that is, the solution to the Maxwell-Maxwell system also solves the Maxwell-BLTP system, a fact that will remain true later in the context of GR. However, the choice
C_{1}=1
is the natural one to make here because it renders
\varphi
bounded around
r=0
: With this choice, we find the everywhere-smooth potential

(2.18)

\varphi^{\mathrm{(BLTP)}}(r)=\frac{Q(1-e^{-\varkappa r})}{r}\quad\text{for }r>0,\qquad\varphi^{\mathrm{(BLTP)}}(0)=\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{Q(1-e^{-\varkappa r})}{r}=Q\varkappa.

Consequently, the electric field

\bm{E}^{\mathrm{(BLTP)}}=\frac{Q\big(1-(1+\varkappa r)e^{-\varkappa r}\big)}{r^{2}}\bm{e_{r}}

also remains bounded at small
r
(though the vector
\bm{E}^{\mathrm{(BLTP)}}(\bm{0})
itself is not defined). We also point out that the relative deviation of
\bm{E}^{\mathrm{(BLTP)}}
compared to the Coulomb field
\bm{E}^{\mathrm{(M)}}
is precisely our quantity
\chi
, justifying the name we chose for it:

(2.19)

\frac{\left\lvert\bm{E}^{\mathrm{(BLTP)}}-\bm{E}^{\mathrm{(M)}}\right\rvert}{\left\lvert\bm{E}^{\mathrm{(M)}}\right\rvert}=\frac{Q\big(1-(1+\varkappa r)e^{-\varkappa r}\big)/r^{2}-Q/r^{2}}{Q/r^{2}}=(1+\varkappa r)e^{-\varkappa r}=\chi(r).

In particular, the analogous scalar
\chi
corresponding to the Coulomb potential would be identically null:

\chi^{(\mathrm{M})}(r):=\frac{1}{Q}r^{2}\big(\varphi^{(\mathrm{M})}\big)^{\prime}(r)+1=0.

Finally, we investigate the electric-field energy (
2.14
) of our static particle. Using
\bm{D}=(Q/r^{2})\bm{e_{r}}
and
\bm{E}=-\varphi^{\prime}(r)\bm{e_{r}}
, we find

\bm{E}\cdot\bm{D}=-\frac{Q\varphi^{\prime}}{r^{2}},\quad|\bm{E}|^{2}=(\varphi^{\prime})^{2},\quad(\nabla\cdot\bm{E})^{2}=\left(-\varphi^{\prime\prime}-\frac{2\varphi^{\prime}}{r}\right)^{2}

(the last of these equations comes from the standard formula for the divergence in spherical coordinates). This culminates in a finite value for the energy:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\mathrm{(BLTP)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\pi}\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(2\bm{E}\cdot\bm{D}-|\bm{E}|^{2}-\varkappa^{-2}(\nabla\cdot\bm{E})^{2}\right)\ \mathrm{d}\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\pi}{8\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(-2Q\frac{\varphi^{\prime}(r)}{r^{2}}-\big(\varphi^{\prime}(r)\big)^{2}-\varkappa^{-2}\left(-\varphi^{\prime\prime}(r)-\frac{2\varphi^{\prime}(r)}{r}\right)^{2}\right)\ \mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{Q^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{r^{2}}\bigg(1-\left(1+2\varkappa r+2\varkappa^{2}r^{2}\right)e^{-2\varkappa r}\bigg)\ \mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=\frac{Q^{2}}{2\varkappa}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\varkappa^{2}}{s^{2}}\bigg(1-\left(1+2s+2s^{2}\right)e^{-2s}\bigg)\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\frac{Q^{2}\varkappa}{2}\left(\frac{-1+(1+s)e^{-2s}}{s}\right)\bigg|_{0}^{\infty}

\displaystyle=\frac{Q^{2}\varkappa}{2}<\infty.

Remark 2.3
.

Assuming for a moment that BLTP theory is valid in nature, it is possible to make a cursory guess for the range of the parameter
\varkappa
by looking at what happens at a sub-atomic distance from a charged particle. Considering that the classical Coulomb’s law of electric attraction/repulsion (which indirectly follows from the statement that a point charge’s electric field is given by
\bm{E}^{\mathrm{(M)}}
) has been experimentally verified down to sub-atomic distances, experimentalists would only find evidence for BLTP theory at a distance of about
10^{-10}
m or smaller away from a point charge. Since the relative deviation (
2.19
) between
\bm{E}^{\mathrm{(BLTP)}}
and
\bm{E}^{\mathrm{(M)}}
is of about 1% at
r=10^{-10}
m when
\varkappa=6.64\cdot 10^{10}

\mathrm{m}^{-1}
and decreases exponentially as
r
increases, we will assume the ballpark estimate

(2.20)

\varkappa>10^{11}\ \mathrm{m}^{-1}

whenever we do calculations involving real-life constants. On the other hand, at a distance as small as
10^{-16}
m — safely inside atomic nuclei and therefore within the quantum realm, — one can expect that BLTP theory, as a classical theory of electromagnetism, would have already made its presence clear. Since (
2.19
) is of about 50% at
r=10^{-16}
m when
\varkappa=1.68\cdot 10^{16}

\mathrm{m}^{-1}
, we will also assume

(2.21)

\varkappa<10^{16}\ \mathrm{m}^{-1}.

2.4.
Covariant formulation of electromagnetism in SR

As is well known, the equations of electromagnetism assume a simpler, manifestly covariant form when written in the language of differential forms and other tensor fields. We briefly explain this formulation now, as it will be needed to treat the GR case next.

Let source terms
\rho,\bm{j}
and their associated fields
\bm{E},\bm{D},\bm{B},\bm{H}
and field energy and momenta
\mathfrak{e}
,
\bm{\mathfrak{g}}
,
\bm{\mathfrak{S}}
and
\bm{\mathfrak{T}}
, satisfying
\bm{\mathfrak{S}}=c^{2}\bm{\mathfrak{g}}
and
\mathfrak{T}_{ij}=\mathfrak{T}_{ji}
, be observed in an inertial coordinate system
(x^{\mu})
to solve the EM equations presented in the previous subsection, either with or without the
\varkappa^{-1}
terms. Now consider a 1-form field
J
(called the
four-current
1-form), two 2-form fields
F,M
(called
Faraday
’s and
Maxwell
’s tensors, among many other names in the literature) and a symmetric
(2,0)
-tensor field
T
(called the
stress-energy
tensor) which are given in these coordinates by

(2.22)

J=-c\rho\ \mathrm{d}x^{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{3}j_{k}\ \mathrm{d}x^{k},

(2.23)

(F_{\mu\nu})=\left(\begin{array}[]{rrrr}0&-E_{1}&-E_{2}&-E_{3}\\
E_{1}&0&B_{3}&-B_{2}\\
E_{2}&-B_{3}&0&B_{1}\\
E_{3}&B_{2}&-B_{1}&0\end{array}\right),\quad(M_{\mu\nu})=\left(\begin{array}[]{rrrr}0&H_{1}&H_{2}&H_{3}\\
-H_{1}&0&D_{3}&-D_{2}\\
-H_{2}&-D_{3}&0&D_{1}\\
-H_{3}&D_{2}&-D_{1}&0\end{array}\right),

(2.24)

T^{00}=\mathfrak{e},\quad T^{0j}=\frac{\mathfrak{S}_{j}}{c}=c\mathfrak{g}_{j}=T^{j0},\quad T^{jk}=\mathfrak{T}_{jk},\quad j,k=1,2,3,

where
\bm{j}=(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3})
,
\bm{E}=(E_{1},E_{2},E_{3})
etc. We remark that the tensors
F
and
M
themselves have dimension
[Charge]
; indeed, each of their components has dimension
\texttt{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\texttt{L}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\texttt{T}^{-1}
, as mentioned before, while each of the basis tensors
\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\otimes\mathrm{d}x^{\nu}
for
\mathcal{T}^{0}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
has dimension
\texttt{L}^{2}
, yielding for example

\dim(F)=\dim\left(E_{1}\ \mathrm{d}x^{0}\otimes\mathrm{d}x^{1}+\cdots\right)=\texttt{M}^{\frac{1}{2}}\texttt{L}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\texttt{T}^{-1}\texttt{L}^{2}=\texttt{[Charge]}.

The reader is now invited to check that the continuity equation (
2.2
), Maxwell equations (
2.3
), vacuum law (
2.6
) and field-energy and –momenta conservation laws (
2.9
) and (
2.10
) can be written as follows:

(2.25)

\displaystyle\delta J=0,

(2.26)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}F=0,\quad\mathrm{d}M=\dfrac{4\pi}{c}{\star}J,

(2.27)

\displaystyle M={\star}\big(F+\varkappa^{-2}\mathrm{d}\delta F\big),

(2.28)

\displaystyle\partial_{\nu}T^{\mu\nu}=-\dfrac{1}{c}\mathchoice{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}J^{\lambda}.

The tensors
J
,
F
,
M
and
T
satisfying the above tensorial equations (the first three of which are covariant) should be regarded as the intrinsic constructs of electromagnetism, while the sources and fields from the previous subsection are merely observer-dependent functions that constitute their components in a given inertial coordinate system.

Remark 2.4
.

The reader should remain aware that different sign conventions exist in the literature for the metric signature, the components of
F
and
M
, and the definitions of
{\star}
and
\delta
. Usually a choice of placement of minus signs in the definitions of these objects is adopted in order for certain standard formulas to always remain the same. As an example: For a test particle of charge
q
, 4-velocity vector
U
and 4-momentum vector
P
, equations (
2.7
) and (
2.8
) can be collected together as

(2.29)

\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{\mu}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}=qF_{\mu\nu}U^{\nu},\quad\mu=0,1,2,3,

where
\tau
denotes the particle’s proper time. The appearance of this formula is almost universal in the physics literature. But note that what features on its left side are the
index-lowered
components of
P
, whose definition depends on the metric. In particular, if the
({+}\,{-}\,{-}\,{-})
metric signature were to be used instead of ours, then
(F_{\mu\nu})
would need to be defined as the negative of what we introduced above, in order to keep (
2.29
) unaltered.

Next we present the
Lagrangian formulation
of both the Maxwell-Maxwell and the Maxwell-BLTP systems of tensor equations. For this purpose, a
four-potential

A
must be introduced: The equation
\mathrm{d}F=0
is equivalent (at least on simply connected regions of spacetime) to the existence of a 1-form field
A
such that

(2.30)

F=\mathrm{d}A.

It can be easily checked that this relation is equivalent to equations (
2.4
) for
\bm{E}
and
\bm{B}
in terms of the potentials
\varphi
and
\bm{A}=(A_{1},A_{2},A_{3})
when
A
is defined in the inertial coordinate system
(x^{\mu})
by

(2.31)

A=-\varphi\ \mathrm{d}x^{0}+\sum_{k=1}^{3}A_{k}\ \mathrm{d}x^{k}.

Then the remaining equations of EM theory can be put together as one equation for
A
: We take a
{\star}
on both sides of the
M
equation (
2.26
) and replace
M
in it by the expression given in (
2.27
) to find the fourth-order equation

(2.32)

\frac{1}{c}J-\frac{1}{4\pi}\delta\mathrm{d}A-\frac{\varkappa^{-2}}{4\pi}\delta\mathrm{d}\delta\mathrm{d}A=0.

It is this equation that arises from an action principle expressed in terms of the dynamical variable
A
:

Proposition 2.1
.

Let a 1-form field
J
satisfying
\delta J=0
be given. For an arbitrary 1-form field
A
, consider the Lagrangian 4-form

(2.33)

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{EM}}(A)=\frac{1}{c}A\wedge{\star}J-\frac{1}{8\pi}(\mathrm{d}A)\wedge({\star}\mathrm{d}A)-\frac{\varkappa^{-2}}{8\pi}(\delta\mathrm{d}A)\wedge({\star}\delta\mathrm{d}A),

and define the action functional
\mathcal{S}(A;U)=\int_{U}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{EM}}(A)
for arbitrary open sets
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{4}
. Then equation
(
2.32
)
is equivalent to the Euler-Lagrange equations of
\mathcal{S}
, that is, in an inertial coordinate system,

(2.34)

\frac{\partial\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}}{\partial A_{\xi}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}\left(\frac{\partial\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}}{\partial(\partial_{\mu}A_{\xi})}\right)+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{\mu}\partial x^{\nu}}\left(\frac{\partial\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}}{\partial(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}A_{\xi})}\right)=0,\quad\xi=0,1,2,3,

where
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{EM}}(A)=\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}(A,\partial A,\partial^{2}A)\ \mathrm{vol}_{\eta}
expresses
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{EM}}
as a scalar
\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}
multiplied by the canonical volume form
\mathrm{vol}_{\eta}=\mathrm{d}x^{0}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{3}=:\mathrm{d}^{4}x
of Minkowski space, with
\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}
considered as a function of the components of
A
and of their derivatives up to second order.

Proof.

Using the definitions (
1.5
) and (
1.4
) of the Hodge star and of the inner product
\langle\ast,\ast\rangle_{\eta}
on
p
-forms, we find that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{EM}}=\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}\ \mathrm{vol}_{\eta}
for the scalar

(2.35)

\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}=\frac{1}{c}A_{\alpha}J^{\alpha}-\frac{1}{16\pi}(\mathrm{d}A)_{\alpha\beta}(\mathrm{d}A)^{\alpha\beta}-\frac{\varkappa^{-2}}{8\pi}(\delta\mathrm{d}A)_{\alpha}(\delta\mathrm{d}A)^{\alpha}=\frac{1}{c}\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}^{(1)}-\frac{1}{16\pi}\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}^{(2)}-\frac{\varkappa^{-2}}{8\pi}\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}^{(3)},

where each
\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}^{(j)}
only depends on the
(j-1)
-st–order derivatives of the components
A_{\alpha}
. Now we need to write
\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}
explicitly in terms of these components. For
\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}^{(1)}=A_{\alpha}J^{\alpha}
, the job is already done. For
\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}^{(2)}
, we use the coordinate expression (
1.8
) for the exterior derivative:

\displaystyle\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{d}A)_{\alpha\beta}(\mathrm{d}A)^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{d}A)_{\alpha\beta}\eta^{\alpha\rho}\eta^{\beta\sigma}(\mathrm{d}A)_{\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle=\big(\partial_{\alpha}A_{\beta}-\partial_{\beta}A_{\alpha}\big)\eta^{\alpha\rho}\eta^{\beta\sigma}\big(\partial_{\rho}A_{\sigma}-\partial_{\sigma}A_{\rho}\big)

\displaystyle=2\eta^{\alpha\rho}\eta^{\beta\sigma}(\partial_{\alpha}A_{\beta})\big(\partial_{\rho}A_{\sigma}-\partial_{\sigma}A_{\rho}\big).

For
\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}^{(3)}
, we also use the coordinate expression (
1.9
) for the codifferential, noting that
\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert=1
and
\partial_{\xi}\eta_{\mu\nu}=0
:

\displaystyle\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=(\delta\mathrm{d}A)_{\alpha}(\delta\mathrm{d}A)^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\eta_{\alpha\rho}(\delta\mathrm{d}A)^{\rho}(\delta\mathrm{d}A)^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\eta_{\alpha\rho}\big(-\partial_{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}A)^{\lambda\rho}\big)\big(-\partial_{\theta}(\mathrm{d}A)^{\theta\alpha}\big)

\displaystyle=\eta_{\alpha\rho}\partial_{\lambda}\big(\eta^{\lambda\sigma}\eta^{\rho\tau}(\mathrm{d}A)_{\sigma\tau}\big)\partial_{\theta}\big(\eta^{\theta\phi}\eta^{\alpha\chi}(\mathrm{d}A)_{\phi\chi}\big)

\displaystyle=\eta_{\alpha\rho}\eta^{\lambda\sigma}\eta^{\rho\tau}\eta^{\theta\phi}\eta^{\alpha\chi}\partial_{\lambda}\big(\partial_{\sigma}A_{\tau}-\partial_{\tau}A_{\sigma}\big)\partial_{\theta}\big(\partial_{\phi}A_{\chi}-\partial_{\chi}A_{\phi}\big)

\displaystyle=2\eta_{\alpha\rho}\eta^{\lambda\sigma}\eta^{\rho\tau}\eta^{\theta\phi}\eta^{\alpha\chi}\partial_{\lambda}\big(\partial_{\sigma}A_{\tau}\big)\partial_{\theta}\big(\partial_{\phi}A_{\chi}-\partial_{\chi}A_{\phi}\big)

\displaystyle=2\eta^{\lambda\sigma}\eta^{\theta\phi}\eta^{\alpha\chi}(\partial_{\lambda}\partial_{\sigma}A_{\alpha})(\partial_{\theta}\partial_{\phi}A_{\chi})-2\eta^{\lambda\sigma}\eta^{\alpha\phi}\eta^{\theta\chi}(\partial_{\lambda}\partial_{\sigma}A_{\alpha})(\partial_{\theta}\partial_{\phi}A_{\chi}).

Then, for each fixed
\mu,\nu,\xi
, we differentiate the above expressions with respect to
A_{\xi}
,
\partial_{\mu}A_{\xi}
and
\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}A_{\xi}
respectively. The use of the Kronecker Delta
\delta^{\ast}_{\ast}
helps with writing this calculation in a compact form, due to

\frac{\partial A_{\alpha}}{\partial A_{\xi}}=\delta^{\xi}_{\alpha},\quad\frac{\partial(\partial_{\rho}A_{\alpha})}{\partial(\partial_{\mu}A_{\xi})}=\delta^{\mu}_{\rho}\delta^{\xi}_{\alpha},\quad\frac{\partial(\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\sigma}A_{\alpha})}{\partial(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}A_{\xi})}=\delta^{\mu}_{\rho}\delta^{\nu}_{\sigma}\delta^{\xi}_{\alpha}.

We find

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}^{(1)}}{\partial A_{\xi}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial A_{\xi}}\big(A_{\alpha}J^{\alpha}\big)=\delta_{\alpha}^{\xi}J^{\alpha}=J^{\xi},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}^{(2)}}{\partial(\partial_{\mu}A_{\xi})}

\displaystyle=2\frac{\partial}{\partial(\partial_{\mu}A_{\xi})}\bigg(\eta^{\alpha\rho}\eta^{\beta\sigma}(\partial_{\alpha}A_{\beta})(\partial_{\rho}A_{\sigma}-\partial_{\sigma}A_{\rho})\bigg)

\displaystyle=2\eta^{\alpha\rho}\eta^{\beta\sigma}\delta^{\mu}_{\alpha}\delta^{\xi}_{\beta}(\partial_{\rho}A_{\sigma}-\partial_{\sigma}A_{\rho})+2\eta^{\alpha\rho}\eta^{\beta\sigma}(\partial_{\alpha}A_{\beta})(\delta^{\mu}_{\rho}\delta^{\xi}_{\sigma}-\delta^{\mu}_{\sigma}\delta^{\xi}_{\rho})

\displaystyle=2\eta^{\mu\rho}\eta^{\xi\sigma}(\partial_{\rho}A_{\sigma}-\partial_{\sigma}A_{\rho})+2\eta^{\alpha\mu}\eta^{\beta\xi}\partial_{\alpha}A_{\beta}-2\eta^{\alpha\xi}\eta^{\beta\mu}\partial_{\alpha}A_{\beta}

\displaystyle=2\partial^{\mu}A^{\xi}-2\partial^{\xi}A^{\mu}+2\partial^{\mu}A^{\xi}-2\partial^{\xi}A^{\mu}

\displaystyle=4\partial^{\mu}A^{\xi}-4\partial^{\xi}A^{\mu},

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}^{(3)}}{\partial(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}A_{\xi})}

\displaystyle=2\frac{\partial}{\partial(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}A_{\xi})}\bigg(\eta^{\lambda\sigma}\eta^{\theta\phi}\eta^{\alpha\chi}(\partial_{\lambda}\partial_{\sigma}A_{\alpha})(\partial_{\theta}\partial_{\phi}A_{\chi})-\eta^{\lambda\sigma}\eta^{\alpha\phi}\eta^{\theta\chi}(\partial_{\lambda}\partial_{\sigma}A_{\alpha})(\partial_{\theta}\partial_{\phi}A_{\chi})\bigg)

\displaystyle=2\eta^{\lambda\sigma}\eta^{\theta\phi}\eta^{\alpha\chi}\delta^{\mu}_{\lambda}\delta^{\nu}_{\sigma}\delta^{\xi}_{\alpha}(\partial_{\theta}\partial_{\phi}A_{\chi})+2\eta^{\lambda\sigma}\eta^{\theta\phi}\eta^{\alpha\chi}(\partial_{\lambda}\partial_{\sigma}A_{\alpha})\delta^{\mu}_{\theta}\delta^{\nu}_{\phi}\delta^{\xi}_{\chi}

\displaystyle\qquad-2\eta^{\lambda\sigma}\eta^{\alpha\phi}\eta^{\theta\chi}\delta^{\mu}_{\lambda}\delta^{\nu}_{\sigma}\delta^{\xi}_{\alpha}(\partial_{\theta}\partial_{\phi}A_{\chi})-2\eta^{\lambda\sigma}\eta^{\alpha\phi}\eta^{\theta\chi}(\partial_{\lambda}\partial_{\sigma}A_{\alpha})\delta^{\mu}_{\theta}\delta^{\nu}_{\phi}\delta^{\xi}_{\chi}

\displaystyle=2\eta^{\mu\nu}\eta^{\theta\phi}\eta^{\xi\chi}\partial_{\theta}\partial_{\phi}A_{\chi}+2\eta^{\lambda\sigma}\eta^{\mu\nu}\eta^{\alpha\xi}\partial_{\lambda}\partial_{\sigma}A_{\alpha}-2\eta^{\mu\nu}\eta^{\xi\phi}\eta^{\theta\chi}\partial_{\theta}\partial_{\phi}A_{\chi}-2\eta^{\lambda\sigma}\eta^{\alpha\nu}\eta^{\mu\xi}\partial_{\lambda}\partial_{\sigma}A_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=2\eta^{\mu\nu}\partial^{\phi}\partial_{\phi}A^{\xi}+2\eta^{\mu\nu}\partial^{\sigma}\partial_{\sigma}A^{\xi}-2\eta^{\mu\nu}\partial^{\chi}\partial^{\xi}A_{\chi}-2\eta^{\mu\xi}\partial^{\sigma}\partial_{\sigma}A^{\nu}.

The coordinate derivatives of the last two of these expressions, as featured in (
2.34
), are

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}\left(\frac{\partial\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}^{(2)}}{\partial(\partial_{\mu}A_{\xi})}\right)

\displaystyle=4\partial_{\mu}\big(\partial^{\mu}A^{\xi}-\partial^{\xi}A^{\mu}\big)

\displaystyle=4\square A^{\xi}-4\partial^{\xi}\mathrm{div}(A),

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{\mu}\partial x^{\nu}}\left(\frac{\partial\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}^{(3)}}{\partial(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}A_{\xi})}\right)

\displaystyle=2\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\big(\eta^{\mu\nu}\partial^{\phi}\partial_{\phi}A^{\xi}+\eta^{\mu\nu}\partial^{\sigma}\partial_{\sigma}A^{\xi}-\eta^{\mu\nu}\partial^{\chi}\partial^{\xi}A_{\chi}-\eta^{\mu\xi}\partial^{\sigma}\partial_{\sigma}A^{\nu}\big)

\displaystyle=2\partial^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\partial^{\phi}\partial_{\phi}A^{\xi}+2\partial^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\partial^{\sigma}\partial_{\sigma}A^{\xi}-2\partial^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}\partial^{\xi}\partial^{\chi}A_{\chi}-2\partial^{\xi}\partial_{\nu}\partial^{\sigma}\partial_{\sigma}A^{\nu}

\displaystyle=2\square^{2}A^{\xi}+2\square^{2}A^{\xi}-2\partial^{\xi}\square\mathrm{div}(A)-2\partial^{\xi}\square\mathrm{div}(A)

\displaystyle=4\square^{2}A^{\xi}-4\partial^{\xi}\square\mathrm{div}(A),

where we used the symbols
\mathrm{div}(A)=\partial_{\lambda}A^{\lambda}=\partial^{\lambda}A_{\lambda}
and
\square=\partial_{\lambda}\partial^{\lambda}=\partial^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}
. Thus the Euler-Lagrange equation (
2.34
) becomes

(2.36)

\frac{1}{c}J^{\xi}-\frac{1}{4\pi}\big(\partial^{\xi}\mathrm{div}(A)-\square A^{\xi}\big)-\frac{\varkappa^{-2}}{4\pi}\big(\square^{2}A^{\xi}-\partial^{\xi}\square\mathrm{div}(A)\big)=0.

Finally, we need to compare this to the coordinate expression of the target equation (
2.32
):

(2.37)

0=\frac{1}{c}J-\frac{1}{4\pi}\delta\mathrm{d}A-\frac{\varkappa^{-2}}{4\pi}\delta\mathrm{d}\delta\mathrm{d}A=\frac{1}{c}B_{(1)}-\frac{1}{4\pi}B_{(2)}-\frac{\varkappa^{-2}}{4\pi}B_{(3)},

whose terms are found to be

\displaystyle B_{(1)}^{\xi}

\displaystyle=J^{\xi},

\displaystyle B_{(2)}^{\xi}

\displaystyle=(\delta\mathrm{d}A)^{\xi}=-\partial_{\lambda}\big(\eta^{\lambda\rho}\eta^{\xi\sigma}(\partial_{\rho}A_{\sigma}-\partial_{\sigma}A_{\rho})\big)=-\partial^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}A^{\xi}+\partial^{\rho}\partial^{\xi}A_{\rho}=-\square A^{\xi}+\partial^{\xi}\mathrm{div}(A),

\displaystyle B_{(3)}^{\xi}

\displaystyle=(\delta\mathrm{d}\delta\mathrm{d}A)^{\xi}

\displaystyle=-\square(\delta\mathrm{d}A)^{\xi}+\partial^{\xi}\mathrm{div}(\delta\mathrm{d}A)

\displaystyle=-\square(\delta\mathrm{d}A)^{\xi}+\partial^{\xi}\partial_{\lambda}(\delta\mathrm{d}A)^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=-\square\big(-\square A^{\xi}+\partial^{\xi}\mathrm{div}(A)\big)+\partial^{\xi}\partial_{\lambda}\big(-\square A^{\lambda}+\partial^{\lambda}\mathrm{div}(A)\big)

\displaystyle=\square^{2}A^{\xi}-\partial^{\xi}\square\mathrm{div}(A)-\partial^{\xi}\square\mathrm{div}(A)+\partial^{\xi}\square\mathrm{div}(A)

\displaystyle=\square^{2}A^{\xi}-\partial^{\xi}\square\mathrm{div}(A).

Then (
2.37
) is immediately seen to also be equivalent to (
2.36
).
∎

Remark 2.5
.

The Lagrangian 4-form (
2.33
), which looks like

(2.38)

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{EM}}(A)=\frac{1}{c}A\wedge{\star}J-\frac{1}{8\pi}F\wedge{\star}F-\frac{\varkappa^{-2}}{8\pi}(\delta F)\wedge({\star}\delta F)

when written in terms of
F=\mathrm{d}A
, can be used to explain why BLTP theory is a
natural
Lagrangian generalization of the conventional Maxwell theory of electromagnetism: If one wishes to generalize the latter while retaining the
linear
nature of the field equations, one needs to take the Lagrangian given by the first two terms in (
2.38
) and add to it a new term that is
quadratic
in quantities related to
F
. But there are no quadratic invariants of
F
itself left to use apart from the term
F\wedge{\star}F
that is already present. One might consider using
F\wedge F
, but this expression would not contribute anything to the Euler-Lagrange equations on account of it being an exact differential, namely of
A\wedge F
. Thus one is led to consider the next best thing: quadratic invariants involving
derivatives
of
F
, such as the term
(\delta F)\wedge({\star}\delta F)
that appears in (
2.38
).

We close this section by deriving a stress-energy tensor
(T^{\mu\nu})
from which the expression for
\mathfrak{e}
claimed in (
2.11
) can be extracted. As mentioned before, formulas for the fields’ (energy/momenta)-(density/flux) quantities are derived by rewriting the right sides of (
2.9
) and (
2.10
) into the form
\partial_{t}(\ast)+\nabla\cdot(\bm{\ast})
. In tensorial language, however, what this amounts to is a rewriting of the right side of the equivalent equation (
2.28
) into the form
\partial_{\nu}T^{\mu\nu}
for some symmetric tensor
T
.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let a 1-form field
J
and two 2-form fields
F
and
M
satisfy Maxwell’s equations
(
2.26
)
and the vacuum law
(
2.27
)
. Let an inertial coordinate system be fixed. Then the relation

\partial_{\nu}T^{\mu\nu}=-\frac{1}{c}\mathchoice{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}J^{\lambda}

holds for the symmetric
(2,0)
-tensor
T
whose components are defined by

(2.39)

T^{\mu\nu}=T^{\mu\nu}_{\mathrm{(M)}}+\varkappa^{-2}T^{\mu\nu}_{\mathrm{(BLTP)}}

for

\displaystyle 4\pi T^{\mu\nu}_{\mathrm{(M)}}=F^{\alpha\mu}\mathchoice{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{F^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}-\frac{\eta^{\mu\nu}}{4}F_{\alpha\beta}F^{\alpha\beta},

\displaystyle 4\pi T^{\mu\nu}_{\mathrm{(BLTP)}}=F^{\alpha\mu}\mathchoice{(\mathrm{d}\delta F)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{(\mathrm{d}\delta F)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{(\mathrm{d}\delta F)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{(\mathrm{d}\delta F)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}+F^{\alpha\nu}\mathchoice{(\mathrm{d}\delta F)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{(\mathrm{d}\delta F)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{(\mathrm{d}\delta F)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{(\mathrm{d}\delta F)^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}_{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}-\frac{\eta^{\mu\nu}}{2}F_{\alpha\beta}(\mathrm{d}\delta F)^{\alpha\beta}-(\delta F)^{\mu}(\delta F)^{\nu}+\frac{\eta^{\mu\nu}}{2}(\delta F)^{\alpha}(\delta F)_{\alpha}.

Proof.

Equation (
2.32
), recast in terms of the tensor
F
given by
F=\mathrm{d}A
, becomes

\frac{4\pi}{c}J^{\lambda}=(\delta F)^{\lambda}+\varkappa^{-2}(\delta\mathrm{d}\delta F)^{\lambda}=-\partial_{\nu}F^{\nu\lambda}-\varkappa^{-2}\partial_{\nu}(\mathrm{d}\delta F)^{\nu\lambda},

where we used formula (
1.9
) in inertial coordinates to find
\delta\psi
for the 2-forms
\psi=F
and
\psi=\mathrm{d}\delta F
. Hence, we need to find a symmetric tensor
(T^{\mu\nu})
satisfying

4\pi\partial_{\nu}T^{\mu\nu}=-\frac{4\pi}{c}\mathchoice{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}J^{\lambda}=\mathchoice{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\make \displaystyle=\partial_{\nu}\big(\mathchoice{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}F^{\nu\lambda}\big)-F^{\nu\lambda}\partial_{\nu}\mathchoice{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{F (2.40)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\nu}U_{(1)}^{\mu\nu}-\eta^{\mu\alpha}F^{\nu\lambda}\partial_{\nu}F_{\alpha\lambda}\qquad\text{where}\quad\boxed{U_{(1)}^{\mu\nu}:=\mathchoice{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}F^{\nu\lambda}}.

By switching the positions of the upper and lower copies of the index
\lambda
, we realize that
U_{(1)}
is symmetric:

U_{(1)}^{\mu\nu}=\mathchoice{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}{F^{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\lambda}}}}F^{\nu\lambda}=\mathchoice{F^{{{\mu}{\lambda}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74307pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.85834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.04167pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{F^{{{\mu}{\lambda}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.8623
