2,g=g^{\mathrm{T}}],

where the change in the first term on the last step is explained by

\partial_{\lambda}g_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\lambda}\big(g_{\sigma\mu}g_{\tau\nu}g^{\sigma\tau}\big)=(\partial_{\lambda}g_{\sigma\mu})g_{\tau\nu}g^{\sigma\tau}+g_{\sigma\mu}(\partial_{\lambda}g_{\tau\nu})g^{\sigma\tau}+g_{\sigma\mu}g_{\tau\nu}(\partial_{\lambda}g^{\sigma\tau})=2\partial_{\lambda}g_{\mu\nu}+g_{\sigma\mu}g_{\tau\nu}(\partial_{\lambda}g^{\sigma\tau}).

Similarly, differentiating (
3.16
) with respect to
\partial_{\xi}g^{\mu\nu}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(\delta F)_{\alpha}}{\partial(\partial_{\xi}g^{\mu\nu})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}g_{\sigma\tau}\delta^{\xi}_{\lambda}\delta^{\sigma}_{\mu}\delta^{\tau}_{\nu}g^{\lambda\zeta}g^{\alpha\eta}F_{\zeta\eta}-\delta^{\xi}_{\lambda}\delta^{\lambda}_{\mu}\delta^{\zeta}_{\nu}g^{\alpha\eta}F_{\zeta\eta}-g^{\lambda\zeta}\delta^{\xi}_{\lambda}\delta^{\alpha}_{\mu}\delta^{\eta}_{\nu}F_{\zeta\eta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}g^{\xi\zeta}g^{\alpha\eta}F_{\zeta\eta}-\delta^{\xi}_{\mu}g^{\alpha\eta}F_{\nu\eta}-g^{\xi\zeta}\delta^{\alpha}_{\mu}F_{\zeta\nu}

(3.20)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}F^{\xi\alpha}+\delta^{\xi}_{\mu}\mathchoice{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}-\delta^{\alpha}_{\mu}\mathchoice{F^{{{\xi}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.6389pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.6389pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53125pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{ 2,g=g^{\mathrm{T}}].

What is left is to plug (
3.15
), (
3.19
) and (
3.20
) back into formula (
3.11
) for
\widetilde{T}_{\mu\nu}^{(3)}
. The long term in parentheses on line (

) simplifies considerably, and the symmetrization operation, carried over from the previous steps, turns out to not matter for this part:

\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle(\delta F)^{\alpha}-4\frac{\partial(\delta F)^{\alpha}}{\partial g^{\mu\nu}}+4\Gamma^{\sigma}_{\sigma\xi}\frac{\partial(\delta F)^{\alpha}}{\partial(\partial_{\xi}g^{\mu\nu})}+4\partial_{\xi}\left(\frac{\partial(\delta F)^{\alpha}}{\partial(\partial_{\xi}g^{\mu\nu})}\right)

\displaystyle=-g_{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\sigma}_{\sigma\lambda}F^{\lambda\alpha}-g_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\lambda}F^{\lambda\alpha}

F)_{\alpha}+4\mathchoice{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}\partial_{\mu}(\delta F)_{\alpha}-4\mathchoice{F^{{{\xi}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.6389pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.6389pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53125pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\xi}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.6389pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.6389pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53125pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\xi}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.6389pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.6389pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53125pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\xi}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.6389pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.6389pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.14375pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.53125pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}\partial_{\xi}(\delta F)_{\mu}

\displaystyle=g_{\mu\nu}F^{\xi\alpha}\partial_{\xi}(\delta F)_{\alpha}+g_{\mu\nu}F^{\xi\alpha}\partial_{\xi}(\delta F)_{\alpha}+4\mathchoice{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}\partial_{\mu}(\delta F)_{\alpha}-4\mathchoice{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}\partial_{\alpha}(\delta F)_{\mu}

\displaystyle=g_{\mu\nu}F^{\xi\alpha}\partial_{\xi}(\delta F)_{\alpha}-g_{\mu\nu}F^{\xi\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}(\delta F)_{\xi}+4\mathchoice{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}\big(\partial_{\mu}(\delta F)_{\alpha}-\partial_{\alpha}(\delta F)_{\mu}\big)

\displaystyle=g_{\mu\nu}F^{\xi\alpha}(\mathrm{d}\delta F)_{\xi\alpha}-4\mathchoice{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}(\mathrm{d}\delta F)_{\alpha\mu}\quad[+(\mu\leftrightarrow\nu),\div 2,g=g^{\mathrm{T}}]

\displaystyle=g_{\mu\nu}F^{\xi\alpha}(\mathrm{d}\delta F)_{\xi\alpha}-2\mathchoice{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}(\mathrm{d}\delta F)_{\alpha\mu}-2\mathchoice{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}(\mathrm{d}\delta F)_{\alpha\nu}.

Going back to (
3.11
), we have proved

\widetilde{T}_{\mu\nu}^{(3)}=-2\mathchoice{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}}}}(\mathrm{d}\delta F)_{\alpha\nu}-2\mathchoice{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}{F^{{{\alpha}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\nu}}}}(\mathrm{d}\delta F)_{\alpha\mu}+g_{\mu\nu}F^{\alpha\beta}(\mathrm{d}\delta F)_{\alpha\beta}+2(\delta F)_{\mu}(\delta F)_{\nu}-g_{\mu\nu}(\delta F)^{\alpha}(\delta F)_{\alpha},

and using (
3.10
) we finally arrive at precisely the
T^{(3)}_{\mu\nu}
that we wanted.

The
j=1
term, unimportant for us, is all that is left: Given
\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}^{(1)}=J^{\alpha}A_{\alpha}=g^{\alpha\beta}J_{\beta}A_{\alpha}
, we get

\displaystyle T^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}=\widetilde{T}^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=g_{\mu\nu}\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}^{(1)}-2\frac{\partial\ell_{\mathrm{EM}}^{(1)}}{\partial g^{\mu\nu}}

\displaystyle=g_{\mu\nu}J^{\alpha}A_{\alpha}-2\delta^{\alpha}_{\mu}\delta^{\beta}_{\nu}J_{\beta}A_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=g_{\mu\nu}J^{\alpha}A_{\alpha}-2J_{\nu}A_{\mu}\quad[+(\mu\leftrightarrow\nu),\div 2,g=g^{\mathrm{T}}]

\displaystyle=g_{\mu\nu}J^{\alpha}A_{\alpha}-J_{\mu}A_{\nu}-J_{\nu}A_{\mu}.\qed

Remark 3.1
.

Our formula (
3.3
) for
(T_{\mu\nu})
is the same as the one that appears in the original paper
[
13
]
by Podolsky as well as the ones in the recent works
[
17
]
and
[
7
]
. The appendix of the latter presents a different derivation of this formula, not relying so heavily on component calculations, but we do point out that their end result (formula (128) on p. 28) contains an error: The term
F_{cb}\partial^{a}\partial_{a}\mathchoice{F^{{{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d}}}}{F^{{{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d}}}}{F^{{{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d}}}}{F^{{{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d}}}}
is not symmetrized with respect to the pair of indices
(c,d)
.

3.3.
The E-M-BLTP system for a static point charge

From now on, we assume that a single point particle of electric charge
Q
, obeying either Maxwell-Maxwell or Maxwell-BLTP theory, sits statically alone in the Universe, and we consider the electrovacuum spacetime
\mathcal{M}
“around it.” On account of its symmetries, this must be a static, spherically symmetric 4-manifold whose topological boundary (not considered part of
\mathcal{M}
) is a line representing the “singularity worldline” of the particle. It is well-known (see
[
12
]
) that a system of coordinates
(ct,r,\theta,\phi)\in\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)\times[0,\pi]\times[0,2\pi)
can be found in which the metric of such a spacetime takes a diagonal form, with the
\mathrm{d}(ct)^{2}
and
\mathrm{d}r^{2}
terms accompanied by two unknown functions of the so-called
area-radius
coordinate
r
, and the
\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}
and
\mathrm{d}\phi^{2}
terms figuring as in the usual spherical volume element. We choose to write it in the form

(3.21)

g=-\frac{e^{2\alpha(r)}}{\beta(r)}\ \mathrm{d}(ct)\otimes\mathrm{d}(ct)+\beta(r)\ \mathrm{d}r\otimes\mathrm{d}r+r^{2}\big(\mathrm{d}\theta\otimes\mathrm{d}\theta+\sin^{2}\theta\ \mathrm{d}\phi\otimes\mathrm{d}\phi\big)

for undetermined functions
\alpha
and
\beta
, with a requirement that

(3.22)

\beta(r)>0\quad\text{for all }r>0.

By convention, the unknowns
\alpha,\beta
are dimensionless, with the differentials
\mathrm{d}(ct)^{2}
,
\mathrm{d}r^{2}
both carrying the dimension of
\texttt{[Length]}^{2}
, while the angles
\theta
,
\phi
and differentials
\mathrm{d}\theta
,
\mathrm{d}\phi
are dimensionless, with their coefficients satisfying
\dim(r^{2})=\dim(r^{2}\sin\theta)=\texttt{[Length]}^{2}
. We will use the symbols
t,r,\theta,\phi
as indices in tensor equations written in the
(ct,r,\theta,\phi)
coordinates; for example, the first metric coefficient
-\beta^{-1}e^{2\alpha}
is denoted by
g_{tt}
instead of
g_{(ct)(ct)}
or
g_{00}
.

We assume these coordinates to be valid
globally
on
\mathcal{M}
. This means, in particular, that the particle is modeled as a
naked singularity
, not hidden behind any event horizons (where
\beta
would take the value 0). We also assume the physically motivated condition of
asymptotic flatness
of the spacetime, which for a metric of the form (
3.21
) implies, among a stronger set of conditions (see
[
15
]
, p. 528), that

(3.23)

\lim_{r\to\infty}\alpha(r)=0,\quad\beta(r)=1+O(r^{-1})\quad\text{as }r\to\infty.

An interpretation of these conditions is that, away from the influence of the point particle, the metric approximates the Minkowski metric expressed in spherical coordinates, which has
\alpha\equiv 0
and
\beta\equiv 1
. In particular, the following limit exists:

(3.24)

M_{\mathrm{ADM}}:=\frac{c^{2}}{G}\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{r}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{\beta(r)}\right).

With this,
\beta
takes the form

\beta(r)=\left(1-\frac{2GM_{\mathrm{ADM}}}{c^{2}r}+O(r^{-2})\right)^{-1}\quad\text{as }r\to\infty.

The constant (
3.24
) is called the spacetime’s
ADM mass
, representing the singularity’s active gravitational mass as measured by a distant observer. For this reason, physically relevant spacetimes should satisfy
M_{\mathrm{ADM}}>0
. We also define the spacetime’s
bare mass
as the limit of the same quantity on the other end of the domain
(0,\infty)
, in case it exists:

(3.25)

M_{\mathrm{bare}}:=\frac{c^{2}}{G}\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{r}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{\beta(r)}\right).

If
M_{\mathrm{bare}}
is defined, it is not hard to see that condition (
3.22
) implies
M_{\mathrm{bare}}\leq 0
. A physical interpretation for this constant, however, is not immediately apparent, nor is it clear whether
\left\lvert M_{\text{bare}}\right\rvert
should have any relation to the inertial mass of the particle. We will see in equations (
4.31
) and (
4.33
) that physically meaningful solutions to our problem will have
\left\lvert M_{\text{bare}}\right\rvert\gg M_{\mathrm{ADM}}
.

Next we describe the electromagnetic quantities in
\mathcal{M}
. The Faraday tensor
F\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{2}(\mathcal{M})
also has to obey staticity and spherical symmetry, properties which, by definition, are expressed in terms of vanishing Lie derivatives: The equation
\mathcal{L}_{X}F=0
must hold for each of the Killing vector fields
X\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{M})
associated to the properties of
\mathcal{M}
being static and spherically symmetric. This condition imposes (see
[
5
]
, p. 254) that the only nonzero components of
F
in our coordinates are
F_{tr}=-F_{rt}
(which must be a general function of
r
) and
F_{\theta\phi}=-F_{\phi\theta}
(which must have the form
\psi(r)\sin\theta
for
\psi
being a general function of
r
). However, a nonzero
F_{\theta\phi}
would signify the presence of magnetic fields, which is unphysical for a single point source. Thus we assume that only the components
F_{tr}=-F_{rt}
are not identically zero. We choose to call
F_{tr}=:e^{\alpha}\varphi^{\prime}
for an unknown
electric potential

\varphi:(0,\infty)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
, and therefore

(3.26)

F=e^{\alpha(r)}\varphi^{\prime}(r)\ \mathrm{d}(ct)\wedge\mathrm{d}r,

while
\varphi
is defined so as to vanish at infinity:

(3.27)

\varphi(r)=-\int_{r}^{\infty}\varphi^{\prime}(s)\ \mathrm{d}s=-\int_{r}^{\infty}e^{-\alpha(s)}F_{tr}(s)\ \mathrm{d}s.

See subsection A.1 in the appendix for an explanation of why the factor
e^{\alpha}
is present in (
3.26
). As it turns out, the Einstein and Maxwell equations are most easily written not in terms of
\varphi
or
\varphi^{\prime}
, but rather in terms of the same
electric-deviation scalar
as introduced in (
2.17
) in the context of SR:

(3.28)

\chi(r):=\frac{1}{Q}r^{2}\varphi^{\prime}(r)+1,

which is dimensionless: Since
\texttt{[Charge]}=\dim(F)=\dim(\varphi^{\prime})\dim(\mathrm{d}(ct)\wedge\mathrm{d}r)=\dim(\varphi^{\prime})\texttt{L}^{2}
, we get
\dim(\chi)=\dim(r^{2}/Q)\dim(\varphi^{\prime})=\texttt{L}^{2}\texttt{[Charge]}^{-1}\dim(\varphi^{\prime})=\texttt{[1]}
. In particular (
3.26
) becomes

(3.29)

F=Qe^{\alpha(r)}\frac{\chi(r)-1}{r^{2}}\ \mathrm{d}(ct)\wedge\mathrm{d}r.

We can now formulate the equations of the problem:

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
g
and
F
be the metric
(
3.21
)
and Faraday tensor
(
3.29
)
, depending on sufficiently differentiable unknowns
\alpha,\beta,\chi:(0,\infty)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Then the system of the EM equations
(
2.26
)
,
(
2.27
)
and the Einstein equations
(
1.1
)
with stress-energy tensor given by
(
3.3
)
is equivalent to

(3.30)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\alpha^{\prime}=-\varkappa^{-2}\dfrac{GQ^{2}}{c^{4}}\dfrac{(\chi^{\prime})^{2}}{r^{3}},\\
\\
\beta^{\prime}=\dfrac{\beta(1-\beta)}{r}+\dfrac{GQ^{2}}{c^{4}}\dfrac{\beta^{2}(1-\chi^{2})-\varkappa^{-2}\beta(\chi^{\prime})^{2}}{r^{3}},\\
\\
\varkappa^{-2}\dfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\left(\dfrac{\chi^{\prime}e^{\alpha}}{r^{2}\beta}\right)=\dfrac{\chi e^{\alpha}}{r^{2}},\end{array}\right.

which we call the
Einstein-Maxwell-BLTP (E-M-BLTP) system
.

Proof.

Deriving these equations is “merely” a matter of plugging the above
F
into (
2.26
), (
2.27
), (
1.1
). We carry out this long calculation in subsection A.2 of the appendix.
∎

Next we need to establish an intrinsic way to measure the electric-field energy in our spacetime. We choose a canonical definition of the energy contained in a constant-time slice
\{t=t_{0}\}
of a static spacetime: the conserved
Noether charge
corresponding to the Killing vector field
X=\partial_{t}
that is associated to the property of staticity. To define this concept, consider the 1-form
P\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}(\mathcal{M})
given by

(3.31)

P(Y):=T(X,Y)\quad\text{for all }Y\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{M}),

where
T=(T_{\mu\nu})
is the stress-energy tensor (
3.3
). We compute the divergence of
P
using (
1.10
) and the Leibniz rule for the component expression
P_{\mu}=T_{\mu\nu}X^{\nu}
:

-{\star}\mathrm{d}{\star}P=-\delta P=\nabla^{\mu}P_{\mu}=(\nabla^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu})X^{\nu}+T_{\mu\nu}(\nabla^{\mu}X^{\nu}).

The first term on the right vanishes because
T
is divergence-free. Using the symmetry of
T
and the formula
0=(\mathcal{L}_{X}g)^{\mu\nu}=\nabla^{\mu}X^{\nu}-\nabla^{\nu}X^{\mu}
for the Lie derivative of the metric, this equation turns into

-{\star}\mathrm{d}{\star}P=\frac{1}{2}T_{\mu\nu}(\nabla^{\mu}X^{\nu}-\nabla^{\nu}X^{\mu})=\frac{1}{2}T_{\mu\nu}(\mathcal{L}_{X}g)^{\mu\nu}=0.

In particular
\mathrm{d}{\star}P=0
, and now Stokes’ Theorem implies that the integral
\int_{\{t=t_{0}\}}{\star}P
is constant as a function of
t_{0}
:

\int_{\{t=t_{1}\}}{\star}P-\int_{\{t=t_{0}\}}{\star}P=\int_{\partial\{t_{0}<t<t_{1}\}}{\star}P=\int_{\{t_{0}<t<t_{1}\}}\mathrm{d}{\star}P=0.

Strictly speaking, one needs to consider in this calculation a
bounded
region of the spacetime and then let it grow out towards spatial infinity. Hence this result only holds when
T
is assumed to vanish sufficiently rapidly at spatial infinity, which indeed will be true in our case. We skip these details; we merely want to use this calculation as justification for the following:

Definition 3.1
.

Let
P\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}(\mathcal{M})
be as in (
3.31
). Then the
electric-field energy
in
\mathcal{M}
is defined by the integral (where
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
is arbitrary)

(3.32)

\mathcal{E}:=-\int_{\{t=t_{0}\}}{\star}P=\frac{Q^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{e^{\alpha}}{r^{2}}\left(1-\chi^{2}-\varkappa^{-2}\frac{(\chi^{\prime})^{2}}{\beta}\right)\ \mathrm{d}r.

The explicit calculation of
{\star}P
to derive the above expression in terms of
\alpha,\beta,\chi
will be shown in the appendix, subsection A.3, where the reason for the choice of the normalization constant
-1
in front of the integral will also be explained.

Remark 3.2
.

Using the Maxwell-Maxwell equations (that is, letting
\varkappa=\infty
), system (
3.30
) becomes much simpler:

\alpha^{\prime}=0,\qquad\beta^{\prime}=\frac{\beta(1-\beta)}{r}+\frac{GQ^{2}}{c^{4}}\frac{\beta^{2}(1-\chi^{2})}{r^{3}},\qquad\chi=0.

Its asymptotically flat solution is the so-called
Reissner-Weyl-Nordström
or
RWN
spacetime:

(3.33)

\alpha^{(\mathrm{RWN})}\equiv 0,\qquad\beta^{(\mathrm{RWN})}_{M}(r)=\left(1-\frac{2GM}{c^{2}r}+\frac{GQ^{2}}{c^{4}r^{2}}\right)^{-1},\qquad\chi^{(\mathrm{RWN})}\equiv 0

for an arbitrary parameter
M\in\mathbb{R}
that is quickly seen to correspond to this spacetime’s ADM mass according to (
3.24
). Using (
3.28
), the electric potential (
3.27
) is found to be the same Coulomb potential
\varphi^{(\mathrm{M})}
as in (
2.15
). Furthermore, the condition (
3.22
) for the absence of event horizons in this case is equivalent to
GM<Q^{2}
, which holds — with
lots
of room to spare — for the values of
M
and
Q
corresponding to typical atomic particles. Thus a lonely point charge under the conventional Einstein-Maxwell theory is indeed modeled as a
naked singularity
of a static, spherically symmetric spacetime.

We pointed out in Section
2
that the Coulomb potential
\varphi^{\mathrm{(M)}}
solves the SR equations of BLTP theory for any
0<\varkappa\leq\infty
. We remark now that the analogous fact is true in GR as well: The RWN solution (
3.33
) solves system (
3.30
) for any
0<\varkappa\leq\infty
. This is trivial to check: Since all derivatives of
\chi
in the system come accompanied by a
\varkappa^{-2}
factor, we obtain one and the same reduction of the equations if we either set
\varkappa^{-2}=0
(yielding the system whose solution is RWN) or set
\chi\equiv 0
(which holds for the RWN solution). Nevertheless, RWN must be considered an undesirable solution to the E-M-BLTP system for our purposes because of the divergence of its energy integral and electric potential at
r=0
:

\mathcal{E}=\frac{Q^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{r^{2}}(1-0-0)\ \mathrm{d}r=\frac{Q^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}r}{r^{2}}=\infty,\quad\varphi^{(\mathrm{M})}(0)=\frac{Q}{r}\bigg|_{r=0}=\infty.

A recent result by Cuzinatto et al.
[
6
]
shows that, in a sense made precise in their work, RWN is in fact the only physically relevant
exterior solution
of system (
3.30
) when the spacetime contains a black hole — that is, if the expression of the metric (
3.21
) is only valid in a domain of the form
r\in(r_{0},\infty)
for an
r_{0}>0
where
\beta(r_{0})=0
. So it is remarkable that we will be able to find other solutions in the naked-singularity case.

Our goal in this paper is to show that the E-M-BLTP system in the case
0<\varkappa<\infty
admits solutions whose values for
\mathcal{E}
and
\varphi(0)
are finite. We will also investigate the value of
M_{\mathrm{ADM}}
for these solutions and find conditions for it to be positive. Given that
\chi^{\text{(RWN)}}\equiv 0
, we will restrict the search to only those solutions that obey

(3.34)

\lim_{r\to\infty}\chi(r)=0,

so that the laws of electromagnetism away from the point charge approximate the ones observed in the real world, that is, the conventional Maxwell theory. In fact, our solutions
(\alpha,\beta,\chi)
will be such that the quantities
\alpha-\alpha^{(\mathrm{RWN})}=\alpha
,
\beta-\beta^{(\mathrm{RWN})}
and
\chi-\chi^{(\mathrm{RWN})}=\chi
decay exponentially for large
r
. There are also other necessary properties of
\chi
that can be deduced now from the desired behavior of our solution; we state them in a Lemma:

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
(\alpha,\beta,\chi)
solve
(
3.30
)
for
0<\varkappa<\infty
and obey
\beta(r)>0
for all
r>0
,
\chi(\infty)=0
and
\left\lvert\varphi(0)\right\rvert<\infty
, where
\varphi\in C\big([0,\infty)\big)
is related to
\chi
as in
(
3.28
)
. Then

(3.35)

\chi(0)=1

and

(3.36)

0<\chi(r)<1,\quad\chi^{\prime}(r)<0\quad\text{for all }r>0.

Proof.

Since

\varphi(r)-\varphi(0)=\int_{0}^{r}\varphi^{\prime}(s)\ \mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{r}\frac{\chi(s)-1}{s^{2}}\ \mathrm{d}s,

the condition
\left\lvert\varphi(0)\right\rvert<\infty
already gives (
3.35
) — in fact, we see that
\chi(s)-1
needs to decay faster than
s
as
s\to 0^{+}
in order for this integral to be finite. Now define
\overline{\chi}:=e^{\alpha}\chi^{\prime}/(r^{2}\beta)
, so that the
\chi
equation in (
3.30
) can be interpreted as a first-order system for the unknowns
\chi
and
\overline{\chi}
:

\chi^{\prime}=\frac{r^{2}\beta}{e^{\alpha}}\overline{\chi},\quad\overline{\chi}^{\prime}=\varkappa^{2}\frac{e^{\alpha}}{r^{2}}\chi.

Considering that
\beta>0
, these equations imply that, at each
r>0
, we have
\chi(r)>0
if and only if
\overline{\chi}^{\prime}(r)>0
, and the same also holds when exchanging the roles of
\chi
and
\overline{\chi}
. This allows us to rule out certain scenarios involving the mutual signs of
\chi
and
\overline{\chi}
:

•

If
\chi(r_{0})>0
and
\overline{\chi}(r_{0})>0
at some
r_{0}
, then both
\chi
and
\overline{\chi}
would be increasing at
[r_{0},\infty)
(potentially blowing up before
r=\infty
), contradicting
\chi(\infty)=0
. If
\chi(r_{0})<0
and
\overline{\chi}(r_{0})<0
at some
r_{0}
, the same contradiction would arise, only with decreasing behavior.

•

If either
\chi
or
\overline{\chi}
(but not both) vanished at some
r_{0}
, we would fall into the previous case at any point
r_{1}>r_{0}
, ultimately also leading to a contradiction.

•

If
\chi(r_{0})=\overline{\chi}(r_{0})=0
at some
r_{0}
, the existence-and-uniqueness theorem would show that
\chi
must be null everywhere, contradicting
\chi(0)=1
.

Therefore,
\chi(r)
and
\overline{\chi}(r)
must have opposite, nonzero signs at all
r>0
, and, taking into account that
\chi(0)=1>0
and
\mathrm{sgn}(\chi^{\prime})=\mathrm{sgn}(\overline{\chi})
, we have the proof that
\chi>0
and
\chi^{\prime}<0
everywhere in
(0,\infty)
. Since
\chi(0)=1
and
\chi(\infty)=0
, with
\chi
a strictly decreasing function, it follows as well that
0<\chi<1
everywhere in
(0,\infty)
.
∎

4.
GR solutions for a static point charge

This essentially self-contained section includes the statement and proof of our main result, Theorem
4.1
.

4.1.
Statement of the existence theorem

Before anything, we must define a special function that will appear in our theorem. As is well-known, given parameters
a,b\in\mathbb{C}
, the corresponding
confluent hypergeometric function of the second kind
, also known as
Tricomi’s function
and denoted by
U=U(a,b;\ast):\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, is by definition the only solution
w=w(z)
of
Kummer’s equation

(4.1)

zw^{\prime\prime}+(b-z)w^{\prime}-aw=0

satisfying the following condition:

(4.2)

\lim_{\real z\to\infty}z^{a}w(z)=1.

Depending on the values of the parameters
a
and
b
, it may be a multi-valued function whose principal branch is normally taken to be that where
z=re^{i\theta}
for
-\pi<\theta<\pi
. For our work, we will only need to consider this function for a real argument
z>0
and real parameters
a,b
, in which case
U(a,b;z)
is well-defined and real-valued. Also well-known is the fact that
U
has an integral representation in a special case:

(4.3)

U(a,b;z)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(a)}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-tz}t^{a-1}(1+t)^{b-a-1}\ \mathrm{d}t\quad\text{for }\real a>0,\ \real z>0,

where
\Gamma
denotes the usual Gamma function. This formula is easily proved via the Laplace Transform of Kummer’s equation (
4.1
). See
[
1
]
for details. We now make the following definition:

Definition 4.1
.

For all reals
\mathsf{h}\geq 0
, we denote by
U_{\mathsf{h}}
the function

(4.4)

U_{\mathsf{h}}:(0,\infty)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R},\quad U_{\mathsf{h}}(s):=U(1-\mathsf{h},2,s).

One can promptly check that
U_{0}(s)=1/s
,
U_{1}(s)=1
,
U_{2}(s)=s-2
and that, for
\mathsf{h}\in\mathbb{N}
, the function
U_{\mathsf{h}}
is a polynomial of degree
\mathsf{h}-1
. However, for most
\mathsf{h}\notin\mathbb{N}
, it cannot be written in terms of elementary functions.

The following properties of
U_{\mathsf{h}}
follow directly from (
4.2
) and (
4.3
):

(4.5)

\lim_{s\to\infty}s^{1-\mathsf{h}}U_{\mathsf{h}}(s)=1\quad\text{for all }\mathsf{h}\geq 0,\text{ and}

(4.6)

\Gamma(1-\mathsf{h})\,U_{\mathsf{h}}(s)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-ts}t^{-\mathsf{h}}(1+t)^{\mathsf{h}}\ \mathrm{d}t\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ts\longmapsto t}}{{=}}\frac{1}{s}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}t^{-\mathsf{h}}(t+s)^{\mathsf{h}}\ \mathrm{d}t\quad\text{for all }s>0,\ 0\leq\mathsf{h}<1.

For our work in this section, we will also need the fact that
U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})>0
for all
\mathsf{h}>0
. For this we offer a “proof by plotting” — see Figure
1
. From this fact, we will be able to prove later that in fact
U_{\mathsf{h}}(s)>0
for all
s\geq 4\mathsf{h}
.

(a)

U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})
plotted for
0.05<\mathsf{h}<2.00
. If this were plotted from
\mathsf{h}=0
, a blow-up towards
\infty
would be present around
\mathsf{h}=0
, because
U_{0}(s)=1/s
.

(b)

U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})
plotted for
0.05<\mathsf{h}<10
, showing that the expression grows unboundedly with
\mathsf{h}
after attaining its positive minimum between
\mathsf{h}=0.5
and
\mathsf{h}=1
.

Figure 1
.
U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})
is strictly positive for all
\mathsf{h}>0
.

Remark 4.1
.

Our solutions to the E-M-BLTP system will depend on a constant
\mathsf{h}\geq 0
, related to
\left\lvert M_{\text{bare}}\right\rvert
, that is fixed
a priori
. Formula (
4.6
) will then enable us to produce
quantitative
estimates for the metric coefficients and electric potential when
0\leq\mathsf{h}<1
, whereas if
\mathsf{h}\geq 1
our statements will only be
qualitative
as a consequence of (
4.5
). More-concrete statements for all values of
\mathsf{h}
can likely be obtained by making use of other representation formulas for Tricomi’s function, but we will leave this to a future paper if we ever find a need for such statements. For now, we are content with having a better grasp on our spacetimes only for
0\leq\mathsf{h}<1
, since, as we shall see, the physically meaningful values of
\mathsf{h}
for the spacetime of a point charge are within this range — in fact between 0 and about
10^{-18}
.

We can now state our result:

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
G,c,\varkappa
be Newton’s gravitational constant, the speed of light, and the BLTP constant of dimension
\emph{\texttt{[Length]}}^{-1}
, respectively. Also fix a finite constant
M_{\mathrm{bare}}\leq 0
with dimension
[Mass]
. Then there exists a threshold
\mathsf{g}_{0}>0
such that, if a constant
Q\neq 0
of dimension
[Charge]
is such that

(4.7)

\mathsf{g}:=\frac{GQ^{2}\varkappa^{2}}{c^{4}}<\mathsf{g}_{0},

then a solution to the Einstein-Maxwell-BLTP system of a point particle of charge
Q
exists with a naked singularity having bare mass
M_{\mathrm{bare}}
and with finite values for both its total field energy and its electric potential at the singularity. More precisely: A static, spherically symmetric, asymptotically flat spacetime
\mathcal{M}
exists equipped with:

•

a global coordinate system such that
\mathcal{M}=\big\{(ct,r,\theta,\phi):\,t\in\mathbb{R},\ r>0,\ \theta\in[0,\pi],\ \phi\in[0,2\pi)\big\}
;

•

a Lorentzian metric of the form
\mathrm{d}s^{2}=-\beta(r)^{-1}e^{2\alpha(r)}\ \mathrm{d}(ct)^{2}+\beta(r)\ \mathrm{d}r^{2}+r^{2}\big(\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta\ \mathrm{d}\phi^{2}\big)
, for coefficients
\alpha,\beta:(0,\infty)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying

(4.8)

\lim_{r\to\infty}\alpha(r)=0,\quad\beta(r)=1+O(r^{-1})\quad\text{as }r\to\infty

and

(4.9)

\beta(r)>0\quad\text{for all }r>0;

•

a current 1-form
J\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{1}(\overline{\mathcal{M}})
, defined on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}=\big\{(ct,r,\theta,\phi):\,t\in\mathbb{R},\ r\geq 0,\ \theta\in[0,\pi],\ \phi\in[0,2\pi)\big\}
and expressed in
(ct,r,\theta,\phi)
coordinates by

(4.10)

J=-Qc\,\delta_{\bm{0}}(r)\frac{e^{2\alpha}}{r^{2}\beta\sin\theta}\ \mathrm{d}(ct),

where the Dirac Delta
\delta_{\bm{0}}
is such that
\int_{B_{R}(0)}\delta_{\bm{0}}\ \mathrm{d}^{3}\bm{x}=1
for all balls
B_{R}(0)=\big\{(r,\theta,\phi):\,0\leq r<R,\ \theta\in[0,\pi],\ \phi\in[0,2\pi)\big\}
, with
\mathrm{d}^{3}\bm{x}=r^{2}\sin\theta\ \mathrm{d}r\wedge\mathrm{d}\theta\wedge\mathrm{d}\phi
;

•

a 2-form field
F\in\bigwedge\nolimits^{2}(\mathcal{M})
defined by
F=-e^{\alpha(r)}\varphi^{\prime}(r)\ \mathrm{d}(ct)\wedge\mathrm{d}r
for an electric potential
\varphi:(0,\infty)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying

(4.11)

\lim_{r\to\infty}\varphi(r)=\lim_{r\to\infty}\chi(r)=0,\quad\text{where }\chi(r)=\frac{1}{Q}r^{2}\varphi^{\prime}(r)+1;

•

a stress-energy tensor
(T_{\mu\nu})
defined in terms of
F
as in
(
3.3
)
;

such that the system of Einstein-Maxwell-BLTP equations is verified, namely

R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}Rg_{\mu\nu}=\frac{8\pi G}{c^{4}}T_{\mu\nu},\quad\mathrm{d}F=0,\quad\mathrm{d}M=\frac{4\pi}{c}{\star}J,\quad M={\star}F+\varkappa^{-2}{\star}\mathrm{d}\delta F,

and such that the following properties hold:

(4.12)

\frac{c^{2}}{G}\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{r}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{\beta(r)}\right)=M_{\mathrm{bare}},

(4.13)

0<\mathcal{E}:=\frac{Q^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{e^{\alpha}}{r^{2}}\left(1-\chi^{2}-\varkappa^{-2}\frac{(\chi^{\prime})^{2}}{\beta}\right)\ \mathrm{d}r<\infty,

(4.14)

0<\left\lvert\varphi(0)\right\rvert<\infty.

In addition, let constants
\mathsf{h}
,
M_{\mathrm{ADM}}
,
\mathring{\mathcal{E}}
and a function
\mathring{\varphi}
, of respective dimensions
[1]
,
[Mass]
,
[Energy]
and
[Charge]
, be defined as follows:

(4.15)

\mathsf{h}:=\frac{\varkappa G}{c^{2}}\left\lvert M_{\mathrm{bare}}\right\rvert,

(4.16)

M_{\mathrm{ADM}}:=\frac{c^{2}}{G}\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{r}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{\beta(r)}\right),

(4.17)

\mathring{\mathcal{E}}:=Q^{2}\varkappa\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{4\mathsf{h}}-\frac{U_{\mathsf{h}}^{\prime}(4\mathsf{h})}{U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})}\right)\text{ if }\mathsf{h}>0,\quad\mathring{\mathcal{E}}:=Q^{2}\varkappa\lim_{\mathsf{h}\to 0^{+}}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{4\mathsf{h}}-\frac{U_{\mathsf{h}}^{\prime}(4\mathsf{h})}{U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})}\right)=\frac{Q^{2}\varkappa}{2}\text{ if }\mathsf{h}=0,

(4.18)

\mathring{\mathcal{\varphi}}(r):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\dfrac{Q}{r}-\dfrac{Q\,U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h}+2\varkappa r)}{2\mathsf{h}\,U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})}\left(1-\dfrac{2GM_{\mathrm{bare}}}{c^{2}r}\right)e^{-\varkappa r}&\quad\text{if }\mathsf{h}>0,\\
\\
\dfrac{Q(1-e^{-\varkappa r})}{r}&\quad\text{if }\mathsf{h}=0.\end{array}\right.

Then the solution described above satisfies the following estimates at all
r>0
, for
\mathsf{h}
-dependent constants
C_{1}^{\prime},C_{j}
:

(4.19)

\left\lvert e^{\alpha(r)}-1\right\rvert\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{g}C_{1}\left(e+\dfrac{e}{2\varkappa r}\right)^{\delta}\ln\left(e+\dfrac{e}{2\varkappa r}\right)e^{-2\varkappa r}&\text{if }\mathsf{h}=0\text{ (where }0<\delta\ll 1\text{),}\\
\\
\mathsf{g}C_{1}\exp\left(\dfrac{C_{1}^{\prime}}{\mathsf{h}^{2}}\right)(4\mathsf{h}+2\varkappa r)^{-2}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2\varkappa r)^{2+2\mathsf{h}}e^{-2\varkappa r}&\text{if }\mathsf{h}>0,\end{array}\right.

(4.20)

\left\lvert\beta(r)-\beta^{\mathrm{(RWN)}}_{M_{\mathrm{ADM}}}(r)\right\rvert\leq\mathsf{g}C_{2}r(4\mathsf{h}+2\varkappa r)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2\varkappa r)^{2+2\mathsf{h}}e^{-2\varkappa r}\beta^{\mathrm{(RWN)}}_{M_{\mathrm{ADM}}}(r),

(4.21)

\left\lvert\beta(r)-\left(1-\frac{2GM_{\mathrm{bare}}}{c^{2}r}\right)^{-1}\right\rvert\leq\mathsf{g}C_{3}(\varkappa r)^{3}(4\mathsf{h}+2\varkappa r)^{-3}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2\varkappa r)^{-1},

(4.22)

\left\lvert\varphi(r)-\frac{Q}{r}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert Q\right\rvert C_{4}(\varkappa r)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2\varkappa r)^{\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-\varkappa r},

(4.23)

\left\lvert\varphi(r)-\mathring{\varphi}(r)\right\rvert\leq\mathsf{g}\left\lvert Q\right\rvert C_{5}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2\varkappa r)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-\varkappa r},

(4.24)

\left\lvert\varphi(0)-\frac{2\mathring{\mathcal{E}}}{Q\varkappa}\right\rvert\leq\mathsf{g}\left\lvert Q\right\rvert C_{6},

(4.25)

\left\lvert\mathcal{E}-\mathring{\mathcal{E}}\right\rvert\leq\mathsf{g}Q^{2}\varkappa C_{7},

(4.26)

\left\lvert M_{\mathrm{ADM}}-\big(M_{\mathrm{bare}}+c^{-2}\mathring{\mathcal{E}}\big)\right\rvert\leq\frac{\mathsf{g}Q^{2}\varkappa}{c^{2}}C_{8}.

Finally, if
0\leq\mathsf{h}<1
, the following values of the constants
\mathsf{g}_{0}
and
C_{1}^{\prime},C_{j}
may be chosen:

(4.27)

\begin{array}[]{|l||c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c}\hline\cr&\mathsf{g}_{0}&C_{1}&C_{1}^{\prime}&C_{2}&C_{3}&C_{4}&C_{5}&\cdots\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{h}=0&2\cdot 10^{-4}&1.57&\text{---}&13.9&389&2.14&1240&\cdots\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{h}\in(0,1)&1.17\cdot 10^{-17}&58.5&2.68\cdot 10^{-14}&1910&7.41\cdot 10^{6}&45&2.59\cdot 10^{16}&\cdots\\
\hline\cr\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{cc|c|c|}\hline\cr\cdots&C_{6}&C_{7}&C_{8}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\cdots&1240&623&620\\
\hline\cr\cdots&5.80\cdot 10^{16}&2.91\cdot 10^{16}&2.90\cdot 10^{16}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Before the proof, we make some comments on the significance of the theorem’s hypotheses and conclusions:

•

Approximate solution formulas.
Estimates (
4.19
), (
4.21
) and (
4.23
) provide expressions of
r
that approximate the true values of
e^{\alpha(r)}
,
\beta(r)
and
\varphi(r)
. The corresponding error terms are small due to the presence of
\mathsf{g}
in them, and they decay exponentially for large
r
in the case of (
4.19
) and (
4.23
). These error terms are also uniformly bounded over all
r\geq 0
, except for the one for
\left\lvert e^{\alpha}-1\right\rvert
when
\mathsf{h}=0
, which blows up at
r=0
at the slow rate of
r^{-\delta}\ln(e+e/r)
, reflecting the fact that
e^{\alpha}
diverges at
r=0
in the zero-bare-mass case.

•

Comparison with RWN.
Estimates (
4.19
), (
4.20
) and (
4.22
) show how
e^{\alpha(r)}
,
\beta(r)
and
\varphi(r)
compare to their RWN counterparts. As expected, the respective error terms decay exponentially for large
r
(where RWN is expected to accurately approximate the true solution), while not necessarily being small for small
r
(where RWN becomes singular, contrary to the true solution). Also note there is no
\mathsf{g}
in the error term for (
4.22
); this is not a typo.

•

Finiteness of EM constants and ADM mass.
Estimates (
4.24
), (
4.25
) and (
4.26
) provide good approximations for the values of the electric potential at
r=0
, the total field energy, and the ADM mass, also showing that they are finite.

•

Blow-up of
\bm{g_{tt}}
near the singularity.
Information about the first metric coefficient
g_{tt}(r)=-\beta(r)^{-1}e^{2\alpha(r)}
is immediately derivable from the estimates (
4.19
) and (
4.21
) on
e^{\alpha(r)}
and
\beta(r)
. Interestingly, and contrary to what happens to
g_{rr}(r)=\beta(r)
, we find that
g_{tt}(0)
is infinite no matter what the value of
\mathsf{h}
is: In the zero-bare-mass case
\mathsf{h}=0
, we have
\beta(0)\approx 1
but
e^{\alpha(0)}=\infty
, while in the negative-bare-mass case
\mathsf{h}>0
we have
e^{\alpha(0)}\approx 1
but
\beta(0)^{-1}=\infty
.

•

Formula for
\bm{J}
.
Equation (
4.10
) expresses the general-relativistic form of the 4-current
J
corresponding to a static point charge. An explanation for why it looks like this can be found in the appendix, inside of the proof of Proposition
4.8
, which is the only place where we truly need it.

•

Physical
\bm{\mathsf{g}}
is small.
Let us assume that the typical value of
\left\lvert Q\right\rvert
for a charged atomic particle is between one and ten times the elementary charge:

1.52\cdot 10^{-4}\ \mathrm{kg}^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\mathrm{m}^{\frac{3}{2}}\cdot\mathrm{s}^{-1}<\left\lvert Q\right\rvert<1.52\cdot 10^{-3}\ \mathrm{kg}^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\mathrm{m}^{\frac{3}{2}}\cdot\mathrm{s}^{-1}

Then, using our crude postulated bounds (
2.20
) and (
2.21
) for
\varkappa
as well as the known values

G=6.67\cdot 10^{-11}\ \mathrm{kg}^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{m}^{3}\cdot\mathrm{s}^{-2},\quad c=3.00\cdot 10^{8}\ \mathrm{m}\cdot\mathrm{s}^{-1},

correct to three significant digits, we calculate that the physical value of
\mathsf{g}=GQ^{2}\varkappa^{2}/c^{4}
is indeed very small and falls within the size of the threshold
\mathsf{g}_{0}
that is given in the theorem for the case
0\leq\mathsf{h}<1
:

(4.28)

1.90\cdot 10^{-30}<\mathsf{g}<1.90\cdot 10^{-18}.

•

Physical
\bm{\mathsf{h}}
is small if
\bm{M_{\mathrm{ADM}}>0}
.
Let us analyze the constant
\mathring{\mathcal{E}}
defined in (
4.17
). Conventional mathematical software often includes the capability to compute Tricomi’s function
U(a,b;z)
— for example, in MATLAB it is implemented under the name
KummerU
. In particular, the expression

\frac{\mathring{\mathcal{E}}}{Q^{2}\varkappa}=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{4\mathsf{h}}-\frac{U_{\mathsf{h}}^{\prime}(4\mathsf{h})}{U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})},\quad\mathsf{h}>0,

can be plotted without much effort — we note the general property
(\mathrm{d}/\mathrm{d}z)U(a,b;z)=-a\ U(a+1,b+1;z)
of Tricomi’s function, which implies that also the derivative term
U_{\mathsf{h}}^{\prime}(4\mathsf{h})=(\mathsf{h}-1)\,U(2-\mathsf{h},3,4\mathsf{h})
can be easily managed by a computer. The plots in Figure
2
reveal that
\mathring{\mathcal{E}}\leq Q^{2}\varkappa/2
for all
\mathsf{h}\geq 0
.

(a)

\mathring{\mathcal{E}}/(Q^{2}\varkappa)
plotted for
0\leq\mathsf{h}\leq 2
. The exact values
1/2
,
1/4
and
5/24
at
\mathsf{h}=0
,
1
and
2
, respectively, are explicitly computable from the formulas
U_{0}(s)=1/s
,
U_{1}(s)=1
and
U_{2}(s)=s-2
.

(b)

\mathring{\mathcal{E}}/(Q^{2}\varkappa)
plotted for
0\leq\mathsf{h}\leq 100
, showing that the expression continues to decrease as
\mathsf{h}
grows.

Figure 2
.
\mathring{\mathcal{E}}/(Q^{2}\varkappa)
stays below
1/2
for all
\mathsf{h}>0
.

As a consequence of this and of estimate (
4.26
), we find

c^{2}M_{\mathrm{ADM}}\leq c^{2}M_{\mathrm{bare}}+\mathring{\mathcal{E}}+\mathsf{g}Q^{2}\varkappa C_{8}\leq c^{2}M_{\mathrm{bare}}+\frac{Q^{2}\varkappa}{2}+\mathsf{g}Q^{2}\varkappa C_{8}.

If we wish for our spacetime to have a positive ADM mass, it is therefore necessary that the bare mass satisfy

(4.29)

M_{\mathrm{bare}}>-\frac{Q^{2}\varkappa}{c^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{g}C_{8}\right)\approx-\frac{Q^{2}\varkappa}{2c^{2}},

where we used the fact that
\mathsf{g}C_{8}
is small compared to
1/2
(we will see in our proof that
\mathsf{g}_{0}\ll C_{j}
for each
j=1,\ldots,8
). Using the definitions (
4.7
) and (
4.15
) of
\mathsf{g}
and
\mathsf{h}
, inequality (
4.29
) turns into

(4.30)

\mathsf{h}\leq\mathsf{g}\left(\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{g}C_{8}\right)\approx\frac{\mathsf{g}}{2}.

Hence the physically relevant values of
\mathsf{h}
are indeed small, of the order of at most
10^{-18}
according to (
4.28
).

On the other hand, the constants
\mathsf{g}_{0}
and
C_{j}
given in the second line of table (
4.27
) are valid not only for
0\leq\mathsf{h}\leq 10^{-18}
, but also in the whole range
0<\mathsf{h}\leq 1
. This renders them far from optimal when
\mathsf{h}
is as small as
10^{-18}
. At this point we have to mention that our upcoming proofs could easily be adapted to find better values for these constants for small, positive
\mathsf{h}
(they would come out much closer to the ones on the first line of the table, corresponding to the
\mathsf{h}=0
case), but we opted to leave out this improvement, lest we obscure the arguments with too many calculations.

•

Physically relevant range for
\bm{|M_{\mathrm{bare}}|}
.
Let us consider typical values for the mass of an atomic particle, represented in our spacetime by the ADM mass (
4.16
):

(4.31)

10^{-30}\ \mathrm{kg}<M_{\mathrm{ADM}}<10^{-25}\ \mathrm{kg}.

Since this assigns a positive value for
M_{\mathrm{ADM}}
, the upper bound (
4.30
) holds, which makes
\mathsf{h}
very small. The first plot in Figure
4.17
then shows that
\mathring{\mathcal{E}}\approx Q^{2}\varkappa/2
. We plug this into estimate (
4.26
) and find

(4.32)

\left\lvert\frac{c^{2}M_{\mathrm{bare}}}{Q^{2}\varkappa}-\left(\frac{c^{2}M_{\mathrm{ADM}}}{Q^{2}\varkappa}-\frac{1}{2}\right)\right\rvert\approx\left\lvert\frac{c^{2}M_{\mathrm{bare}}}{Q^{2}\varkappa}-\left(\frac{c^{2}M_{\mathrm{ADM}}}{Q^{2}\varkappa}-\frac{\mathring{\mathcal{E}}}{Q^{2}\varkappa}\right)\right\rvert\leq\mathsf{g}C_{8}<0.0551,

where we used the physical upper bound (
4.28
) on
\mathsf{g}
and the value of
C_{8}
from (
4.27
). Moreover, with (
4.31
) and our values for the constants
Q,c,\varkappa
, one checks that
c^{2}M_{\mathrm{ADM}}/(Q^{2}\varkappa)<3.90\cdot 10^{-12}
is negligible in comparison to
1/2
and can practically be left out of (
4.32
). This gives us an approximate range

-0.556<\frac{c^{2}M_{\mathrm{bare}}}{Q^{2}\varkappa}<-0.444.

By then applying to this our known values/ranges for the physical constants, we finally arrive at

(4.33)

1.13\cdot 10^{-14}\ \mathrm{kg}<\left\lvert M_{\mathrm{bare}}\right\rvert<1.43\cdot 10^{-7}\ \mathrm{kg}.

Hence the physically meaningful values for
\left\lvert M_{\mathrm{bare}}\right\rvert
in the E-M-BLTP spacetimes are seen to be several orders of magnitude larger than the particle’s inertial mass
M_{\mathrm{ADM}}
.

The rest of this section is dedicated to proving Theorem
4.1
. We begin with some simplifications followed by auxiliary results, some with entire subsections dedicated to them. The proof is then concluded in Subsection
4.5
. The core of the argument will be to treat the problem as a perturbation of the
G=0
equations, which is a valid strategy because, for a point charge, gravity can be said to be much weaker than electromagnetic effects — a fact expressed by the smallness of the dimensionless constant
\mathsf{g}
.

To begin simplifying the E-M-BLTP system, we point out that the unknown
\alpha
does not appear on the right side of the
\alpha
and
\beta
equations in (
3.30
). To add to this, if we expand the derivative on the left side of the
\chi
equation, cancel the factor
e^{\alpha}
from both sides and use the
\alpha
and
\beta
equations to simplify everything, the result is also an equation from which
\alpha
is absent:

(4.34)

\frac{1}{\varkappa^{-2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\left(\frac{\chi^{\prime}}{r^{2}\beta}\right)=\frac{GQ^{2}}{c^{4}\varkappa^{4}}\frac{(\chi^{\prime})^{3}}{r^{5}\beta}+\frac{\chi}{r^{2}}.

Hence
\beta
and
\chi
can be solved for independently from
\alpha
, and the
\alpha
equation can subsequently be integrated once
\chi
is known. We may therefore focus our initial attention only on the unknowns
\beta
and
\chi
. Expanding the derivative on the left side of (
4.34
) with help of the
\beta
equation and then simplifying the result, we find the following second-order equation for
\chi
:

(4.35)

\chi^{\prime\prime}=\frac{3-\beta}{r}\chi^{\prime}+\varkappa^{2}\beta\chi+\frac{GQ^{2}}{c^{4}}\frac{\beta(1-\chi^{2})\chi^{\prime}}{r^{3}}.

At this point, like in many other situations involving spherical symmetry in GR, it is better to start considering in place of
\beta
the spacetime’s
mass function

\mu
— except that we will actually consider a different, closely related function
\nu
. Both of these quantities are defined next:

Definition 4.2
.

The spacetime’s
mass function
in the coordinates
(ct,r,\theta,\phi)
is defined by

(4.36)

\mu(r):=\frac{c^{2}r}{2G}\left(1-\frac{1}{\beta(r)}\right)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\beta(r)=\left(1-\frac{2G\,\mu(r)}{c^{2}r}\right)^{-1}=\frac{c^{2}r}{c^{2}r-2G\,\mu(r)}.

It has physical significance: An observer who interprets the coordinate
r
as measuring the distance from the singularity will find that
\mu(r)
measures the amount of mass contained inside the sphere of radius
r
, at least in the Newtonian regime where
G\mu(r)\ll c^{2}r
(see
[
12
]
). Building on this, we define the
re-scaled mass function
by absorbing the factor of
G
in (
4.36
) into it:

(4.37)

\nu(r):=G\,\mu(r)=\frac{c^{2}r}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{\beta(r)}\right)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\beta(r)=\left(1-\frac{2\nu(r)}{c^{2}r}\right)^{-1}=\frac{c^{2}r}{c^{2}r-2\nu(r)}.

Note the dimensions

\dim(\mu)=\texttt{[Mass]}=\texttt{M},\quad\dim(\nu)=\texttt{[Mass]}\cdot\dim(G)=\texttt{L}^{3}\texttt{T}^{-2}.

Remark 4.2
.

One reason to consider
\nu
instead of
\mu
in our calculations has to do with our proof method: We will later decompose
\nu
in the form
\mathring{\nu}+G\xi
, where
\mathring{\nu}
solves the first equation of the E-M-BLTP system for
G=0
, while
\xi
is a new unknown such that
G
is supposed to be small in comparison to
\lVert\xi\rVert
(for a norm that will be defined in our proof). If we instead applied the same idea to
\mu
, that is, if we considered the decomposition
\mu=\mathring{\mu}+G\rho
for a new unknown
\rho
, we would find that
\rho=\xi/G
is too large for the proof to work. Another way of thinking about this is that, by writing our equations in terms of
\nu=G\mu
instead of
\mu
, we ensure that our proof method is a perturbation only with respect to the parameter
G
that appears in Einstein’s equations
G_{\mu\nu}=(8\pi G/c^{4})T_{\mu\nu}
, and not with respect to the
G
that appears in the definition (
4.36
) of the mass function.

The differential equations (
3.30
), (
4.35
) for
\beta
and
\chi
, when rewritten in terms of
\nu
and
\chi
, are checked to become

(4.38)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\nu^{\prime}=\dfrac{GQ^{2}}{c^{2}}\left(\dfrac{(1+\chi)(1-\chi)}{2r^{2}}-\varkappa^{-2}\dfrac{(c^{2}r-2\nu)(\chi^{\prime})^{2}}{2c^{2}r^{3}}\right),\\
\\
\chi^{\prime\prime}=\dfrac{2(c^{2}r-3\nu)}{r(c^{2}r-2\nu)}\chi^{\prime}+\dfrac{\varkappa^{2}c^{2}r}{c^{2}r-2\nu}\chi+\dfrac{GQ^{2}}{c^{2}}\dfrac{(1+\chi)(1-\chi)\chi^{\prime}}{r^{2}(c^{2}r-2\nu)},\end{array}\right.

while the definitions (
3.25
) and (
3.24
) of the bare and ADM masses give

(4.39)

\nu(0)=GM_{\mathrm{bare}},\quad\nu(\infty)=GM_{\mathrm{ADM}}.

Next, since there are three independent parameters that will remain constant in this proof, namely
Q,c,\varkappa
, they can be used to define characteristic scales of mass, length and time (and therefore also of any other dimension) for our problem:

\texttt{[Mass]}=\mathrm{dim}(Q^{2}c^{-2}\varkappa),\quad\texttt{[Length]}=\mathrm{dim}(\varkappa^{-1}),\quad\texttt{[Time]}=\mathrm{dim}(c^{-1}\varkappa^{-1}).

(The only reason why we waited so long to finally mention this simplification is that there is no
\varkappa
in the conventional Maxwell theory, which we also wished to expand upon for comparison with BLTP theory). Using these scales, all variables and constants in the theorem can be made dimensionless: We define
\widetilde{r}=\varkappa r
as well as
\widetilde{f}(\widetilde{r})=f(\varkappa^{-1}\widetilde{r})
for each of the already dimensionless functions
f=\alpha,\beta,\chi
. Similarly, we set
\widetilde{\varphi}(\widetilde{r})=Q^{-1}\varphi(\varkappa^{-1}\widetilde{r})
, as well as
\widetilde{\mu}(\widetilde{r})=Q^{-2}c^{2}\varkappa^{-1}\mu(\varkappa^{-1}\widetilde{r})
and analogously for
\widetilde{\nu}
. Then the equations obeyed by
\widetilde{\alpha}(\widetilde{r})
etc. are the same as those for the original functions, except that the constants
Q,c,\varkappa
get replaced by 1 and the constant
G
gets replaced by its corresponding re-scaled parameter
\widetilde{G}=\mathsf{g}
— see (
4.7
). To recover the original objects from the dimensionless ones, they simply need to be multiplied by the only combination of
Q,c,\varkappa
that produces their correct dimension; for example, for the field energy
\mathcal{E}
, measured in units of
\texttt{M}\texttt{L}^{2}\texttt{T}^{-2}
, the following relation holds:

\mathcal{E}=\texttt{[Mass]}\texttt{[Length]}^{2}\texttt{[Time]}^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}=(Q^{2}c^{-2}\varkappa)(\varkappa^{-1})^{2}(c^{-1}\varkappa^{-1})^{-2}\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}=Q^{2}\varkappa\,\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}.

We stop writing the tildes from now on, but they are implied above all constants, functions and their arguments in everything that follows. The claims of the theorem will only be proved for these dimensionless variables, and the reader is tasked with recovering the original version of these claims by applying the procedure just explained. We also note that definition (
4.15
) of the constant
\mathsf{h}
is now written as

(4.40)

\mathsf{h}=\mathsf{g}\left\lvert M_{\mathrm{bare}}\right\rvert=-\mathsf{g}M_{\mathrm{bare}}=-\nu(0),

where we used (
4.39
). Putting together the differential equations (
4.38
) and boundary conditions (
4.40
), (
3.35
) and (
3.34
) for the re-scaled mass function
\nu
and the relative-deviation scalar
\chi
, we arrive at the following boundary-value problem, which constitutes the core of the whole proof:

Definition 4.3
.

The
mass/deviation problem of parameters

\bm{(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{h})}
, where
\mathsf{g}>0
and
\mathsf{h}\geq 0
are given
a priori
, consists of finding a solution
(\nu,\chi)\in C^{1}\big((0,\infty)\big)\times C^{2}\big((0,\infty)\big)
to the equations

(4.41)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\nu^{\prime}=\mathsf{g}\left(\dfrac{(1+\chi)(1-\chi)}{2r^{2}}-\dfrac{(r-2\nu)(\chi^{\prime})^{2}}{2r^{3}}\right),\\
\\
\chi^{\prime\prime}=\dfrac{2(r-3\nu)}{r(r-2\nu)}\chi^{\prime}+\dfrac{r}{r-2\nu}\chi+\dfrac{\mathsf{g}(1+\chi)(1-\chi)\chi^{\prime}}{r^{2}(r-2\nu)}\end{array}\right.

such that

(4.42)

\nu(0)=-\mathsf{h},\quad\chi(0)=1,\quad\chi(\infty)=0.

We now dedicate three subsections to solving this problem. The rest of the claims in the theorem will follow easily afterwards. We will be able to prove that, for all
\mathsf{h}\geq 0
, a solution exists as long as
\mathsf{g}
is suitably small. The idea will be to look for solutions in the form
\nu=\mathring{\nu}+\mathsf{g}\xi
and
\chi=\mathring{\chi}+\mathsf{g}\psi
, with
(\mathring{\nu},\mathring{\chi})
being a solution to the problem with parameter
\mathsf{g}=0
, and with
(\xi,\psi)
being a new pair of unknowns.

4.2.
Integral lemmas

Here we collect three auxiliary results on the asymptotic behavior of certain integrals.

Lemma 4.2
.

For all
0\leq\mathsf{h}<1
, we have

(4.43)

\frac{0.232}{(1-\mathsf{h})\,\Gamma(1-\mathsf{h})}s^{-1}(1+s)^{\mathsf{h}}\leq U_{\mathsf{h}}(s)\leq\frac{1.37}{(1-\mathsf{h})\,\Gamma(1-\mathsf{h})}s^{-1}(1+s)^{\mathsf{h}}\quad\text{for all }s\geq 4\mathsf{h},

where
U_{\mathsf{h}}
is the function defined in
(
4.4
)
.

Proof.

Given the integral representation (
4.6
) for
U_{\mathsf{h}}
, valid precisely for
0\leq\mathsf{h}<1
, we will be done if we can bracket the expression

(4.44)

\Gamma(1-\mathsf{h})\,U_{\mathsf{h}}(s)\,s(1+s)^{-\mathsf{h}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-t}t^{-\mathsf{h}}\left(\frac{t+s}{1+s}\right)^{\mathsf{h}}\ \mathrm{d}t

between multiples of
(1-\mathsf{h})^{-1}
. Using the fact that
t+s\leq 1+s
if
t\leq 1
, and
t+s\leq t+ts
if
t\geq 1
, we find the upper bound

\Gamma(1-\mathsf{h})\,U_{\mathsf{h}}(s)\,s(1+s)^{-\mathsf{h}}\leq\int_{0}^{1}e^{-t}t^{-\mathsf{h}}\ \mathrm{d}t+\int_{1}^{\infty}e^{-t}t^{-\mathsf{h}}t^{\mathsf{h}}\ \mathrm{d}t\\
<\int_{0}^{1}t^{-\mathsf{h}}\ \mathrm{d}t+\int_{1}^{\infty}e^{-t}\ \mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{1-\mathsf{h}}+e^{-1}<\frac{1.37}{1-\mathsf{h}}.

To find a lower bound, we ignore values
t>1
and use the fact that
(t+s)/(1+s)
is increasing in
s
for fixed
t\leq 1
. Thus, considering that
s\geq 4\mathsf{h}
and
\mathsf{h}<1
, we have

(4.45)

\frac{t+s}{1+s}\geq\frac{t+4\mathsf{h}}{1+4\mathsf{h}}\geq\frac{4\mathsf{h}}{1+4\mathsf{h}}>\frac{4\mathsf{h}}{5},

which gives

\Gamma(1-\mathsf{h})\,U_{\mathsf{h}}(s)\,s(1+s)^{-\mathsf{h}}\geq\left(\min_{0\leq\mathsf{h}<1}\left(\frac{4\mathsf{h}}{5}\right)^{\mathsf{h}}\right)\int_{0}^{1}e^{-t}t^{-\mathsf{h}}\ \mathrm{d}t>e^{-5/(4e)}e^{-1}\int_{0}^{1}t^{-\mathsf{h}}\ \mathrm{d}t>\frac{0.232}{1-\mathsf{h}}.\qed

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
c,d,k\in\mathbb{R}
with
c\neq-1
. Let
f:(0,\infty)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a continuous, strictly positive function such that

(4.46)

\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}r^{-c}f(r)\in(0,\infty),\quad\lim_{r\to\infty}r^{-d}f(r)\in(0,\infty),

and consider the following integrals for
r>0
:

\displaystyle I_{0}(r)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{r}f(s)e^{ks}\ \mathrm{d}s,\quad\ \text{well-defined when }c>-1,

\displaystyle I_{\infty}(r)

\displaystyle:=\int_{r}^{\infty}f(s)e^{ks}\ \mathrm{d}s,\quad\text{well-defined when }(k<0)\text{ or }(k=0,\,d<-1).

Then the following description of the behavior of
I_{0}(r)
and
I_{\infty}(r)
holds (see Definition
1.1
for the symbol
\simeq
):

\begin{array}[]{lll}\text{(a) If }c>-1,\,k<0,&\text{then}&\ I_{0}(r)\simeq r^{1+c}(1+r)^{-1-c}.\\
\text{(b) If }c>-1,\,k=0,\,d\neq-1,&\text{then}&\ I_{0}(r)\simeq r^{1+c}(1+r)^{-c+d}.\\
\text{(c) If }c>-1,\,k>0,&\text{then}&\ I_{0}(r)\simeq r^{1+c}(1+r)^{-1-c+d}e^{kr}.\\
\text{(d) If }c<-1,\,k<0,&\text{then}&I_{\infty}(r)\simeq r^{1+c}(1+r)^{-1-c+d}e^{kr}.\\
\text{(e) If }c<-1,\,k=0,\,d<-1,&\text{then}&I_{\infty}(r)\simeq r^{1+c}(1+r)^{-c+d}.\\
\text{(f) If }c>-1,\,k<0,&\text{then}&I_{\infty}(r)\simeq(1+r)^{d}e^{kr}.\\
\text{(g) If }c>-1,\,k=0,\,d<-1,&\text{then}&I_{\infty}(r)\simeq(1+r)^{1+d}.\\
\end{array}

Proof.

Condition (
4.46
) quickly implies
f(r)\simeq r^{c}(1+r)^{-c+d}
. Therefore, instead of
I_{0}
and
I_{\infty}
, it is enough to prove the lemma for the integrals

J_{0}(r):=\int_{0}^{r}s^{c}(1+s)^{-c+d}e^{ks}\ \mathrm{d}s,\quad J_{\infty}(r):=\int_{r}^{\infty}s^{c}(1+s)^{-c+d}e^{ks}\ \mathrm{d}s.

Calling
g_{1}(r),\ldots,g_{7}(r)
the functions of
r
that appear on the right side of the
\simeq
symbol in items (a)–(g) of the lemma, we can prove each of the claims by showing that the limits
\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}
and
\lim_{r\to\infty}
of the ratios
J_{0}/g_{m}
,
m=1,2,3
, and
J_{\infty}/g_{m}
,
m=4,5,6,7
, are finite and nonzero. In each case, these limits can be found either directly or with help of the L’Hôpital Rule when applicable. We only show items (a) and (d) as examples, the others being completely analogous:

(a)

Since the integrand
r^{c}(1+r)^{-c+d}e^{kr}
is integrable near
\infty
due to
k<0
, the limit
J_{0}(\infty)
is some positive number, just like
g_{1}(\infty)
for the function
g_{1}(r)=r^{1+c}(1+r)^{-1-c}
. Thus
J_{0}(\infty)/g_{1}(\infty)\in(0,\infty)
. As for the
\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}
limit, the L’Hôpital Rule is applicable due to both
J_{0}(0)
and
g_{1}(0)
being null (note that
r^{1+c}
vanishes at
r=0
), and it gives

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{J_{0}(r)}{g_{1}(r)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{J_{0}^{\prime}(r)}{g_{1}^{\prime}(r)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{r^{c}(1+r)^{-c+d}e^{kr}}{r^{1+c}(1+r)^{-1-c}\big((1+c)r^{-1}+(-1-c)(1+r)^{-1}\big)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{(1+r)^{d}e^{kr}}{(1+c)(1+r)^{-1}\big(1-r(1+r)^{-1}\big)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+c}\in(0,\infty).

(d)

In this case, with
g_{4}(r)=r^{1+c}(1+r)^{-1-c+d}e^{kr}
, we have
g_{4}(0)=J_{\infty}(0)=\infty
and
g_{4}(\infty)=J_{\infty}(\infty)=0
. Hence L’Hôpital is applicable to find
\lim_{r\to a}
at both extremes
a=0^{+}
and
a=\infty
:

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to a}\frac{J_{\infty}(r)}{g_{4}(r)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to a}\frac{J_{\infty}^{\prime}(r)}{g_{4}^{\prime}(r)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to a}\frac{-r^{c}(1+r)^{-c+d}e^{kr}}{r^{1+c}(1+r)^{-1-c+d}e^{kr}\big((1+c)r^{-1}+(-1-c+d)(1+r)^{-1}+k\big)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to a}\frac{-(1+r)}{(1+c)+(-1-c+d)r(1+r)^{-1}+kr},

which equals
-1/(1+c)\in(0,\infty)
at
a=0^{+}
and
-1/k\in(0,\infty)
at
a=\infty
. ∎

The next lemma provides bounds for 14 different integrals that will show up at various points of our proofs.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\mathsf{h}\geq 0
and
0<\varepsilon\leq 1/2
be real constants. Define, for
r>0
,

p(r)=4\mathsf{h}+2r,\quad q(r)=1+4\mathsf{h}+2r.

Then there exist constants
K_{1},\ldots,K_{14}>0
, possibly depending on
\mathsf{h}
and/or
\varepsilon
, such that for all
r>0

(4.47)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r}s^{2}p(s)^{-1}q(s)^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq K_{1}\ r^{3}p(r)^{-1}q(r)^{-2},

(4.48)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r}s^{2}p(s)^{-2}q(s)^{2\mathsf{h}+2\varepsilon}e^{-2s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq K_{2}\ r^{3}p(r)^{-2}q(r)^{-1},

(4.49)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r}sp(s)^{-1}q(s)^{-2}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq K_{3}\ r^{2}p(r)^{-1}q(r)^{-1},

(4.50)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r}s^{4}p(s)^{-3}q(s)^{-2+\varepsilon}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq K_{4}\ r^{5}p(r)^{-3}q(r)^{-2+\varepsilon},

(4.51)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r}s^{5}p(s)^{-4}q(s)^{-2+\varepsilon}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq K_{5}\ r^{6}p(r)^{-4}q(r)^{-2+\varepsilon},

(4.52)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r}s^{3}p(s)^{-1}q(s)^{-2-2\mathsf{h}}e^{2s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq K_{6}\ r^{4}p(r)^{-1}q(r)^{-3-2\mathsf{h}}e^{2r},

(4.53)

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{\infty}s^{-2}q(s)^{\frac{3}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq K_{7}\ r^{-1}q(r)^{\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r},

(4.54)

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{\infty}s^{-2}q(s)^{3+2\mathsf{h}}e^{-2s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq K_{8}\ r^{-1}q(r)^{2+2\mathsf{h}}e^{-2r},

(4.55)

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{\infty}p(s)^{-2}q(s)^{1+2\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-2s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq K_{9}\ p(r)^{-1}q(r)^{2\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-2r},

(4.56)

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{\infty}sp(s)^{-3}q(s)^{1+2\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-2s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq K_{10}\ p(r)^{-1}q(r)^{2\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-2r},

(4.57)

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{\infty}s^{2}p(s)^{-2}q(s)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq K_{11}\ q(r)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r},

(4.58)

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{\infty}sp(s)^{-2}q(s)^{1+2\mathsf{h}}e^{-2s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq K_{12}\ p(r)^{-2}q(r)^{2+2\mathsf{h}}e^{-2r},

(4.59)

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{\infty}s^{2}p(s)^{-1}q(s)^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq K_{13}\ q(r)^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r},

(4.60)

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{\infty}s^{2}p(s)^{-1}q(s)^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s\geq K_{14}\ q(r)^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}.

Furthermore, if
0\leq\mathsf{h}<1
, the above inequalities hold for the following values of the constants
K_{j}
:

(4.61)

\begin{array}[]{|l||c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr&K_{1}&K_{2}&K_{3}&K_{4}&K_{5}&K_{6}&K_{7}&K_{8}&K_{9}&K_{10}&K_{11}&K_{12}&K_{13}&K_{14}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{h}=0&4.65&2.37&1.00&1/\varepsilon&1/\varepsilon&1.30&2.00&2.00&1.00&0.500&0.250&0.350&0.500&0.250\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{h}\in(0,1)&91.4&625&1.00&1/\varepsilon&1/\varepsilon&5.00&5.00&5.00&1.00&0.500&0.300&0.380&0.750&0.0169\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Proof.

The existence of all but one of the constants
K_{j}
is a direct consequence of Lemma
4.3
. Indeed, considering

p(s)\simeq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}s&\text{in case }\sf\mathsf{h}=0,\\
1+s&\text{in case }\mathsf{h}>0,\end{array}\right.\qquad q(s)\simeq 1+s,

we see that

•

(
4.47
) and (
4.48
) follow from item (a) of Lemma
4.3
,

•

(
4.49
), (
4.50
) and (
4.51
) follow from item (b),

•

(
4.52
) follows from item (c),

•

(
4.53
) and (
4.54
) follow from item (d),

•

(
4.55
) and (
4.56
) follow from item (d) if
\sf\mathsf{h}=0
and item (f) if
\mathsf{h}>0
, and

•

(
4.57
), (
4.58
), (
4.59
) and (
4.60
) follow from item (f), with the exception of (
4.58
) in case
\mathsf{h}=0
, which will be proven below.

But finding explicit values for these constants, which we only wish to do for
0\leq\mathsf{h}<1
, requires more work. We analyze each integral individually. A helpful general remark, to be used repeatedly in what follows, is that, for
a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{R}
,

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\big(r^{a}p(r)^{b}q(r)^{c}e^{dr}\big)=r^{a}p(r)^{b}q(r)^{c}e^{dr}\big(a\,r^{-1}+2b\,p(r)^{-1}+2c\,q(r)^{-1}+d\big).

We also make the following disclaimers about the values to be obtained:

•

most of them are in no way optimal;

•

for some of them, there will clearly be no need to restrict the value of
\mathsf{h}
to
[0,1)
or
\varepsilon
to
(0,1/2]
, other than that these just happen to be the ranges that we wish to consider;

•

for all of them, the proof of the
0<\mathsf{h}<1
case would also apply to the
\mathsf{h}=0
case; however, we provide separate derivations for when
\mathsf{h}=0
because we wish to derive better constants for this special case, at least when a better derivation is indeed available.

(
\bm{K_{1}}
)

For
s\leq r
, one can check that
p(s)\geq(s/r)p(r)
. We use this to estimate
p(s)^{-1}
from above:

\int_{0}^{r}s^{2}p(s)^{-1}q(s)^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq p(r)^{-1}\int_{0}^{r}s^{2}\left(\frac{s}{r}\right)^{-1}q(s)^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s=rp(r)^{-1}\int_{0}^{r}sq(s)^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s.

Thus we will be done if we can find
K_{1}
such that

(4.62)

\int_{0}^{r}sq(s)^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq K_{1}r^{2}q(r)^{-2}\quad\text{for all }r>0.

For
\mathsf{h}=0
, the integral on the left is explicitly computable and we can choose

\max_{r>0}r^{-2}q(r)^{2}\int_{0}^{r}se^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s=\max_{r>0}r^{-2}(1+2r)^{2}\big(1-(1+r)e^{-r}\big)<4.65=:K_{1}.

The existence of this maximum is clear — the apparent
r^{-2}
singularity for small
r
is canceled by the term
1-(1+r)e^{-r}=O(r^{2})
, — however, the explicit value
4.65
was found with computer assistance. This will also be the case for many of the other inequalities below in the
\mathsf{h}=0
case.

For
0<\mathsf{h}<1
, noting that both sides of (
4.62
) vanish at
r=0
, we can instead compare their derivatives: We will be done if we can find
K_{1}
such that

rq(r)^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}\leq K_{1}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\big(r^{2}q(r)^{-2}\big)=K_{1}\cdot 2(1+4\mathsf{h})rq(r)^{-3}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad K_{1}\geq\max_{r>0}\frac{q(r)^{3+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}}{2+8\mathsf{h}}.

With a little bit of Calculus, this maximum is found to be exactly
(2+8\mathsf{h})^{-1}(6+2\mathsf{h})^{3+\mathsf{h}}e^{\mathsf{h}-5/2}
, which in turn is checked to be increasing in
\mathsf{h}
and therefore bounded above by its value
10^{-1}8^{4}e^{-\frac{3}{2}}<91.4=:K_{1}
at
\mathsf{h}=1
.

(
\bm{K_{2}}
)

We start by estimating
p(s)^{-2}
like we did
p(s)^{-1}
above, as well as bounding
q(s)^{2\varepsilon}
by
q(s)
(using the fact that
\varepsilon\leq 1/2
):

\int_{0}^{r}s^{2}p(s)^{-2}q(s)^{2\mathsf{h}+2\varepsilon}e^{-2s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq r^{2}p(r)^{-2}\int_{0}^{r}q(s)^{1+2\mathsf{h}}e^{-2s}\ \mathrm{d}s.

Thus we will be done if we can find
K_{2}
such that

(4.63)

\int_{0}^{r}q(s)^{1+2\mathsf{h}}e^{-2s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq K_{2}rq(r)^{-1}\quad\text{for all }r>0.

For
\mathsf{h}=0
, the integral on the left is explicitly computable and we can choose

\max_{r>0}r^{-1}(1+2r)\big(1-(1+r)e^{-2r}\big)<2.37=:K_{2}.

For
0<\mathsf{h}<1
, we again compare derivatives in (
4.63
): We will be done if we can find
K_{2}
such that

q(r)^{1+2\mathsf{h}}e^{-2r}\leq K_{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\big(rq(r)^{-1}\big)=K_{2}(1+4\mathsf{h})q(r)^{-2}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad K_{2}\geq\max_{r>0}\frac{q(r)^{3+2\mathsf{h}}e^{-2r}}{1+4\mathsf{h}}.

This maximum is found to be
(1+4\mathsf{h})^{-1}(3+2\mathsf{h})^{3+2\mathsf{h}}e^{2\mathsf{h}-2}
, which, for
0<\mathsf{h}<1
, is bounded above by
5^{-1}5^{5}e^{0}=625=:K_{2}
similarly to the previous item.

(
\bm{K_{3}}
)

We bound
p(s)^{-1}
as before, but this time the leftover integral can be computed explicitly for any
\mathsf{h}\geq 0
:

\int_{0}^{r}sp(s)^{-1}q(s)^{-2}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq\int_{0}^{r}s\left(\frac{s}{r}\right)^{-1}p(r)^{-1}q(s)^{-2}\ \mathrm{d}s=rp(r)^{-1}\int_{0}^{r}q(s)^{-2}\ \mathrm{d}s\\
=rp(r)^{-1}\frac{1}{1+4\mathsf{h}}rq(r)^{-1}\leq r^{2}p(r)^{-1}q(r)^{-1}.

(
\bm{K_{4}}
)

We use not only
p(s)^{-3}\leq(s/r)^{-3}p(r)^{-3}
like before, but also the analogous bound for
q(s)^{-2+\varepsilon}
— note that the exponent
-2+\varepsilon
is negative. This gives

\int_{0}^{r}s^{4}p(s)^{-3}q(s)^{-2+\varepsilon}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq p(r)^{-3}q(r)^{-2+\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{r}s^{4}\left(\frac{s}{r}\right)^{-3}\left(\frac{s}{r}\right)^{-2+\varepsilon}\ \mathrm{d}s\\
=r^{5-\varepsilon}p(r)^{-3}q(r)^{-2+\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{r}s^{-1+\varepsilon}\ \mathrm{d}s=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}r^{5}p(r)^{-3}q(r)^{-2+\varepsilon}.

Remark: The L’Hôpital Rule reveals that
