\lim_{r\to\infty}\big(r^{5}p(r)^{-3}q(r)^{-2+\varepsilon}\big)^{-1}\int_{0}^{r}s^{4}p(s)^{-3}q(s)^{-2+\varepsilon}\ \mathrm{d}s=1/\varepsilon
, proving that the value
K_{4}=1/\varepsilon
is optimal. The fact that it blows up when
\varepsilon
is taken close to 0 is what will explain the unfortunate fact that, in Theorem
4.7
ahead, the value of
\mathsf{g}_{0}=\mathsf{g}_{0}(\varepsilon)
becomes worse (smaller) when a better (smaller)
\varepsilon
is chosen.

(
\bm{K_{5}}
)

The same proof as for
K_{4}
above applies here, only with different exponents.

(
\bm{K_{6}}
)

Again we begin by using estimates for
p(s)^{-1}
and
q(s)^{-2}
like before:

\int_{0}^{r}s^{3}p(s)^{-1}q(s)^{-2-2\mathsf{h}}e^{2s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq r^{3}p(r)^{-1}q(r)^{-2}\int_{0}^{r}q(s)^{-2\mathsf{h}}e^{2s}.

Now we will be done if we can find
K_{6}
such that

(4.64)

\int_{0}^{r}q(s)^{-2\mathsf{h}}e^{2s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq K_{6}rq(r)^{-1-2\mathsf{h}}e^{2r}\quad\text{for all }r>0.

If
\mathsf{h}=0
, the explicit computation of the integral leads to the choice

\max_{r>0}(2r^{-1})(1+2r)\big(1-e^{-2r}\big)<1.30=:K_{6}.

If
0<\mathsf{h}<1
, we compare derivatives in (
4.64
): It suffices to find
K_{6}
such that

\displaystyle q(r)^{-2\mathsf{h}}e^{2r}

\displaystyle\leq K_{6}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\big(rq(r)^{-1-2\mathsf{h}}e^{2r}\big)

\displaystyle=K_{6}rq(r)^{-1-2\mathsf{h}}e^{2r}\big(r^{-1}-2(1+2\mathsf{h})q(r)^{-1}+2\big)

\displaystyle=q(r)^{-2\mathsf{h}}e^{2r}\cdot K_{6}q(r)^{-2}\big(q(r)-2(1+2\mathsf{h})r+2rq(r)\big)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow K_{6}

\displaystyle\geq\max_{r>0}q(r)^{2}\big(q(r)-2(1+2\mathsf{h})r+2rq(r)\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\max_{r>0}\frac{(1+4\mathsf{h})^{2}+(4+16\mathsf{h})r+4r^{2}}{1+4\mathsf{h}+(2+4\mathsf{h})r+4r^{2}}.

We estimate this maximum by comparing the coefficients of the distinct powers of
r
: Using
\mathsf{h}<1
, we have
(1+4\mathsf{h})^{2}<5(1+4\mathsf{h})
and
4+16\mathsf{h}<(10/3)(2+4\mathsf{h})<5(2+4\mathsf{h})
, so we can choose
K_{6}:=5
.

(
\bm{K_{7}}
)

The idea is again to compare the derivatives of both sides of the desired inequality, but now what we have to prove looks different because the integral is of the form
\int_{r}^{\infty}
instead of
\int_{0}^{r}
: We claim it suffices to find
K_{7}>0
such that

(4.65)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\bigg(r^{-1}q(r)^{\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}\bigg)\leq-\frac{1}{K_{7}}r^{-2}q(r)^{\frac{3}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}.

The reason why this is enough is that, if (
4.65
) is known, then

r^{-1}q(r)^{\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}=-\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\bigg(s^{-1}q(s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\bigg)\ \mathrm{d}s\geq\frac{1}{K_{7}}\int_{r}^{\infty}s^{-2}q(s)^{-\frac{3}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s

follows, which is the desired inequality. To prove (
4.65
), we estimate:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\bigg(r^{-1}q(r)^{\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}\bigg)

\displaystyle=r^{-1}q(r)^{\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}\bigg(-r^{-1}+(1+2\mathsf{h})q(r)^{-1}-1\bigg)

\displaystyle=-r^{-2}q(r)^{\frac{3}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}\frac{1+4\mathsf{h}+2(1+\mathsf{h})r+2r^{2}}{(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{2}}.

Thus we can define
K_{7}
by

\frac{1}{K_{7}}=\inf_{r\geq 0}f_{\mathsf{h}}(r),\quad\text{where }f_{\mathsf{h}}(r):=\frac{1+4\mathsf{h}+2(1+\mathsf{h})r+2r^{2}}{(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{2}}.

For
\mathsf{h}=0
, one checks that
f_{0}
is decreasing in
r
and thus bounded below by its limit
1/2
at
r=\infty
, which yields
K_{7}=2
. For
0<\mathsf{h}<1
, one checks that
f_{\mathsf{h}}
attains the global minimum
m_{\mathsf{h}}:=(1+6\mathsf{h}-\mathsf{h}^{2})(2+12\mathsf{h}+16\mathsf{h}^{2})^{-1}
at some finite value of
r\geq 0
, and furthermore
m_{\mathsf{h}}
is decreasing in
\mathsf{h}
; therefore
K_{7}=m_{1}^{-1}=5
works.

(
\bm{K_{8}}
)

Analogously to the above case, we compute

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\bigg(r^{-1}q(r)^{2+2\mathsf{h}}e^{-2r}\bigg)

\displaystyle=r^{-1}q(r)^{2+2\mathsf{h}}e^{-2r}\bigg(-r^{-1}+4(1+\mathsf{h})q(r)^{-1}-2\bigg)

\displaystyle=-r^{-2}q(r)^{3+2\mathsf{h}}e^{-2r}\frac{1+4\mathsf{h}+4\mathsf{h}r+4r^{2}}{(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{2}},

which leads to the ansatz

\frac{1}{K_{8}}=\inf_{r\geq 0}f_{\mathsf{h}}(r),\quad\text{where now }f_{\mathsf{h}}(r):=\frac{1+4\mathsf{h}+4\mathsf{h}r+4r^{2}}{(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{2}}.

This time, for any
0\leq\mathsf{h}\leq 1
, the global minimum
m_{\mathsf{h}}:=(1+4\mathsf{h}-\mathsf{h}^{2})(2+10\mathsf{h}+8\mathsf{h}^{2})^{-1}
of
f_{\mathsf{h}}
is attained at a finite
r\geq 0
and is also decreasing with
\mathsf{h}
, again leading to the values
K_{8}=2
when
\mathsf{h}=0
and
K_{8}=5
otherwise.

(
\bm{K_{9}}
)

We proceed similarly to the previous two cases, except that now a simpler estimate is available in the middle of the calculation:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\bigg(p(r)^{-1}q(r)^{2\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-2r}\bigg)

\displaystyle=p(r)^{-1}q(r)^{2\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-2r}\bigg(-2p(r)^{-1}+2(2\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon)q(r)^{-1}-2\bigg)

\displaystyle=-2p(r)^{-2}q(r)^{1+2\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-2r}\bigg(1-(2\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon)\frac{4\mathsf{h}+2r}{(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{2}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq-2p(r)^{-2}q(r)^{1+2\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-2r}\bigg(1-\frac{\varepsilon+2\mathsf{h}}{1+4\mathsf{h}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq-p(r)^{-2}q(r)^{1+2\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-2r},

where we used
\varepsilon\leq 1/2
in the last step. This proves that the value
K_{9}=1
is good enough.

(
\bm{K_{10}}
)

Use
sp(s)^{-1}\leq 1/2
:

\int_{r}^{\infty}sp(s)^{-3}q(s)^{1+2\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-2s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{r}^{\infty}p(s)^{-2}q(s)^{1+2\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-2s}\ \mathrm{d}s.

This reduces the estimate to that of the previous case, only with an extra factor of
1/2
included. Thus
K_{10}=K_{9}/2=1/2
is good enough.

(
\bm{K_{11}}
)

We start by using
s^{2}p(s)^{-2}\leq 1/4
:

\int_{r}^{\infty}s^{2}p(s)^{-2}q(s)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{1}{4}\int_{r}^{\infty}q(s)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s.

Then we will be done if we can find
K_{11}>0
such that

(4.66)

\int_{r}^{\infty}q(s)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq 4K_{11}\,q(r)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}.

For
\mathsf{h}\leq 1/2
, the factor
q(s)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}
in the integrand is decreasing and thus bounded above by
q(r)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}
:

\int_{r}^{\infty}q(s)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq q(r)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}\int_{r}^{\infty}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s=q(r)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad K_{11}=\frac{1}{4}\text{ works.}

For
\mathsf{h}>1/2
, we integrate by parts once and then use the fact that
q(s)^{-\frac{3}{2}+\mathsf{h}}
is decreasing and
-1+2\mathsf{h}>0
:

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{\infty}q(s)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=q(r)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}+(-1+2\mathsf{h})\int_{r}^{\infty}q(s)^{-\frac{3}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq q(r)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}+(-1+2\mathsf{h})q(r)^{-\frac{3}{2}+\mathsf{h}}\int_{r}^{\infty}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\big(1+(-1+2\mathsf{h})q(r)^{-1}\big)q(r)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{-1+2\mathsf{h}}{1+4\mathsf{h}}\right)q(r)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}

\displaystyle<\frac{6}{5}q(r)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad K_{11}=\frac{3}{10}\text{ works}.

(
\bm{K_{12}}
)

We start by using
p(s)^{-2}\leq p(r)^{-2}
and
s\leq q(s)/2
. Then we also note that
q(s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}
is decreasing — its derivative reads
-2q(s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}(\mathsf{h}+s)/(1+4\mathsf{h}+2s)<0
— and therefore bounded above by its value at
s=r
:

\int_{r}^{\infty}sp(s)^{-2}q(s)^{1+2\mathsf{h}}e^{-2s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{1}{2}p(r)^{-2}\int_{r}^{\infty}q(s)^{2+2\mathsf{h}}e^{-2s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{1}{2}p(r)^{-2}q(r)^{\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}\int_{r}^{\infty}q(s)^{\frac{3}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s.

Thus we will be done if we can find
K_{12}
such that

(4.67)

\int_{r}^{\infty}q(s)^{\frac{3}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq 2K_{12}\,q(r)^{\frac{3}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}.

After integrating by parts twice, the integral on the left reduces to one for which we already have a bound in (
4.66
) above:

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{\infty}q(s)^{\frac{3}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=q(r)^{\frac{3}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}+(3+2\mathsf{h})\int_{r}^{\infty}q(s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=q(r)^{\frac{3}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}+(3+2\mathsf{h})q(r)^{\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}+(3+2\mathsf{h})(1+2\mathsf{h})\int_{r}^{\infty}q(s)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq q(r)^{\frac{3}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}+(3+2\mathsf{h})q(r)^{\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}+4K_{11}(3+2\mathsf{h})(1+2\mathsf{h})q(r)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}

\displaystyle=\big(1+(3+2\mathsf{h})q(r)^{-1}+4K_{11}(3+2\mathsf{h})(1+2\mathsf{h})q(r)^{-2}\big)q(r)^{\frac{3}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{3+2\mathsf{h}}{1+4\mathsf{h}}+4K_{11}\frac{(3+2\mathsf{h})(1+2\mathsf{h})}{(1+4\mathsf{h})^{2}}\right)q(r)^{\frac{3}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}.

If
\mathsf{h}=0
, using
K_{11}=1/4
, the expression in parenthesis becomes
7
. For all other values of
\mathsf{h}
, using
K_{11}=3/10
instead, it is bounded above by its limit as
\mathsf{h}\to 0^{+}
, which is now
38/5
. Therefore (
4.67
) implies that
K_{12}=7/2
can be taken for
\mathsf{h}=0
and
K_{12}=19/5
can be taken for
0<\mathsf{h}<1
.

(
\bm{K_{13}}
)

If
\mathsf{h}=0
, the desired inequality becomes

\frac{1}{2}\int_{r}^{\infty}se^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq K_{13}\ (1+2r)e^{-r},

whose optimal constant is easily seen to be
\sup_{r>0}(1+2r)^{-1}(1+r)/2=1/2=:K_{13}
. If
0<\mathsf{h}<1
, we bound a factor of
s
by
q(s)/2
and the expression
sp(s)^{-1}
by
1/2
, then we integrate by parts until a negative coefficient is reached in front of the integral, at which point it may get ignored:

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{\infty}s^{2}p(s)^{-1}q(s)^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}\int_{r}^{\infty}q(s)^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left(q(r)^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}+2(1+\mathsf{h})\int_{r}^{\infty}q(s)^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}q(r)^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}+\frac{1+\mathsf{h}}{2}\left(q(r)^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}+2\mathsf{h}\int_{r}^{\infty}q(s)^{\mathsf{h}-1}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}q(r)^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}+\frac{1+\mathsf{h}}{2}q(r)^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}+\mathsf{h}(1+\mathsf{h})\bigg(q(r)^{\mathsf{h}-1}e^{-r}

\displaystyle\qquad+2(\mathsf{h}-1)\int_{r}^{\infty}q(s)^{\mathsf{h}-2}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}q(r)^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}+\frac{1+\mathsf{h}}{2}q(r)^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}+\mathsf{h}(1+\mathsf{h})q(r)^{\mathsf{h}-1}e^{-r}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1+\mathsf{h}}{2}q(r)^{-1}+\mathsf{h}(1+\mathsf{h})q(r)^{-2}\right)q(r)^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1+\mathsf{h}}{2(1+4\mathsf{h})}+\frac{\mathsf{h}(1+\mathsf{h})}{(1+4\mathsf{h})^{2}}\right)q(r)^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{4}q(r)^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}.

(
\bm{K_{14}}
)

This is the only item in the lemma whose inequality sign goes the other way. If
\mathsf{h}=0
, replace
\sup
with
\inf
in the derivation of
K_{13}
above to find
K_{14}:=1/4
. If
0<\mathsf{h}<1
, we bound the
q(s)^{\mathsf{h}}
term below by
q(r)^{\mathsf{h}}
, while the problematic
s^{2}
term can be dealt with by first throwing out the
(r,r+2\mathsf{h})
piece of the integration domain and then changing variables to
t=s-2\mathsf{h}
:

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{\infty}s^{2}p(s)^{-1}q(s)^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\geq q(r)^{\mathsf{h}}\int_{r+2\mathsf{h}}^{\infty}s^{2}p(s)^{-1}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=q(r)^{\mathsf{h}}\int_{r}^{\infty}(2\mathsf{h}+t)^{2}p(2\mathsf{h}+t)^{-1}e^{-(2\mathsf{h}+t)}\ \mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=e^{-2\mathsf{h}}q(r)^{\mathsf{h}}\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{(2\mathsf{h}+t)^{2}}{8\mathsf{h}+2t}e^{-t}\ \mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\frac{e^{-2\mathsf{h}}}{4}q(r)^{\mathsf{h}}\int_{r}^{\infty}(2\mathsf{h}+t)e^{-t}\ \mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-2\mathsf{h}}}{4}q(r)^{\mathsf{h}}(1+2\mathsf{h}+r)e^{-r}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-2\mathsf{h}}}{4}q(r)^{1+\mathsf{h}}\frac{1+2\mathsf{h}+r}{1+4\mathsf{h}+2r}e^{-r}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{e^{-2}}{8}q(r)^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}

\displaystyle>0.0169\ q(r)^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}.\qed

4.3.
The mass/deviation problem without gravity

Although the parameter
\mathsf{g}
defined in (
4.7
) is positive, having an explicit solution of the mass/deviation problem for
\mathsf{g}=0
in our hands will be essential for our subsequent study of it as a perturbation problem. So we derive this solution now:

Theorem 4.5
.

Let
\mathsf{h}\geq 0
. Then there exist functions
(\nu,\chi)\in C^{1}\big((0,\infty)\big)\times C^{2}\big((0,\infty)\big)
solving the mass/deviation problem
(
4.41
)
,
(
4.42
)
of parameters
(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{h})=(0,\mathsf{h})
and such that there are constants
X_{0},\ldots,X_{5}>0
, depending on
\mathsf{h}
, with

(4.68)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\chi(r)\right\rvert=\chi(r)\geq X_{0}\,(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r},

(4.69)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\chi(r)\right\rvert=\chi(r)\leq X_{1}\,(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r},

(4.70)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\chi^{\prime}(r)\right\rvert=-\chi^{\prime}(r)\leq X_{2}\,r^{2}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-r},

(4.71)

\displaystyle\left\lvert 1-\chi(r)\right\rvert=1-\chi(r)\leq X_{3}\,r^{3}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-2},

(4.72)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\upsilon(r)\right\rvert=\upsilon(r)\leq X_{4}\,r^{4}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-2-\mathsf{h}}e^{r},

(4.73)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\upsilon^{\prime}(r)\right\rvert\leq X_{5}\,r^{3}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1-\mathsf{h}}e^{r}

for all
r>0
, where

(4.74)

\upsilon(r):=\chi(r)\int_{0}^{r}\frac{t^{3}\ \mathrm{d}t}{(2\mathsf{h}+t)\chi(t)^{2}}

is called the
conjugate solution
to
\chi
. Furthermore, if
0\leq\mathsf{h}<1
, the above inequalities hold for the following values of the constants
X_{j}
:

(4.75)

\begin{array}[]{|l||c|c|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr&X_{0}&X_{1}&X_{2}&X_{3}&X_{4}&X_{5}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{h}=0&0.500&1.00&2.00&9.29&4.00&2.60\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{h}\in(0,1)&1.14\cdot 10^{-3}&8.93&11.9&1.09\cdot 10^{3}&6.88\cdot 10^{7}&2.87\cdot 10^{6}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Proof.

The
\nu
equation in (
4.41
) with
\mathsf{g}
set equal to 0 becomes trivial, and its boundary condition (the first condition in (
4.42
)) implies
\nu\equiv-\mathsf{h}
. The rest of the problem is now a boundary-value problem for
\chi
:

(4.76)

\chi^{\prime\prime}(r)=\frac{2(r+3\mathsf{h})}{r(r+2\mathsf{h})}\chi^{\prime}(r)+\frac{r}{r+2\mathsf{h}}\chi(r),\quad\chi(0)=1,\quad\chi(\infty)=0.

In the case
\mathsf{h}=0
, there is not much to do: Elementary formulas for the solution
\chi
and its conjugate
\upsilon
are available, as can be promptly checked:

(4.77)

\chi(r)=(1+r)e^{-r},\quad\upsilon(r)=\frac{(1+r)e^{-r}-(1-r)e^{r}}{2}.

Note that this
\chi
is the same electric-deviation scalar (
2.19
) from section
2
, which makes sense because setting
\mathsf{g}=0
turns General Relativity into Special Relativity, while
\mathsf{h}=0
means
\nu\equiv 0
and thus
\beta\equiv 1
, that is, the metric becomes Minkowski. Also note the derivative
\chi^{\prime}
for future use:

(4.78)

\chi^{\prime}(r)=-re^{-r}.

The desired inequalities (
4.68
)–(
4.73
) for these functions become

\displaystyle(1+r)e^{-r}\geq X_{0}\,(1+2r)e^{-r},

\displaystyle(1+r)e^{-r}\leq X_{1}\,(1+2r)e^{-r},

\displaystyle re^{-r}\leq X_{2}\,r^{2}(2r)^{-1}e^{-r},

\displaystyle 1-(1+r)e^{-r}\leq X_{3}\,r^{3}(2r)^{-1}(1+2r)^{-2},

\displaystyle\frac{(1+r)e^{-r}-(1-r)e^{r}}{2}\leq X_{4}\,r^{4}(2r)^{-1}(1+2r)^{-2}e^{r},

\displaystyle\frac{re^{r}-re^{-r}}{2}\leq X_{5}\,r^{3}(2r)^{-1}(1+2r)^{-1}e^{r}.

Here the values
X_{0}=1/2
,
X_{1}=1
,
X_{2}=2
are easily seen to be optimal, while
X_{3},X_{4},X_{5}
can be defined and numerically estimated as in the proof of Lemma
4.4
, that is, by bounding the ratio between the left and right sides of each inequality:

(4.79)

\displaystyle X_{3}

\displaystyle=\max_{r>0}\frac{1-(1+r)e^{-r}}{r^{3}(2r)^{-1}(1+2r)^{-2}}<9.29,

(4.80)

\displaystyle X_{4}

\displaystyle=\sup_{r>0}\frac{\big((1+r)e^{-r}-(1-r)e^{r}\big)}{2\big(r^{4}(2r)^{-1}(1+2r)^{-2}e^{r}\big)}=4,

(4.81)

\displaystyle X_{5}

\displaystyle=\max_{r>0}\frac{re^{r}-re^{-r}}{2\big(r^{3}(2r)^{-1}(1+2r)^{-1}e^{r}\big)}<2.60.

This concludes the
\mathsf{h}=0
case. Now let
\mathsf{h}>0
for the remainder of the proof. The non-trivial step in the study of problem (
4.76
) is to realize that the transformations

\overline{\chi}(r):=-\frac{2\mathsf{h}\,\chi^{\prime}(r)}{r^{2}},\quad\overline{\overline{\chi}}(s):=e^{-2\mathsf{h}}\,\overline{\chi}\left(\frac{s}{2}-2\mathsf{h}\right)e^{s/2}

reduce the ODE to Kummer’s equation (
4.1
) of parameters
a=1-\mathsf{h}
and
b=2
, ultimately leading to the ansatz

(4.82)

\chi(r):=\frac{1}{2\mathsf{h}\,U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})}\int_{r}^{\infty}t^{2}U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h}+2t)e^{-t}\ \mathrm{d}t.

We do not have to show proof of this reduction; for mathematical correctness, it suffices to prove now that (
4.82
) is indeed a solution to (
4.76
). To this end, first note that
\chi
is well-defined: We have already noted that
U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})
is never zero, and the integrability of
t^{2}U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h}+2t)e^{-t}
around
t=\infty
is granted by the
e^{-t}
term, which decays faster than the polynomial-like term
t^{2}U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h}+2t)\sim t^{2}t^{\mathsf{h}-1}
grows (see (
4.5
)). This also implies
\chi(\infty)=0
. Next, to check that
\chi
solves the differential equation in (
4.76
), we find an expression for
\chi
not involving any integrals. Consider the auxiliary function

V(t)=U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h}+2t)e^{-t}.

The Kummer equation (
4.1
) for
U_{\mathsf{h}}
yields, after some simple algebra, the following second-order equation for
V
:

(4.83)

(2\mathsf{h}+t)V^{\prime\prime}(t)+2V^{\prime}(t)=tV(t).

Then we use integration by parts to express a multiple of
\chi
in terms of
V
and
V^{\prime}
without having to use any integral signs in the end:

(4.84)

\displaystyle-2\mathsf{h}\,U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})\chi(r)

\displaystyle=-\int_{r}^{\infty}t^{2}V(t)\ \mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=-\int_{r}^{\infty}t\big((2\mathsf{h}+t)V^{\prime\prime}(t)+2V^{\prime}(t)\big)\ \mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=-(t^{2}+2\mathsf{h}t)V^{\prime}(t)\bigg|_{t=r}^{\infty}+\int_{r}^{\infty}(2\mathsf{h}+t)V^{\prime}(t)\ \mathrm{d}t-2tV(t)\bigg|_{t=r}^{\infty}+\int_{r}^{\infty}2V(t)\ \mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=(r^{2}+2\mathsf{h}r)V^{\prime}(r)+(2\mathsf{h}+t)V(t)\bigg|_{t=r}^{\infty}-\int_{r}^{\infty}2V(t)\ \mathrm{d}t+2rV(r)+\int_{r}^{\infty}2V(t)\ \mathrm{d}t

(4.85)

\displaystyle=(r^{2}+2\mathsf{h}r)V^{\prime}(r)-2\mathsf{h}V(r).

Differentiating this two times and using the
V
equation (
4.83
), we also find

-2\mathsf{h}\,U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})\chi^{\prime}(r)=r^{2}V(r),\quad-2\mathsf{h}\,U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})\chi^{\prime\prime}(r)=2rV(r)+r^{2}V^{\prime}(r),

and now the
\chi
equation (
4.76
) is easy to verify:

\displaystyle-2\mathsf{h}\,

\displaystyle U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})\left(\chi^{\prime\prime}-\frac{2(r+3\mathsf{h})}{r(r-2\mathsf{h})}\chi^{\prime}-\frac{r}{r+2\mathsf{h}}\chi\right)

\displaystyle=2rV+r^{2}V^{\prime}-\frac{2(r+3\mathsf{h})}{r(r+2\mathsf{h})}r^{2}V-\frac{r}{r+2\mathsf{h}}\big((r^{2}+2\mathsf{h}r)V^{\prime}-2\mathsf{h}V\big)

\displaystyle=\left(2r-\frac{2r(r+3\mathsf{h})}{r+2\mathsf{h}}+\frac{2\mathsf{h}r}{r+2\mathsf{h}}\right)V+\left(r^{2}-\frac{r(r^{2}+2\mathsf{h}r)}{r+2\mathsf{h}}\right)V^{\prime}

\displaystyle=0.

To finish checking the boundary conditions on
\chi
, a different integral expression is more convenient:

(4.86)

\chi(r)=1-\frac{1}{2\mathsf{h}\,U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})}\int_{0}^{r}t^{2}U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h}+2t)e^{-t}\ \mathrm{d}t,\quad r>0.

This expression follows immediately from our definition (
4.82
) of
\chi
after using the fact that

\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{2}U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h}+2t)e^{-t}\ \mathrm{d}t=2\mathsf{h}\,U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h}),

which in turn is obtained from (
4.84
) and (
4.85
) when setting
r=0
. Then plugging
r=0
into (
4.86
) is allowed because the integrand is not singular at
t=0
, and we find
\chi(0)=1
.

Hence a solution
\chi
to (
4.76
) exists also for all
\mathsf{h}>0
. We also point out that, since the ODE (
4.76
) arises from the
\chi
equation from (
3.30
) with
\alpha=0
and
\beta=r/(r+2\mathsf{h})
, our previous Lemma
3.3
is applicable and yields

(4.87)

0<\chi(r)<1,\quad\chi^{\prime}(r)<0\quad\text{for all }r>0.

We will use these properties shortly. Next we tackle the estimates (
4.68
)–(
4.73
). For this we need lower and upper bounds for the quantity
U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h}+2t)/\big(2\mathsf{h}\,U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})\big)
featuring in the solution formulas proved for
\chi
. Lemma
4.2
provides bounds for
U_{\mathsf{h}}(s)
,
s\geq 4\mathsf{h}
, in the
0\leq\mathsf{h}<1
regime by bracketing
U_{\mathsf{h}}(s)
between constant multiples of the function
s^{-1}(1+s)^{\mathsf{h}}
. We claim that this bracketing holds in general (but for different constants): For
\mathsf{h}>0
, there exist
\mathsf{h}
-dependent constants
M_{1},M_{2}>0
such that

(4.88)

M_{1}s^{-1}(1+s)^{\mathsf{h}}\leq U_{\mathsf{h}}(s)\leq M_{2}s^{-1}(1+s)^{\mathsf{h}}\quad\text{for all }s\geq 4\mathsf{h}.

To prove this, we check the sufficient conditions described after Definition
1.1
of the equivalence relation
\simeq
. First note that the two limits

\lim_{s\to a}\frac{U_{\mathsf{h}}(s)}{s^{-1}(1+s)^{\mathsf{h}}},\quad a=4\mathsf{h}^{+}\text{ and }a=\infty,

are finite and nonzero — for
a=4\mathsf{h}^{+}
this is due to
U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})>0
, while for
a=\infty
it follows from the
asymptotic property (
4.5
). Moreover, given that
\chi^{\prime}(r)
is found from (
4.82
) as

(4.89)

\chi^{\prime}(r)=-\frac{U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)}{2\mathsf{h}\,U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})}r^{2}e^{-r},

and considering that we already know
U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})>0
and
\chi^{\prime}(r)<0
(see (
4.87
)), the strict positivity of
U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)
for all
r>0
follows, that is,
U_{\mathsf{h}}(s)>0
for all
s\geq 4\mathsf{h}
. Then
U_{\mathsf{h}}(s)\simeq s^{-1}(1+s)^{\mathsf{h}}
for
s\in[4\mathsf{h},\infty)
, that is, bounds of the form (
4.88
) hold as claimed. They imply, for all
t>0
,

(4.90)

\frac{U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h}+2t)}{2\mathsf{h}\,U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})}\geq\frac{1}{2\mathsf{h}}\frac{M_{1}(4\mathsf{h}+2t)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2t)^{\mathsf{h}}}{M_{2}(4\mathsf{h})^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h})^{\mathsf{h}}}\\
=\frac{2M_{1}}{(1+4\mathsf{h})^{\mathsf{h}}M_{2}}(4\mathsf{h}+2t)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2t)^{\mathsf{h}}=:N_{1}(4\mathsf{h}+2t)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2t)^{\mathsf{h}}

and analogously

(4.91)

\frac{U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h}+2t)}{2\mathsf{h}\,U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})}\leq\frac{2M_{2}}{(1+4\mathsf{h})^{\mathsf{h}}M_{1}}(4\mathsf{h}+2t)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2t)^{\mathsf{h}}=:N_{2}(4\mathsf{h}+2t)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2t)^{\mathsf{h}}.

Also recall that in Lemma (
4.2
) we derived the values

M_{1}=\frac{0.232}{(1-\mathsf{h})\,\Gamma(1-\mathsf{h})},\quad M_{2}=\frac{1.37}{(1-\mathsf{h})\,\Gamma(1-\mathsf{h})}\quad\text{for }0\leq\mathsf{h}<1,

with which we now deduce

(4.92)

N_{1}=\frac{2\cdot 0.232}{1.37(1+4\mathsf{h})^{\mathsf{h}}}\geq\frac{2\cdot 0.232}{1.37\cdot 5}>0.0677,\quad N_{2}=\frac{2\cdot 1.37}{0.232(1+4\mathsf{h})^{\mathsf{h}}}<11.9\quad\text{for }0\leq\mathsf{h}<1.

Now we simply have to use (
4.90
) and (
4.91
) to prove (
4.68
)–(
4.73
) for all
\mathsf{h}>0
. Some of the inequalities from Lemma (
4.4
), employing the constants
K_{j}
, will be needed. Thus each
X_{j}
will be found in terms of the constants
K_{j}
and
N_{1},N_{2}
, and furthermore, as the reader’s calculator will check, the explicit values of
X_{j}
claimed in (
4.92
) for the case
0<\mathsf{h}<1
follow by subbing in the explicit ones for
K_{j}
(see (
4.61
)) and
N_{1},N_{2}
(see (
4.92
) just above).

(
\bm{X_{0}}
)

Use the definition (
4.82
) of
\chi
to see that
X_{0}:=N_{1}K_{14}
works:

\chi(r)=\frac{1}{2\mathsf{h}\,U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})}\int_{r}^{\infty}t^{2}U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h}+2t)e^{-t}\ \mathrm{d}t\geq N_{1}\int_{r}^{\infty}t^{2}(4\mathsf{h}+2t)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2t)^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-t}\ \mathrm{d}t\\
\geq N_{1}K_{14}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}.

(
\bm{X_{1}}
)

Analogously to the above item, we find that
X_{1}:=N_{2}K_{13}
works.

(
\bm{X_{2}}
)

The derivative
\chi^{\prime}(r)
was given in (
4.89
), immediately yielding
X_{2}:=N_{2}
:

(4.93)

\chi^{\prime}(r)=-\frac{U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)}{2\mathsf{h}\,U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})}r^{2}e^{-r}\geq-N_{2}r^{2}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}.

(
\bm{X_{3}}
)

This time we use the alternative formula (
4.86
) for
\chi
to find
X_{3}:=N_{2}K_{1}
:

1-\chi(r)=\frac{1}{2\mathsf{h}\,U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})}\int_{0}^{r}t^{2}U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h}+2t)e^{-t}\ \mathrm{d}t\leq N_{2}\int_{0}^{r}t^{2}(4\mathsf{h}+2t)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2t)^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-t}\ \mathrm{d}t\\
\leq N_{2}K_{1}r^{3}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-2}.

(
\bm{X_{4}}
)

We use (
4.68
) and (
4.69
) to bound the
\chi
factors in formula (
4.74
) for
\upsilon(r)
, ultimately arriving at the value
X_{4}:=2X_{1}K_{6}/X_{0}^{2}
:

\displaystyle\upsilon(r)

\displaystyle=\chi(r)\int_{0}^{r}\frac{t^{3}\ \mathrm{d}t}{(2\mathsf{h}+t)\chi(t)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2X_{1}}{X_{0}^{2}}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}\int_{0}^{r}\frac{t^{3}e^{2t}\ \mathrm{d}t}{(4\mathsf{h}+2t)(1+4\mathsf{h}+2t)^{2+2\mathsf{h}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2X_{1}}{X_{0}^{2}}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}K_{6}r^{4}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-3-2\mathsf{h}}e^{2r}

\displaystyle=\frac{2X_{1}K_{6}}{X_{0}^{2}}r^{4}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-2-\mathsf{h}}e^{r}.

(
\bm{X_{5}}
)

We differentiate
\upsilon(r)
and bound away with the help of (
4.68
) and (
4.70
), ultimately arriving at the value
X_{5}:=X_{2}K_{6}/(4X_{0}^{2})+2/X_{0}
:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\upsilon^{\prime}(r)\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\chi^{\prime}(r)\int_{0}^{r}\frac{t^{3}\ \mathrm{d}t}{(2\mathsf{h}+t)\chi(t)^{2}}+\frac{r^{3}}{(2\mathsf{h}+r)\chi(r)}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\lvert\chi^{\prime}(r)\right\rvert\int_{0}^{r}\frac{t^{3}\ \mathrm{d}t}{(4\mathsf{h}+2t)\chi(t)^{2}}+\frac{2r^{3}}{(4\mathsf{h}+2r)\chi(r)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2X_{2}}{X_{0}^{2}}r^{2}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}\int_{0}^{r}\frac{t^{3}e^{2t}\ \mathrm{d}t}{(4\mathsf{h}+2t)(1+4\mathsf{h}+2t)^{2+2\mathsf{h}}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{2}{X_{0}}\frac{r^{3}}{(4\mathsf{h}+2r)(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2X_{2}}{X_{0}^{2}}r^{2}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}K_{6}r^{4}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-3-2\mathsf{h}}e^{2r}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{2}{X_{0}}r^{3}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1-\mathsf{h}}e^{r}

\displaystyle=r^{3}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1-\mathsf{h}}e^{r}\left(\frac{2X_{2}K_{6}}{X_{0}^{2}}r^{3}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-2}+\frac{2}{X_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq r^{3}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1-\mathsf{h}}e^{r}\left(\frac{X_{2}K_{6}}{4X_{0}^{2}}+\frac{2}{X_{0}}\right).\qed

Remark 4.3
.

The conjugate solution
\upsilon
defined in (
4.74
) also solves the second-order ODE (
4.76
) and is independent from
\chi
at all
r>0
, in the sense that the Wronskian of
\chi
and
\upsilon
never vanishes over this range. In fact,
\upsilon
is the solution that results from the reduction-of-order technique applied to
\chi
. After quickly checking that
\upsilon(0)=0
and
\upsilon(\infty)=\infty
, it can be deduced that
\chi
as given in (
4.82
) is in fact the
unique
solution of the boundary-value problem (
4.76
).

To close this subsection, we prove a formula for the solution to the
inhomogeneous
version of the differential equation (
4.76
) for
\chi
. It will be required when tackling the
\mathsf{g}>0
problem, and it is here that the notion (
4.74
) of the conjugate
\upsilon
to
\chi
is needed.

Corollary 4.6
.

Let
\mathsf{h}\geq 0
. Let
\chi
and
\upsilon
be as in Theorem
4.5
, and suppose that a function
H\in C^{0}\big((0,\infty)\big)
satisfies

(4.94)

\left\lvert H(r)\right\rvert\leq Cr^{x}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{y}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{z}e^{-r}\quad\text{for all }r>0,

for some
C>0
and
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}
with
x>2
,
x+y\in(-2,1)
and
x+y+z\neq\mathsf{h}
. Then the formula

(4.95)

\psi(r)=-\chi(r)\int_{0}^{r}\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\upsilon(s)H(s)\ \mathrm{d}s-\upsilon(r)\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\chi(s)H(s)\ \mathrm{d}s

defines a solution to the inhomogeneous boundary-value problem

(4.96)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\psi^{\prime\prime}(r)=\dfrac{2(r+3\mathsf{h})}{r(r+2\mathsf{h})}\psi^{\prime}(r)+\dfrac{r}{r+2\mathsf{h}}\psi(r)+H(r),\\
\\
\psi(0)=\psi(\infty)=0.\end{array}\right.

We also write down its derivative:

(4.97)

\psi^{\prime}(r)=-\chi^{\prime}(r)\int_{0}^{r}\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\upsilon(s)H(s)\ \mathrm{d}s-\upsilon^{\prime}(r)\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\chi(s)H(s)\ \mathrm{d}s.

Proof.

Assuming that
\psi
is well-defined, it is immediate to check formula (
4.97
) and also that it solves the given inhomogeneous equation. In doing this, it helps to first check that
\upsilon
solves the homogeneous one. These checks are skipped. The non-trivial claim is that
\psi
is well-defined and satisfies the claimed null boundary conditions. We prove these properties separately for the two terms that compose it in formula (
4.95
).

Due to (
4.94
) and the estimate (
4.72
) for
\upsilon
, there is a constant that we also call
C
— we let the value of the arbitrary constant
C
get updated as needed along this proof — such that the first integrand in formula (
4.95
) obeys

\left\lvert\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\upsilon(s)H(s)\right\rvert\leq Cs^{1+x}(4\mathsf{h}+2s)^{y}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2s)^{-2-{\mathsf{h}}+z}.

On the right side of this inequality, we see a function
f(s)
which has positive, finite values for both of the limits
\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}s^{-c}f(s)
and
\lim_{s\to\infty}s^{-d}f(s)
, where

c=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1+x+y>-1&\text{in case }\mathsf{h}=0,\\
1+x>3>-1&\text{in case }\mathsf{h}>0,\end{array}\right.\quad d=-1-\mathsf{h}+x+y+z\neq-1.

Then Lemma
4.3
(item (b)) applies and yields the existence of
C>0
such that

\left\lvert\int_{0}^{r}\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\upsilon(s)H(s)\ \mathrm{d}s\right\rvert\leq C\int_{0}^{r}s^{1+x}(4\mathsf{h}+2s)^{y}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2s)^{-2-{\mathsf{h}}+z}\ \mathrm{d}s\\
\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}Cr^{2+x+y}(1+r)^{-2-{\mathsf{h}}+z}&\text{in case }\mathsf{h}=0,\\
Cr^{2+x}(1+r)^{-2-{\mathsf{h}}+y+z}&\text{in case }\mathsf{h}>0.\end{array}\right.

Now each of these two expressions is bounded above in its corresponding case by a multiple of the function
r^{2+x}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{y}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-2-{\mathsf{h}}+z}
. Thus, also using estimate (
4.69
) for
\chi
, we find for all
\mathsf{h}\geq 0

\left\lvert\chi(r)\int_{0}^{r}\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\upsilon(s)H(s)\ \mathrm{d}s\right\rvert\leq C(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}r^{2+x}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{y}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-2-{\mathsf{h}}+z}\\
=Cr^{2+x}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{y}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1+z}e^{-r},

which is everywhere bounded, vanishes at
r=0
(due to
x+y>-2
if
\mathsf{h}=0
, and due to
x>-2
if
\mathsf{h}>0
), and also clearly vanishes at
r=\infty
, as needed.

We proceed similarly for the second integrand in formula (
4.95
). This time (details skipped), it will be item (d) for
\mathsf{h}=0
and item (f) for
\mathsf{h}>0
from Lemma
4.3
that will help in ultimately deriving

\left\lvert\upsilon(r)\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\upsilon(s)H(s)\ \mathrm{d}s\right\rvert\leq Cr^{4}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-2+x+y}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-2-2\mathsf{h}+z}e^{-r},

which is everywhere bounded, vanishes at
r=0
(due to
x+y>-2
if
\mathsf{h}=0
), and also clearly vanishes at
r=\infty
, as needed.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

The solution formula (
4.95
) was found, of course, through the variation-of-parameters technique applied to the fundamental solutions
\chi
and
\upsilon
of the homogeneous ODE. This fact can be used to prove the
uniqueness
of the solution to the boundary-value problem (
4.96
).

4.4.
The mass/deviation problem with gravity

We now prove the existence of a solution to the mass/deviation problem (
4.41
), (
4.42
) of parameters
\mathsf{g}>0
and
\mathsf{h}\geq 0
when
\mathsf{g}
is small enough. A free parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2]
is part of this result’s statement, and we immediately see that choosing a smaller
\varepsilon
improves the power of the estimates to be obtained, which feature
\varepsilon
as an exponent on the right side, but this comes at the expense of having to make
\mathsf{g}
smaller. For simplicity, we will fix the value
\varepsilon=1/2
when computing explicit bounds in the case
0\leq\mathsf{h}<1
.

Theorem 4.7
.

Let
\mathsf{h}\geq 0
and
0<\varepsilon\leq 1/2
. Then there exist constants
W_{1},\ldots,W_{4},w_{1},\ldots,w_{3}>0
and a threshold value
\mathsf{g}_{0}>0
(all depending on
\mathsf{h}
and/or
\varepsilon
) such that, for all
0<\mathsf{g}<\mathsf{g}_{0}
, a solution
(\nu,\chi)\in C^{1}\big((0,\infty)\big)\times C^{2}\big((0,\infty)\big)
to the mass/deviation problem
(
4.41
)
,
(
4.42
)
of parameters
(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{h})
exists satisfying the following inequalities for all
r>0
:

(4.98)

\displaystyle\left\lvert 1+\chi(r)\right\rvert=1+\chi(r)\leq W_{1},

(4.99)

\displaystyle\left\lvert 1-\chi(r)\right\rvert=1-\chi(r)\leq W_{2}\,r^{3}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-2},

(4.100)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\chi(r)\right\rvert=\chi(r)\leq W_{3}\,(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r},

(4.101)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\chi^{\prime}(r)\right\rvert=-\chi^{\prime}(r)\leq W_{4}\,r^{2}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r},

(4.102)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nu(r)-\mathring{\nu}(r)\right\rvert\leq\mathsf{g}w_{1}r^{2}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1},

(4.103)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\chi(r)-\mathring{\chi}(r)\right\rvert\leq\mathsf{g}w_{2}r^{4}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-2}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r},

(4.104)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\chi^{\prime}(r)-{\mathring{\chi}}^{\prime}(r)\right\rvert\leq\mathsf{g}w_{3}\,r^{3}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-2}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r},

where
(\mathring{\nu},\mathring{\chi})
is the solution corresponding to parameters
(0,\mathsf{h})
. Furthermore, if
0\leq\mathsf{h}<1
and
\varepsilon=1/2
, the constants mentioned above can be taken as

(4.105)

\begin{array}[]{|l||c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr&\mathsf{g}_{0}&W_{1}&W_{2}&W_{3}&W_{4}&w_{1}&w_{2}&w_{3}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr\mathsf{h}=0&2\cdot 10^{-4}&3.04&10.3&1.07&2.50&24.3&4950&2230\\
\hline\cr\mathsf{h}\in(0,1)&1.17\cdot 10^{-17}&504&1120&9.00&12.4&4.63\cdot 10^{5}&8.62\cdot 10^{16}&8.97\cdot 10^{15}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{h}\geq 0
and
0<\varepsilon\leq 1/2
be fixed. We first define
\mathsf{g}_{0}
without giving any indication of why it will work; this will become clear later in the proof. Let
K_{1},\ldots,K_{14}>0
be the constants from Lemma
4.4
(some of which depend on
\mathsf{h}
and/or
\varepsilon
) and
X_{0},\ldots,X_{5}>0
the constants from Theorem
4.5
(all of which depend on
\mathsf{h}
). Let also

(4.106)

Y:=\sup_{r>0}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(2+2\mathsf{h}+2\varepsilon)^{1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{\mathsf{h}-\varepsilon-1/2}&\text{if }\mathsf{h}\leq 1/2+\varepsilon,\\
(1+4\mathsf{h})^{1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}&\text{if }\mathsf{h}\geq 1/2+\varepsilon.\end{array}\right.

Next, choose arbitrary numbers
Z_{1},Z_{2},Z_{3}>0
under the constraint

(4.107)

Z_{1}\leq\frac{1}{2},

define/choose constants
W_{j}
and
weights

w_{j}
such that

(4.108)

W_{1}:=1+X_{1}Y+\dfrac{YZ_{2}}{16},\quad W_{2}:=X_{3}+\dfrac{YZ_{2}}{2},\quad W_{3}:=X_{1}+\dfrac{Z_{2}}{16},\quad W_{4}:=X_{2}+\dfrac{Z_{3}}{2},

(4.109)

w_{1}\geq\frac{K_{3}W_{1}W_{2}}{2}+\frac{3K_{3}W_{4}^{2}Y^{2}}{8},

(4.110)

W_{5}:=4W_{1}W_{2}W_{4}+8w_{1}(2W_{3}+W_{4}),

(4.111)

w_{2}\geq\dfrac{K_{4}W_{5}X_{1}X_{4}}{4}+\dfrac{K_{9}W_{5}X_{1}X_{4}}{2},\quad w_{3}\geq\dfrac{K_{4}W_{5}X_{2}X_{4}}{32}+\frac{K_{9}W_{5}X_{1}X_{5}}{2},

and let constants
T_{j},U_{j},V_{j},T,U,V
be given by

\begin{array}[]{ll}T_{1}:=\dfrac{K_{2}W_{4}^{2}}{4},&T:=16W_{1}W_{2}W_{4}+16(2w_{1}+T_{1})(2W_{3}+W_{4}),\\
\\
U_{1}:=\dfrac{K_{2}W_{3}}{2},&U:=8W_{3}W_{4}Y^{2}+2w_{1}+16U_{1}(2W_{3}+W_{4}),\\
\\
V_{1}:=\dfrac{3K_{2}W_{4}}{8},&V:=4W_{1}W_{2}+8w_{1}+16V_{1}(2W_{3}+W_{4}),\\
\\
T_{2}:=TX_{1}X_{4}\left(\dfrac{K_{5}}{8}+\dfrac{K_{10}}{2}\right),&T_{3}:=T\left(\dfrac{K_{5}X_{2}X_{4}}{16}+K_{10}X_{1}X_{5}\right),\\
\\
U_{2}:=UX_{1}X_{4}\left(\dfrac{K_{5}}{8}+\dfrac{K_{10}}{2}\right),&U_{3}:=U\left(\dfrac{K_{5}X_{2}X_{4}}{16}+K_{10}X_{1}X_{5}\right),\\
\\
V_{2}:=VX_{1}X_{4}\left(\dfrac{K_{5}}{8}+\dfrac{K_{10}}{2}\right),&V_{3}:=V\left(\dfrac{K_{5}X_{2}X_{4}}{16}+K_{10}X_{1}X_{5}\right).\end{array}

Finally, select also a real number

(4.112)

0<L<1.

We choose
\mathsf{g}_{0}>0
so as to satisfy

(4.113)

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\min\left\{\,\frac{Z_{1}}{w_{1}},\,\frac{Z_{2}}{w_{2}},\,\frac{Z_{3}}{w_{3}}\,\right\}\text{ and}

(4.114)

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{L}{3}\min\left\{\,\frac{1}{T_{1}}\,,\frac{w_{1}}{U_{1}w_{2}}\,,\frac{w_{1}}{V_{1}w_{3}}\,,\frac{w_{2}}{T_{2}w_{1}}\,,\frac{1}{U_{2}}\,,\frac{w_{2}}{V_{2}w_{3}}\,,\frac{w_{3}}{T_{3}w_{1}}\,,\frac{w_{3}}{U_{3}w_{2}}\,,\frac{1}{V_{3}}\,\right\}.

When
\varepsilon=1/2
and
0\leq\mathsf{h}<1
, the explicit values claimed in the theorem statement for the
W_{j}
constants come from the following choices, which respect all the restrictions imposed above and have been brute-forced by computer to be close to optimal, in the sense that they yield nearly as large a
\mathsf{g}_{0}
as possible:

Z_{1}=\frac{1}{2},\quad Z_{2}=Z_{3}=1,\quad L=0.999,\quad w_{1},w_{2},w_{3}\text{ as in~{{(\ignorespaces\ref{numerical_W}\/)}}}.

Now let
0<\mathsf{g}<\mathsf{g}_{0}
be fixed and let us find a solution to mass/deviation problem. We define new unknowns
(\xi,\psi)
by the relations

(4.115)

\nu(r)=\mathring{\nu}(r)+\sf\mathsf{g}\xi(r),\quad\chi(r)=\mathring{\chi}(r)+\sf\mathsf{g}\psi(r),

where

(4.116)

\mathring{\nu}(r)=-\mathsf{h},\quad\mathring{\chi}(r)=\frac{1}{2\mathsf{h}\,U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})}\int_{r}^{\infty}t^{2}U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h}+2t)e^{-t}\ \mathrm{d}t

is the solution corresponding to the parameters
(0,\mathsf{h})
found in the previous subsection. In order for
\nu
and
\chi
to satisfy the boundary conditions listed in (
4.42
), we will require null conditions

(4.117)

\xi(0)=0,\quad\psi(0)=0,\quad\psi(\infty)=0,

since
\mathring{\nu}
and
\mathring{\xi}
already satisfy (
4.42
). Plugging (
4.115
) into the
\nu
and
\chi
equations from (
4.41
), then simplifying out the terms corresponding to the
\mathsf{g}=0
equations, we arrive at the following equations for
\xi
and
\psi
:

(4.118)

\xi^{\prime}=\frac{(1-\mathring{\chi}-\mathsf{g}\psi)(1+\mathring{\chi}+\mathsf{g}\psi)}{2r^{2}}-\frac{(r+2\mathsf{h}-2\mathsf{g}\xi)(\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}+\mathsf{g}\psi^{\prime})^{2}}{2r^{3}},

(4.119)

\psi^{\prime\prime}=\frac{2(r+2\mathsf{h})}{r(r+3\mathsf{h})}\psi^{\prime}+\frac{r}{r+2\mathsf{h}}\psi+\frac{(1+\mathring{\chi}+\mathsf{g}\psi)(1-\mathring{\chi}-\mathsf{g}\psi)(\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}+\mathsf{g}\psi^{\prime})}{r^{2}(r+2\mathsf{h}-2\mathsf{g}\xi)}+\frac{2\xi\big(r(\mathring{\chi}+\mathsf{g}\psi)-(\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}+\mathsf{g}\psi^{\prime})\big)}{(r+2\mathsf{h})(r+2\mathsf{h}-2\mathsf{g}\xi)}.

Taking advantage of the smallness of
\mathsf{g}
, we will obtain a solution
(\xi,\psi,\omega)
(where
\omega:=\psi^{\prime}
) to the boundary-value problem composed of (
4.118
) , (
4.119
) and (
4.117
) by following a procedure that is standard in problems involving a small parameter: We will look for a fixed point, in an appropriate complete metric space
\mathcal{B}
, of the map

(4.120)

\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{B}\longrightarrow\mathcal{B},\quad(\xi,\psi,\omega)\longmapsto(\Xi,\Psi,\Omega),

that is defined by letting the functions
(\Xi,\Psi,\Omega)
solve (
4.118
) , (
4.119
) , (
4.117
) and
\Psi^{\prime}=\Omega
, but with
(\xi,\psi,\omega)
plugged into all the terms that come accompanied by
\mathsf{g}
in the equations. That is, given a “guess”
(\xi,\psi,\omega)
, we let
(\Xi,\Psi,\Omega)
solve

(4.121)

\Xi^{\prime}=\frac{(1-\mathring{\chi}-\mathsf{g}\psi)(1+\mathring{\chi}+\mathsf{g}\psi)}{2r^{2}}-\frac{(r+2\mathsf{h}-2\mathsf{g}\xi)(\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}+\mathsf{g}\psi^{\prime})^{2}}{2r^{3}},

(4.122)

\Psi^{\prime\prime}=\frac{2(r+2\mathsf{h})}{r(r+3\mathsf{h})}\Psi^{\prime}+\frac{r}{r+2\mathsf{h}}\Psi+\frac{(1+\mathring{\chi}+\mathsf{g}\psi)(1-\mathring{\chi}-\mathsf{g}\psi)(\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}+\mathsf{g}\omega)}{r^{2}(r+2\mathsf{h}-2\mathsf{g}\xi)}+\frac{2\Xi\big(r(\mathring{\chi}+\mathsf{g}\psi)-(\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}+\mathsf{g}\omega)\big)}{(r+2\mathsf{h})(r+2\mathsf{h}-2\mathsf{g}\xi)},

\Omega=\Psi^{\prime}

with
\Xi(0)=\Psi(0)=\Psi(\infty)=0
. This solution is easy to write out: The unknown
\Xi
is immediately found by integrating the right side of (
4.121
) — which does not include any
\Xi
,
\Psi
or
\Omega
, — while
\Psi
and
\Omega
are given by the formulas (
4.95
) and (
4.97
) in Corollary
4.6
, whose prerequisites will be checked to be met:

(4.123)

\displaystyle\Xi(r)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{r}\left(\frac{(1+\mathring{\chi}+\mathsf{g}\psi)(1-\mathring{\chi}-\mathsf{g}\psi)}{2s^{2}}-\frac{(s+2\mathsf{h}-2\mathsf{g}\xi)(\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}+\mathsf{g}\psi^{\prime})^{2}}{2s^{3}}\right)\ \mathrm{d}s,

(4.124)

\displaystyle\Psi(r)

\displaystyle:=-\mathring{\chi}(r)\int_{0}^{r}\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\mathring{\upsilon}H\ \mathrm{d}s-\mathring{\upsilon}(r)\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\mathring{\chi}H\ \mathrm{d}s,

(4.125)

\displaystyle\Omega(r)

\displaystyle:=-\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}(r)\int_{0}^{r}\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\mathring{\upsilon}H\ \mathrm{d}s-\mathring{\upsilon}^{\prime}(r)\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\mathring{\chi}H\ \mathrm{d}s,

where
\mathring{\upsilon}
is the conjugate of
\mathring{\chi}
(see (
4.74
)) and we abbreviated as
H=H(s)=H(\mathring{\chi},\mathring{\chi}^{\prime},\xi,\psi,\omega;s)
the inhomogeneous term

(4.126)

H(s):=\frac{(1+\mathring{\chi}+\mathsf{g}\psi)(1-\mathring{\chi}-\mathsf{g}\psi)(\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}+\mathsf{g}\omega)}{s^{2}(s+2\mathsf{h}-2\mathsf{g}\xi)}+\frac{2\Xi\big(s(\mathring{\chi}+\mathsf{g}\psi)-(\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}+\mathsf{g}\omega)\big)}{(s+2\mathsf{h})(s+2\mathsf{h}-2\mathsf{g}\xi)}

of the ODE (
4.122
). The argument
s
where all functions on the right sides of (
4.123
), (
4.124
), (
4.125
) and (
4.126
) are evaluated has been omitted to save space. Then it is clear that, if an ordered triple of continuous functions
(\xi,\psi,\omega):(0,\infty)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a fixed point of
\mathcal{F}
in
\mathcal{B}
, then we have
\xi\in C^{1}\big((0,\infty)\big)
,
\psi\in C^{2}\big((0,\infty)\big)
,
\omega=\psi^{\prime}
and the pair
(\xi,\psi)
solves the differential equations (
4.121
) and (
4.122
). The existence of a fixed point will be proved via the Banach Fixed-Point Theorem, while the desired boundary conditions (
4.117
) for
\xi
and
\psi
will follow automatically from the properties of the metric space
\mathcal{B}
, to be defined shortly. With this fixed point, the functions
(\nu,\chi)
given by (
4.115
) will be the solution for the problem in the theorem as we wanted, with the desired inequalities (
4.98
)–(
4.104
) following as collateral damage of the proof.

We now give the definition of
\mathcal{B}
. For the constant
\varepsilon
that has already been fixed since the start, consider the following weighted
L^{\infty}
norms defined for appropriate functions
f:(0,\infty)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
:

(4.127)

\displaystyle\lVert f\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{r>0}r^{-2}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)\left\lvert f(r)\right\rvert,

(4.128)

\displaystyle\lVert f\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{r>0}r^{-4}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{2}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{1-\mathsf{h}-\varepsilon}e^{r}\left\lvert f(r)\right\rvert,

(4.129)

\displaystyle\lVert f\rVert_{3}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{r>0}r^{-3}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{2}(1+4\mathsf{h}+2r)^{-{\mathsf{h}}-\varepsilon}e^{r}\left\lvert f(r)\right\rvert.

Then, for the weights
w_{j}
chosen in (
4.109
) and (
4.111
), construct the following norm for ordered triples of such functions:

(4.130)

\lVert(\xi,\psi,\omega)\rVert:=\max\left\{\frac{\lVert\xi\rVert_{1}}{w_{1}},\frac{\lVert\psi\rVert_{2}}{w_{2}},\frac{\lVert\omega\rVert_{3}}{w_{3}}\right\}.

We define the Banach space

\mathcal{X}:=\big\{(\xi,\psi,\omega):(0,\infty)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}:\ \xi,\psi,\omega\text{ are measurable functions and }\lVert(\xi,\psi,\omega)\rVert<\infty\big\}

and the closed subset
\mathcal{B}:=\big\{(\xi,\psi,\omega)\in\mathcal{X}:\ \lVert(\xi,\psi,\omega)\rVert\leq 1\}
. What is left now is to prove that the map
\mathcal{F}
satisfies
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{B})\subseteq\mathcal{B}
and is a strict contraction in the sense of the statement of the Banach Fixed-Point Theorem. Proving these claims only requires lengthy but elementary estimates; the non-trivial work has already been completed in Lemma
4.4
and in the choice of the norms
\lVert\ast\rVert_{j}
, which have been carefully crafted (especially with the presence of the exponent
-\varepsilon<0
) to make everything work. From now on, as in Lemma
4.4
, we use the following notation for brevity:

(4.131)

p(r):=4\mathsf{h}+2r,\quad q(r):=1+4\mathsf{h}+2r,

and we may omit the argument
(r)
from
p(r)
and
q(r)
when it is clear from context (it will always be either
r
or
s
). Also note, for repeated future use, that

(4.132)

rp(r)^{-1}\leq\frac{1}{2},\quad rq(r)^{-1}\leq\frac{1}{2},\quad r+2\mathsf{h}=\frac{1}{2}p(r),\quad\frac{1}{r+2\mathsf{h}}=2p(r)^{-1}.

Claim 1:

\mathcal{F}
as given by (
4.120
), (
4.123
), (
4.124
) and (
4.125
) maps
\mathcal{B}
into itself.

Proof:
Let
(\xi,\psi,\omega)\in\mathcal{B}
; in particular, from the definitions (
4.127
), (
4.128
) and (
4.129
) of the
\lVert\ast\rVert_{j}
norms, we have

(4.133)

\left\lvert\xi(r)\right\rvert\leq w_{1}\,r^{2}p^{-1}q^{-1},\quad\left\lvert\psi(r)\right\rvert\leq w_{2}\,r^{4}p^{-2}q^{-1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r},\quad\left\lvert\omega(r)\right\rvert\leq w_{3}\,r^{3}p^{-2}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}.

Note that the inequalities (
4.102
)–(
4.104
) claimed in the theorem will follow from here once the existence of the fixed point is shown, because the solution
(\mathring{\nu}+\mathsf{g}\xi,\mathring{\chi}+\mathsf{g}\psi)
of the mass/deviation problem will be such that
(\xi,\psi,\psi^{\prime})\in\mathcal{B}
.

Next we derive bounds for some recurring expressions that appear in the formulas for
\Xi
,
\Psi
and
\Omega
. First, using
q\geq 1
and
\mathsf{g}w_{1}\leq Z_{1}\leq 1/2
(see (
4.113
) and (
4.107
)), we prove

\mathsf{g}\left\lvert\xi(r)\right\rvert\leq\mathsf{g}w_{1}\,r^{2}p^{-1}q^{-1}\leq Z_{1}\,r^{2}p^{-1}\leq\frac{1}{2}(2\mathsf{h}+r)^{2}p^{-1}=\frac{p}{8},

which implies

(4.134)

r+2\mathsf{h}-2\mathsf{g}\xi(r)=\frac{p}{2}-2\mathsf{g}\xi(r)\geq\frac{p}{4}>0\quad\text{for all }r>0

and also yields the following useful bounds:

(4.135)

r+2\mathsf{h}-2\mathsf{g}\xi(r)\leq r+2\mathsf{h}+2\mathsf{g}\left\lvert\xi(r)\right\rvert\leq\frac{p}{2}+\frac{2p}{8}=\frac{3p}{4},\quad\text{and }\frac{1}{r+2\mathsf{h}-2\mathsf{g}\xi(r)}\leq 4p^{-1}.

Using the estimates (
4.69
), (
4.70
) and (
4.71
) for
\mathring{\chi}
,
1-\mathring{\chi}
and
\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}
, as well as the definition (
4.106
) of the constant
Y
and the restrictions
\mathsf{g}w_{j}\leq Z_{j}
from (
4.113
), we also obtain:

(4.136)

\displaystyle\left\lvert 1+\mathring{\chi}(r)+\mathsf{g}\psi(r)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 1+X_{1}\,q^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}+\mathsf{g}w_{2}\,r^{4}p^{-2}q^{-1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}

\displaystyle\leq 1+q^{1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}\big(X_{1}\,q^{-\varepsilon}+\mathsf{g}w_{2}\,r^{4}p^{-2}q^{-2}\big)

\displaystyle\leq 1+Y\left(X_{1}+\frac{Z_{2}}{16}\right)

\displaystyle=W_{1},

(4.137)

\displaystyle\left\lvert 1-\mathring{\chi}(r)-\mathsf{g}\psi(r)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert 1-\mathring{\chi}(r)\right\rvert+g\left\lvert\psi(r)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq X_{3}\,r^{3}p^{-1}q^{-2}+\mathsf{g}w_{2}\,r^{4}p^{-2}q^{-1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}

\displaystyle\leq r^{3}p^{-1}q^{-2}\big(X_{3}+\mathsf{g}w_{2}\,rp^{-1}q^{1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}\big)

\displaystyle\leq r^{3}p^{-1}q^{-2}\left(X_{3}+\frac{Z_{2}Y}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=W_{2}\,r^{3}p(r)^{-1}q(r)^{-2},

(4.138)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathring{\chi}(r)+\mathsf{g}\psi(r)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq X_{1}\,q^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}+\mathsf{g}w_{2}\,r^{4}p^{-2}q^{-1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}

\displaystyle\leq q^{1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}\big(X_{1}\,q^{-\varepsilon}+\mathsf{g}w_{2}\,r^{4}p^{-2}q^{-2}\big)

\displaystyle\leq q^{1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}\left(X_{1}+\frac{Z_{2}}{16}\right)

\displaystyle=W_{3}\,q(r)^{1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r},

(4.139)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}(r)+\mathsf{g}\omega(r)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq X_{2}\,r^{2}p^{-1}q^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}+\mathsf{g}w_{3}\,r^{3}p^{-2}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}

\displaystyle\leq r^{2}p^{-1}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}\big(X_{2}\,q^{-\varepsilon}+\mathsf{g}w_{3}\,rp^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle\leq r^{2}p^{-1}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}\left(X_{2}+\frac{Z_{3}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=W_{4}\,r^{2}p(r)^{-1}q(r)^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r},

which directly yield the rest of the desired inequalities (
4.98
)–(
4.101
) once we establish that the solution
(\xi,\psi,\omega)
does indeed exist. Now we are ready to estimate
\Xi
,
\Psi
and
\Omega
. The goal is to prove
\lVert(\Xi,\Psi,\Omega)\rVert\leq 1
, which, according to (
4.130
), is achieved by showing
\lVert\Xi\rVert_{1}\leq w_{1}
,
\lVert\Psi\rVert_{2}\leq w_{2}
and
\lVert\Omega\rVert_{3}\leq w_{3}
. Lemma (
4.4
) and the simple relations (
4.132
) will also be needed throughout this process. We have, for all
r>0
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Xi(r)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{r}\left(\frac{\left\lvert 1+\mathring{\chi}+\mathsf{g}\psi\right\rvert\,\left\lvert 1-\mathring{\chi}-\mathsf{g}\psi\right\rvert}{2s^{2}}+\frac{\left\lvert s+2\mathsf{h}-2\mathsf{g}\xi\right\rvert\,\left\lvert\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}+\mathsf{g}\omega\right\rvert^{2}}{2s^{3}}\right)\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{r}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}s^{-2}W_{1}W_{2}\,s^{3}p^{-1}q^{-2}+\frac{1}{2}s^{-3}\frac{3p}{4}\big(W_{4}\,s^{2}p^{-1}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}\big)^{2}\bigg)\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{r}\bigg(\frac{W_{1}W_{2}}{2}sp^{-1}q^{-2}+\frac{3W_{4}^{2}}{8}\,sp^{-1}q^{2\mathsf{h}+2\varepsilon}e^{-2s}\bigg)\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{r}\bigg(\frac{W_{1}W_{2}}{2}+\frac{3W_{4}^{2}}{8}\,q^{2+2\mathsf{h}+2\varepsilon}e^{-2s}\bigg)sp^{-1}q^{-2}\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{W_{1}W_{2}}{2}+\frac{3W_{4}^{2}Y^{2}}{8}\right)\int_{0}^{r}sp(s)^{-1}q(s)^{-2}\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{W_{1}W_{2}}{2}+\frac{3W_{4}^{2}Y^{2}}{8}\right)K_{3}\,r^{2}p(r)^{-1}q(r)^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq w_{1}\,r^{2}p(r)^{-1}q(r)^{-1},

where we used the condition (
4.109
) on
w_{1}
in the end. Given the definition (
4.127
) of
\lVert\ast\rVert_{1}
, this proves
\lVert\Xi\rVert_{1}\leq w_{1}
. Next we need a bound for the expression
H
defined in (
4.126
), for which we will also use the freshly proved bound for
\left\lvert\Xi\right\rvert
(note that
H
includes a
\Xi
term in it):

\displaystyle\left\lvert H(s)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left\lvert 1+\mathring{\chi}+\mathsf{g}\psi\right\rvert\,\left\lvert 1-\mathring{\chi}-\mathsf{g}\psi\right\rvert\,\left\lvert\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}+\mathsf{g}\omega\right\rvert}{s^{2}(s+2\mathsf{h}-2\mathsf{g}\xi)}+\frac{2\left\lvert\Xi\right\rvert\,\big(s\left\lvert\mathring{\chi}+\mathsf{g}\psi\right\rvert+\left\lvert\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}+\mathsf{g}\omega\right\rvert\big)}{(s+2\mathsf{h})(s+2\mathsf{h}-2\mathsf{g}\xi)}

\displaystyle\leq s^{-2}4p^{-1}W_{1}W_{2}\,s^{3}p^{-1}q^{-2}W_{4}\,s^{2}p^{-1}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}

\displaystyle\qquad+2\cdot 2p^{-1}4p^{-1}w_{1}\,s^{2}p^{-1}q^{-1}\big(sW_{3}\,q^{1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}+W_{4}\,s^{2}p^{-1}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}\big)

\displaystyle=4W_{1}W_{2}W_{4}\,s^{3}p^{-3}q^{-2+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}+16w_{1}\,s^{3}p^{-3}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}\big(W_{3}+W_{4}\,sp^{-1}q^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle\leq 4W_{1}W_{2}W_{4}\,s^{3}p^{-3}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}+16w_{1}\,s^{3}p^{-3}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}\left(W_{3}+\frac{W_{4}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=W_{5}\,s^{3}p(s)^{-3}q(s)^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}.

The conditions of Corollary
4.6
on the exponents
x=3
,
y=-3
and
z=\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon
that we just obtained here are satisfied; this is why the small
\varepsilon>0
was introduced in our definition of the norms, otherwise
x+y+z
would have been equal to
\mathsf{h}
and the corollary would not have been applicable. Then formula (
4.95
) does indeed give a zero-boundary-value solution to the
\Psi
equation (
4.124
), and, using the above bound for
H
and the estimates (
4.69
) and (
4.72
) for
\mathring{\chi}
and
\mathring{\upsilon}
, we obtain the following bounds on the integrals that appear in the formulas (
4.124
) and (
4.125
) for
\Psi
and
\Omega
:

(4.140)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{0}^{r}\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\mathring{\upsilon}(s)H(s)\ \mathrm{d}s\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{r}s^{-3}\frac{1}{2}pX_{4}\,s^{4}p^{-1}q^{-2-{\mathsf{h}}}e^{s}W_{5}\,s^{3}p^{-3}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\frac{X_{4}W_{5}}{2}\int_{0}^{r}s^{4}p(s)^{-3}q(s)^{-2+\varepsilon}\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{X_{4}W_{5}K_{4}}{2}r^{5}p(r)^{-3}q(r)^{-2+\varepsilon},

(4.141)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\mathring{\chi}(s)H(s)\ \mathrm{d}s\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\int_{r}^{\infty}s^{-3}\frac{1}{2}pX_{1}\,q^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-s}W_{5}\,s^{3}p^{-3}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\frac{X_{1}W_{5}}{2}\int_{r}^{\infty}p(s)^{-2}q(s)^{1+2\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-2s}\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{X_{1}W_{5}K_{9}}{2}p(r)^{-1}q(r)^{2\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-2r}.

Therefore

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Psi(r)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\mathring{\chi}(r)\right\rvert\left\lvert\int_{0}^{r}\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\mathring{\upsilon}(s)H(s)\ \mathrm{d}s\right\rvert+\left\lvert\mathring{\upsilon}(r)\right\rvert\left\lvert\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\mathring{\chi}(s)H(s)\ \mathrm{d}s\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq X_{1}\,q^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}\frac{X_{4}W_{5}K_{4}}{2}r^{5}p^{-3}q^{-2+\varepsilon}+X_{4}\,r^{4}p^{-1}q^{-2-{\mathsf{h}}}e^{r}\frac{X_{1}W_{5}K_{9}}{2}p^{-1}q^{2\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-2r}

\displaystyle=\frac{X_{1}X_{4}W_{5}}{2}\big(K_{4}r^{5}p^{-3}q^{-1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}+K_{9}r^{4}p^{-2}q^{-2+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{X_{1}X_{4}W_{5}}{2}r^{4}p^{-2}q^{-1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}\big(K_{4}r\,p^{-1}+K_{9}q^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{X_{1}X_{4}W_{5}}{2}r^{4}p^{-2}q^{-1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}\left(\frac{K_{4}}{2}+K_{9}\right)

\displaystyle\leq w_{2}\,r^{4}p(r)^{-2}q(r)^{-1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r},

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Omega(r)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}(r)\right\rvert\left\lvert\int_{0}^{r}\frac{(s+2\mathsf{h})\mathring{\upsilon}(s)H(s)}{s^{3}}\ \mathrm{d}s\right\rvert+\left\lvert\mathring{\upsilon}^{\prime}(r)\right\rvert\left\lvert\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{(s+2\mathsf{h})\mathring{\chi}(s)H(s)}{s^{3}}\ \mathrm{d}s\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq X_{2}\,r^{2}p^{-1}q^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}\frac{X_{4}W_{5}K_{4}}{2}r^{5}p^{-3}q^{-2+\varepsilon}+X_{5}\,r^{3}p^{-1}q^{-1-\mathsf{h}}e^{r}\frac{X_{1}W_{5}K_{9}}{2}p^{-1}q^{2\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-2r}

\displaystyle=\frac{W_{5}}{2}\big(X_{2}X_{4}K_{4}r^{7}p^{-4}q^{-2+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}+X_{1}X_{5}K_{9}r^{3}p^{-2}q^{-1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{W_{5}}{2}r^{3}p^{-2}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}\big(X_{2}X_{4}K_{4}r^{4}p^{-2}q^{-2}+X_{1}X_{5}K_{9}q^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{W_{5}}{2}r^{3}p^{-2}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}\left(\frac{X_{2}X_{4}K_{4}}{16}+X_{1}X_{5}K_{9}\right)

\displaystyle\leq w_{3}\,r^{3}p(r)^{-2}q(r)^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r},

where we used the conditions (
4.111
) on
w_{2}
and
w_{3}
. From here, using the definitions (
4.128
) and (
4.129
) of the norms
\lVert\ast\rVert_{2}
and
\lVert\ast\rVert_{3}
, it is immediate that
\lVert\Psi\rVert_{2}\leq w_{2}
and
\lVert\Omega\rVert_{3}\leq w_{3}
.

Claim 2:

\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{B}\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}
is a strict contraction with Lipschitz constant
L
.

Proof:
Recall that
L
was chosen in (
4.112
) to be smaller than 1. Let
(\xi,\psi,\omega),(\overline{\xi},\overline{\psi},\overline{\omega})\in\mathcal{B}
be given. We must prove

(4.142)

\lVert(\Xi-\overline{\Xi},\Psi-\overline{\Psi},\Omega-\overline{\Omega})\rVert\leq L\lVert(\xi-\overline{\xi},\psi-\overline{\psi},\omega-\overline{\omega})\rVert

for
(\Xi,\Psi,\Omega)=\mathcal{F}(\xi,\psi,\omega)
and
(\overline{\Xi},\overline{\Psi},\overline{\Omega})=\mathcal{F}(\overline{\xi},\overline{\psi},\overline{\omega})
— we cannot resist the urge to use the cumbersome symbol
\overline{\Xi}
. To show (
4.142
), it is enough to prove

(4.143)

\displaystyle\lVert\Xi-\overline{\Xi}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{g}T_{1}\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1}+\mathsf{g}U_{1}\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2}+\mathsf{g}V_{1}\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3},

(4.144)

\displaystyle\lVert\Psi-\overline{\Psi}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{g}T_{2}\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1}+\mathsf{g}U_{2}\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2}+\mathsf{g}V_{2}\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3},

(4.145)

\displaystyle\lVert\Omega-\overline{\Omega}\rVert_{3}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{g}T_{3}\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1}+\mathsf{g}U_{3}\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2}+\mathsf{g}V_{3}\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3},

since then it follows immediately from the condition (
4.114
) on
\mathsf{g}_{0}
that

\displaystyle\frac{\lVert\Xi-\overline{\Xi}\rVert_{1}}{w_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{L}{3}\left(\frac{\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1}}{w_{1}}+\frac{\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2}}{w_{2}}+\frac{\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3}}{w_{3}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq L\,\max\left\{\frac{\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1}}{w_{1}},\frac{\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2}}{w_{2}},\frac{\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3}}{w_{3}}\right\}

\displaystyle=L\lVert(\xi-\overline{\xi},\psi-\overline{\psi},\omega-\overline{\omega})\rVert

and similarly for
\lVert\Psi-\overline{\Psi}\rVert_{2}/w_{2}
and
\lVert\Omega-\overline{\Omega}\rVert_{3}/w_{3}
, proving (
4.142
).

So let us show (
4.143
) first. Let the integrand of (
4.123
) be viewed as a function
F(\mathring{\chi},\mathring{\chi}^{\prime},\xi,\psi,\omega;s)
for each fixed value of
s
:

\Xi(r)=\int_{0}^{r}F\big(\mathring{\chi}(s),\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}(s),\xi(s),\psi(s),\omega(s);s\big)\ \mathrm{d}s,\\
F(u,v,x,y,z;s):=\frac{(1+u+\mathsf{g}y)(1-u-\mathsf{g}y)}{2s^{2}}-\frac{(s+2\mathsf{h}-2\mathsf{g}x)(v+\mathsf{g}z)^{2}}{2s^{3}}.

The Mean-Value Theorem gives, for arbitrary arguments
(u,v,x,y,z;s)
and
(u,v,\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z};s)
,

(4.146)

\left\lvert F(u,v,x,y,z;s)-F(u,v,\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z};s)\right\rvert\leq\sup_{x,y,z}\left|\frac{\partial F}{\partial x}\right|\left\lvert x-\overline{x}\right\rvert+\sup_{x,y,z}\left|\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}\right|\left\lvert y-\overline{y}\right\rvert+\sup_{x,y,z}\left|\frac{\partial F}{\partial z}\right|\left\lvert z-\overline{z}\right\rvert.

These partial derivatives are quickly computed as

\frac{\partial F}{\partial x}=\frac{\mathsf{g}(v+\mathsf{g}z)^{2}}{s^{3}},\quad\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}=-\frac{\mathsf{g}(u+\mathsf{g}y)}{s^{2}},\quad\frac{\partial F}{\partial z}=-\frac{\mathsf{g}(s+2\mathsf{h}-2\mathsf{g}x)(v+\mathsf{g}z)}{s^{3}},

and, when evaluated at the arguments
\big(\mathring{\chi}(s),\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}(s),\xi(s),\psi(s),\omega(s);s\big)
, are bounded by

\displaystyle\sup_{(\xi,\psi,\omega)\in\mathcal{B}}\left|\frac{\partial F}{\partial x}\right|

\displaystyle=\mathsf{g}s^{-3}\sup_{(\xi,\psi,\omega)\in\mathcal{B}}\left\lvert\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}+\mathsf{g}\omega\right\rvert^{2}\leq\mathsf{g}W_{4}^{2}\,sp^{-2}q^{2\mathsf{h}+2\varepsilon}e^{-2s},

\displaystyle\sup_{(\xi,\psi,\omega)\in\mathcal{B}}\left|\frac{\partial F}{\partial y}\right|

\displaystyle=\mathsf{g}s^{-2}\,\sup_{(\xi,\psi,\omega)\in\mathcal{B}}\left\lvert\mathring{\chi}+\mathsf{g}\psi\right\rvert\leq\mathsf{g}W_{3}\,s^{-2}q^{1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s},

\displaystyle\sup_{(\xi,\psi,\omega)\in\mathcal{B}}\left|\frac{\partial F}{\partial z}\right|

\displaystyle=\mathsf{g}s^{-3}\,\sup_{(\xi,\psi,\omega)\in\mathcal{B}}\left\lvert s+2\mathsf{h}-2\mathsf{g}\xi\right\rvert\,\left\lvert\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}+\mathsf{g}\omega\right\rvert\leq\frac{3\mathsf{g}W_{4}}{4}s^{-1}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}.

The presence of
\mathsf{g}
in each of these bounds is what will grant us the contraction property. The other terms in (
4.146
) are bounded using the definition of the norms
\lVert\ast\rVert_{j}
:

(4.147)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\xi(s)-\overline{\xi}(s)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq s^{2}p(s)^{-1}q(s)^{-1}\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1},

(4.148)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\psi(s)-\overline{\psi}(s)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq s^{4}p(s)^{-2}q(s)^{-1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2},

(4.149)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\omega(s)-\overline{\omega}(s)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq s^{3}p(s)^{-2}q(s)^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3}.

Therefore we obtain from (
4.123
) and (
4.146
)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Xi(r)-\overline{\Xi}(r)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{r}\bigg(\mathsf{g}W_{4}^{2}\,sp^{-2}q^{2\mathsf{h}+2\varepsilon}e^{-2s}s^{2}p^{-1}q^{-1}\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathsf{g}W_{3}\,s^{-2}q^{1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}s^{4}p^{-2}q^{-1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{3\mathsf{g}W_{4}}{4}s^{-1}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}s^{3}p^{-2}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3}\bigg)\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\mathsf{g}\int_{0}^{r}\bigg(W_{4}^{2}s^{3}p^{-3}q^{-1+2\mathsf{h}+2\varepsilon}e^{-2s}\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1}+W_{3}s^{2}p^{-2}q^{2\mathsf{h}+2\varepsilon}e^{-2s}\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{3W_{4}}{4}s^{2}p^{-2}q^{2\mathsf{h}+2\varepsilon}e^{-2s}\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3}\bigg)\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\mathsf{g}\int_{0}^{r}s^{2}p^{-2}q^{2\mathsf{h}+2\varepsilon}e^{-2s}\left(W_{4}^{2}sp^{-1}q^{-1}\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1}+W_{3}\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2}+\frac{3W_{4}}{4}\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3}\right)\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{g}\left(\frac{W_{4}^{2}}{2}\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1}+W_{3}\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2}+\frac{3W_{4}}{4}\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3}\right)\int_{0}^{r}s^{2}p(s)^{-2}q(s)^{2\mathsf{h}+2\varepsilon}e^{-2s}\ \mathrm{d}s

(4.150)

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{g}\left(\frac{W_{4}^{2}}{2}\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1}+W_{3}\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2}+\frac{3W_{4}}{4}\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3}\right)K_{2}r^{3}p(r)^{-2}q(r)^{-1}

which then gives, using the definition (
4.127
) of
\lVert\ast\rVert_{1}
,

\displaystyle\lVert\Xi-\overline{\Xi}\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{\mathsf{g}W_{4}^{2}K_{2}}{2}\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1}+\mathsf{g}W_{3}K_{2}\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2}+\frac{3\mathsf{g}W_{4}K_{2}}{4}\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3}\right)\sup_{r>0}rp(r)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{g}T_{1}\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1}+\mathsf{g}U_{1}\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2}+\mathsf{g}V_{1}\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3},

as claimed. To conclude the proof of claim 2, we show (
4.144
) and (
4.145
) using the same strategy, noting that formulas (
4.124
) and (
4.125
) yield

(4.151)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Psi(r)-\overline{\Psi}(r)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\mathring{\chi}(r)\right\rvert\int_{0}^{r}\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\left\lvert\mathring{\upsilon}\right\rvert\big|H-\overline{H}\big|\ \mathrm{d}s+\left\lvert\mathring{\upsilon}(r)\right\rvert\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\left\lvert\mathring{\chi}\right\rvert\big|H-\overline{H}\big|\ \mathrm{d}s,

(4.152)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Omega(r)-\overline{\Omega}(r)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}(r)\right\rvert\int_{0}^{r}\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\left\lvert\mathring{\upsilon}\right\rvert\big|H-\overline{H}\big|\ \mathrm{d}s+\left\lvert\mathring{\upsilon}^{\prime}(r)\right\rvert\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\left\lvert\mathring{\chi}\right\rvert\big|H-\overline{H}\big|\ \mathrm{d}s.

In here we will first need an estimate for

\left\lvert H-\overline{H}\right\rvert:=\left\lvert H(\mathring{\chi},\mathring{\chi}^{\prime},\xi,\psi,\omega,\Xi;s)-H(\mathring{\chi},\mathring{\chi}^{\prime},\overline{\xi},\overline{\psi},\overline{\omega},\overline{\Xi};s)\right\rvert,

where
H
, defined in (
4.126
), is now viewed as the function

H(u,v,x,y,z,w;s):=\frac{(1+u+\mathsf{g}y)(1-u-\mathsf{g}y)(v+\mathsf{g}z)}{s^{2}(s+2\mathsf{h}-2\mathsf{g}x)}+\frac{2w\big(s(u+\mathsf{g}y)-(v+\mathsf{g}z)\big)}{(s+2\mathsf{h})(s+2\mathsf{h}-2\mathsf{g}x)}.

Inequality (
4.146
) applies to
H
with an extra term added for its
w
variable:

(4.153)

\left\lvert H(u,v,x,y,z,w;s)-H(u,v,\overline{x},\overline{y},\overline{z},\overline{w};s)\right\rvert\\
\leq\sup_{x,y,z,w}\left|\frac{\partial H}{\partial x}\right|\left\lvert x-\overline{x}\right\rvert+\sup_{x,y,z,w}\left|\frac{\partial H}{\partial y}\right|\left\lvert y-\overline{y}\right\rvert+\sup_{x,y,z,w}\left|\frac{\partial H}{\partial z}\right|\left\lvert z-\overline{z}\right\rvert+\sup_{x,y,z,w}\left|\frac{\partial H}{\partial w}\right|\left\lvert w-\overline{w}\right\rvert.

When evaluated at the arguments
\big(\mathring{\chi}(s),\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}(s),\xi(s),\psi(s),\omega(s),\Xi(s);s\big)
, the partials of
H
are checked to be bounded as follows:

\displaystyle\mathop{\sup_{(\xi,\psi,\omega)\in\mathcal{B}}}_{(\Xi,\psi,\omega)\in\mathcal{B}}\left|\frac{\partial H}{\partial x}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 32\mathsf{g}\bigg(W_{1}W_{2}W_{4}+2w_{1}(2W_{3}+W_{4})\bigg)s^{3}p(s)^{-4}q(s)^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s},

\displaystyle\mathop{\sup_{(\xi,\psi,\omega)\in\mathcal{B}}}_{(\Xi,\psi,\omega)\in\mathcal{B}}\left|\frac{\partial H}{\partial y}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathsf{g}\bigg(4W_{3}W_{4}Y^{2}+w_{1}\bigg)p(s)^{-2}q(s),

\displaystyle\mathop{\sup_{(\xi,\psi,\omega)\in\mathcal{B}}}_{(\Xi,\psi,\omega)\in\mathcal{B}}\left|\frac{\partial H}{\partial z}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 4\mathsf{g}\bigg(W_{1}W_{2}+2w_{1}\bigg)sp(s)^{-2}q(s)^{-1},

\displaystyle\mathop{\sup_{(\xi,\psi,\omega)\in\mathcal{B}}}_{(\Xi,\psi,\omega)\in\mathcal{B}}\left|\frac{\partial H}{\partial w}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 8\bigg(2W_{3}+W_{4}\bigg)sp(s)^{-2}q(s)^{1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}.

The inequalities (
4.147
), (
4.148
) and (
4.149
) for
\left\lvert\xi-\overline{\xi}\right\rvert
,
\left\lvert\psi-\overline{\psi}\right\rvert
and
\left\lvert\omega-\overline{\omega}\right\rvert
are needed again in (
4.153
), and this time we also need (
4.150
) for
\left\lvert\Xi-\overline{\Xi}\right\rvert
, which reads

\left\lvert\Xi(s)-\overline{\Xi}(s)\right\rvert\leq\mathsf{g}\bigg(2T_{1}\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1}+2U_{1}\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2}+2V_{1}\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3}\bigg)s^{3}p(s)^{-2}q(s)^{-1}.

Although
\left\lvert\partial_{w}H\right\rvert
is not bounded by a multiple of
\mathsf{g}
like the other partials of
H
, we see that the corresponding term
\left\lvert\Xi-\overline{\Xi}\right\rvert
is. Consequently, all terms in (
4.153
) are indeed small and we find

\displaystyle\left\lvert H-\overline{H}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 32\mathsf{g}\bigg(W_{1}W_{2}W_{4}+2w_{1}(2W_{3}+W_{4})\bigg)\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1}s^{5}p^{-5}q^{-1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}

\displaystyle\qquad+2\mathsf{g}\bigg(4W_{3}W_{4}Y^{2}+w_{1}\bigg)\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2}s^{4}p^{-4}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}

\displaystyle\qquad+4\mathsf{g}\bigg(W_{1}W_{2}+2w_{1}\bigg)\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3}s^{4}p^{-4}q^{-1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}

\displaystyle\qquad+16\mathsf{g}\bigg(2W_{3}+W_{4}\bigg)\bigg(T_{1}\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1}+U_{1}\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2}+V_{1}\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3}\bigg)\,s^{4}p^{-4}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{g}\bigg(T\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1}+U\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2}+V\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3}\bigg)s^{4}p(s)^{-4}q(s)^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-s}.

We can now promptly estimate the pair of integrals that appeared in (
4.151
) and (
4.152
). Analogously to (
4.140
) and (
4.141
), the result is

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r}\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\left\lvert\mathring{\upsilon}(s)\right\rvert\big|H(s)-\overline{H}(s)\big|\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{gX_{4}}{2}\big(T\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1}+U\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2}+V\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3}\big)K_{5}\,r^{6}p(r)^{-4}q(r)^{-2+\varepsilon},

\displaystyle\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{s+2\mathsf{h}}{s^{3}}\left\lvert\mathring{\chi}(s)\right\rvert\big|H(s)-\overline{H}(s)\big|\ \mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathsf{g}X_{1}}{2}\big(T\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1}+U\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2}+V\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3}\big)K_{10}\,p(r)^{-1}q(r)^{2\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-2r}.

To save space, let us temporarily write
(T\cdots)
for
T\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1}+U\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2}+V\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3}
. Then (
4.151
) and (
4.152
) become

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Psi(r)-\overline{\Psi}(r)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq X_{1}q^{1+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}\frac{\mathsf{g}X_{4}}{2}(T\cdots)K_{5}r^{6}p^{-4}q^{-2+\varepsilon}+X_{4}r^{4}p^{-1}q^{-2-{\mathsf{h}}}e^{r}\frac{\mathsf{g}X_{1}}{2}SK_{10}p^{-1}q^{2\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-2r}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathsf{g}X_{1}X_{4}(T\cdots)}{2}r^{4}p^{-2}q^{-1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}\bigg(K_{5}r^{2}p^{-2}+K_{10}q^{-1}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathsf{g}X_{1}X_{4}(T\cdots)}{2}\left(\frac{K_{5}}{4}+K_{10}\right)r^{4}p^{-2}q^{-1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\mathsf{g}T_{2}\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1}+\mathsf{g}U_{2}\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2}+\mathsf{g}V_{2}\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3}\bigg)r^{4}p^{-2}q^{-1+\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r},

\displaystyle\left\lvert\Omega(r)-\overline{\Omega}(r)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq X_{2}r^{2}p^{-1}q^{\mathsf{h}}e^{-r}\frac{\mathsf{g}X_{4}}{2}(T\cdots)K_{5}r^{6}p^{-4}q^{-2+\varepsilon}+X_{5}r^{3}p^{-1}q^{-1-\mathsf{h}}e^{r}\frac{\mathsf{g}X_{1}}{2}SK_{10}p^{-1}q^{2\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-2r}

\displaystyle=\frac{g(T\cdots)}{2}r^{3}p^{-2}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}\bigg(X_{2}X_{4}K_{5}r^{-5}p^{-3}q^{-2}+X_{1}X_{5}K_{10}q^{-1}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{g(T\cdots)}{2}\left(\frac{X_{2}X_{4}K_{5}}{32}+X_{1}X_{5}K_{10}\right)r^{3}p^{-2}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\mathsf{g}T_{3}\lVert\xi-\overline{\xi}\rVert_{1}+\mathsf{g}U_{3}\lVert\psi-\overline{\psi}\rVert_{2}+\mathsf{g}V_{3}\lVert\omega-\overline{\omega}\rVert_{3}\bigg)r^{3}p^{-2}q^{\mathsf{h}+\varepsilon}e^{-r}.

Using definitions (
4.128
) and (
4.129
) of the norms
\lVert\ast\rVert_{2}
and
\lVert\ast\rVert_{3}
, we arrive at the desired inequalities (
4.144
) and (
4.145
).
∎

4.5.
Proof of the existence theorem

Having completely solved the mass/deviation problem
4.3
, we are ready to finish proving Theorem
4.1
. The theorem initially gave us constants
G,c,\varkappa,M_{\mathrm{bare}}
, with which we construct
\mathsf{h}
as in (
4.15
). For this
\mathsf{h}
and for the value
\varepsilon=1/2
(let us not bother with a general
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2]
anymore), let
\mathsf{g}_{0}
be the threshold provided by Theorem
4.7
. Now let
Q\neq 0
be chosen as in the main theorem, that is, in such a way that
\mathsf{g}<\mathsf{g}_{0}
, for
\mathsf{g}
as in (
4.7
). Using
Q,c,\varkappa
, we define dimensionless variables as explained previously. Theorem
4.7
then gives us a solution
(\nu,\chi)
to the mass/deviation problem of parameters
(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{h})
, which, as explained in subsection
4.1
, has the consequence that the functions
\beta=r/(r-2\nu)
(see (
4.36
) and (
4.37
)) and
\chi
solve their respective differential equations in the E-M-BLTP system (
3.30
) and satisfy the conditions

\frac{1}{\mathsf{g}}\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{r}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{\beta(r)}\right)=M_{\mathrm{bare}},\quad\chi(0)=1,\quad\chi(\infty)=0.

We continue to consider the notation of the proof of Theorem
4.7
, in particular the constants
w_{j},W_{j}
, the definition (
4.115
) of
\xi
and
\psi
, as well as
\omega=\psi^{\prime}
, and the functions
p
and
q
from (
4.131
). Given that
\beta=r/(r+2\mathsf{h}-2g\xi)
, the desired positivity condition
\beta(r)>0
for all
r>0
follows from property (
4.134
). Next we integrate the
\alpha
equation in (
3.30
) to obtain the only solution for
\alpha(r)
that vanishes at infinity:

(4.154)

\alpha(r)=\mathsf{g}\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{(\chi^{\prime})^{2}}{s^{3}}\ \mathrm{d}s.

Thus a solution
(\alpha,\beta,\chi)
has been found with all desired boundary conditions. It remains to find constants
C_{1}^{\prime}
,
C_{j}
that verify the estimates (
4.19
)–(
4.26
). For any
\mathsf{h}\geq 0
, we will be able to express them in terms of the
K_{j}
from Lemma
4.4
and the
W_{j}
,
w_{j}
from Theorem
4.7
. By then using their numerical values (
4.61
), (
4.105
) for the
0\leq\mathsf{h}<1
case, one can check that the values (
4.27
) claimed in the theorem for this case are obtained.

•

Proof of (
4.19
)
. The desired inequality reads

(4.155)

\left\lvert e^{\alpha(r)}-1\right\rvert\leq\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathsf{g}C_{1}\left(e+\dfrac{e}{2r}\right)^{\delta}\ln\left(e+\dfrac{e}{2r}\right)e^{-2r}&\text{if }\mathsf{h}=0\text{ (where }0<\delta\ll 1\text{),}\\
\\
\mathsf{g}C_{1}\exp\left(\dfrac{C_{1}^{\prime}}{\mathsf{h}^{2}}\right)p(r)^{-2}q(r)^{2+2\mathsf{h}}e^{-2r}&\text{if }\mathsf{h}>0.\end{array}\right.

We start by estimating the above definition (
4.154
) of
\alpha
using the bound (
4.101
) for
\chi^{\prime}
:

(4.156)

\alpha(r)=\mathsf{g}\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{(\chi^{\prime})^{2}}{s^{3}}\ \mathrm{d}s\leq\mathsf{g}W_{4}^{2}\int_{r}^{\infty}sp(s)^{-2}q(s)^{1+2\mathsf{h}}e^{-2s}\ \mathrm{d}s.

The behavior of this integral as a function of
r
now depends on whether
\mathsf{h}=0
or
\mathsf{h}>0
. If
\mathsf{h}=0
, which implies
p(s)=2s
and
q(s)=1+2s
, we can integrate it directly with help of the
exponential-integral
special function, defined for all
s>0
as

E_{1}(s):=\int_{s}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-t}}{t}\ \mathrm{d}t

and sometimes denoted by
-\mathrm{Ei}(-s)
in the literature. We find

\alpha(r)\leq\frac{\mathsf{g}W_{4}^{2}}{4}\int_{r}^{\infty}s^{-1}(1+2s)e^{-2s}\ \mathrm{d}s=\frac{\mathsf{g}W_{4}^{2}}{4}\big(e^{-2r}+E_{1}(2r)\big).

Then the well-known inequality
E_{1}(s)\leq\ln(1 + 1/s)e^{-s}
(proof:
f(s):=E_{1}(s)-\ln(1+1/s)e^{-s}
vanishes at
s=\infty
and satisfies
f^{\prime}(s)>0
for all
s>0
) implies

\alpha(r)\leq\frac{\mathsf{g}W_{4}^{2}}{4}e^{-2r}\ln\left(e+\frac{e}{2r}\right).

In particular, using
e^{\alpha}-1\leq\alpha e^{\alpha}
, this proves the first line in (
4.155
) for the constants
C_{1}=W_{4}^{2}/4
and
\delta=\mathsf{g}W_{4}^{2}/4
. With the values
\mathsf{g}\leq\mathsf{g}_{0}=0.0002
and
W_{4}=2.50
corresponding to
\mathsf{h}=0
, we get
\delta\leq 0.0004\ll 1
. If instead
\mathsf{h}>0
, we apply Lemma
4.4
to the integral in (
4.156
) and immediately find

\alpha(r)\leq\mathsf{g}W_{4}^{2}K_{12}p(r)^{-2}q(r)^{2+2\mathsf{h}}e^{-2r}.

In particular, again using
e^{\alpha}-1\leq\alpha e^{\alpha}
, this proves

\left\lvert e^{\alpha(r)}-1\right\rvert\leq\mathsf{g}W_{4}^{2}K_{12}p(r)^{-2}q(r)^{2+2\mathsf{h}}e^{-2r}\exp\left(\mathsf{g}W_{4}^{2}K_{12}(4\mathsf{h})^{-2}(1+4\mathsf{h})^{2+2\mathsf{h}}\right),

where the argument of
\exp
is a direct upper bound for
e^{\alpha(0)}
, which bounds
e^{\alpha(r)}
for all
r>0
since
\alpha^{\prime}<0
, as is clear from the
\alpha
equation in (
3.30
). With this, the second line in (
4.155
) follows with
C_{1}=W_{4}^{2}K_{12}
and
C_{1}^{\prime}=\mathsf{g}_{0}W_{4}^{2}K_{12}4^{-2}(1+4\mathsf{h})^{2+2\mathsf{h}}
. If
0<\mathsf{h}<1
, one has to further bound the
(1+4\mathsf{h})^{2+2\mathsf{h}}
term in
C_{1}^{\prime}
by
5^{4}
before plugging in the known values of
W_{4}
,
K_{12}
and
\mathsf{g}_{0}
to obtain values for
C_{1}
and
C_{1}^{\prime}
as in the theorem.

•

Proof of (
4.23
), (
4.24
) and (
4.22
)
. The desired inequalities read

(4.157)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\varphi(r)-\mathring{\varphi}(r)\right\rvert\leq\mathsf{g}C_{5}q(r)^{-\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r},

(4.158)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\varphi(0)-2\mathring{\mathcal{E}}\right\rvert\leq\mathsf{g}C_{6},

(4.159)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\varphi(r)-\frac{1}{r}\right\rvert\leq C_{4}r^{-1}q(r)^{\frac{1}{2}+\mathsf{h}}e^{-r},

where
\mathring{\varphi}
is as defined in (
4.18
) (but now in the dimensionless convention) and the electric potential is given according to (
4.11
) by

(4.160)

\varphi(r)=\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{1-\chi}{s^{2}}\ \mathrm{d}s.

Note that
\varphi
is strictly positive and decreasing in
r
, as a consequence of
\chi<1
(see Lemma
3.3
), and thus
\varphi(0)>0
— but we note that the original, dimensionful
\varphi
from the theorem could still be negative because the sign of the constant
Q
is not specified. Also note that
\mathring{\varphi}
is the
\mathsf{g}=0
counterpart of
\varphi
:

(4.161)

\int_{r}^{\infty}\frac{1-\mathring{\chi}}{s^{2}}\ \mathrm{d}s=\frac{1}{r}-\frac{(s+2\mathsf{h})\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}(s)}{s^{3}}\bigg|_{s=r}^{\infty}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\dfrac{1}{r}-\dfrac{U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h}+2r)}{2\mathsf{h}\,U_{\mathsf{h}}(4\mathsf{h})}\left(1-\dfrac{2\mathsf{h}}{r}\right)e^{-r}&\quad\text{if }\mathsf{h}>0,\\
\\
\dfrac{1-e^{-r}}{r}&\quad\text{if }\mathsf{h}=0.\end{array}\right\}=\mathring{\varphi}(r).

In this derivation, we first integrated
1/s^{2}
and
\mathring{\chi}/s^{2}
separately, also using the
\mathring{\chi}
equation (that is, (
4.34
) with
\beta=r/(r+2\mathsf{h})
and
\mathsf{g}=0
); then we used formulas (
4.78
) and (
4.89
) for
\mathring{\chi}^{\prime}(r)
to get the final result. Consequently, for all
r>0
, the inequality (
4.103
) for
\chi-\mathring{\chi}
gives

(4.162)
